I. Foundations and Proof
Chapter 01

Propositional and predicate logic

Every precondition, loop guard and test assertion you have ever written is a formula in a logic that has been fully worked out since 1879. Knowing its rules turns "I think this condition is right" into "this condition is provably equivalent to that one".

A proposition is a statement that is definitely true or definitely false. 7 is prime is a proposition. x > 3 is not — it has a free variable, so its truth depends on x. That distinction is the whole difference between propositional logic and predicate logic, and it is also the difference between a boolean constant and a boolean-valued function in your code.

The connectives, and the only one people get wrong

Five connectives generate everything: negation ¬, conjunction ∧, disjunction ∨, implication →, and biconditional ↔. Four of them match intuition. Implication does not.

P Q │ ¬P P∧Q P∨Q P→Q P↔Q ────────┼────────────────────────────── T T │ F T T T T T F │ F F T F F F T │ T F T T F F F │ T F F T T

The two rows that trouble everyone are the last two: P → Q is true whenever P is false. This is not a philosophical claim about causation; it is a definition chosen so that "for all n, if n > 100 then n > 10" comes out true rather than being invalidated by n = 5. An implication only ever makes a promise about the cases where its hypothesis holds. When the hypothesis fails, the promise was never triggered, so it was not broken — the statement is said to be vacuously true.

This has an immediate programming consequence. A loop body that never executes satisfies any postcondition you like; a filter over an empty list satisfies every predicate. all([]) is True in Python and Stream.allMatch on an empty stream is true in Java for exactly this reason, and it is not a quirk — it is the truth table.

Equivalences you should be able to apply without thinking

Two formulas are logically equivalent (written ≡) when they have identical truth tables. The core set:

¬(P ∧ Q) ≡ ¬P ∨ ¬Q De Morgan ¬(P ∨ Q) ≡ ¬P ∧ ¬Q De Morgan P → Q ≡ ¬P ∨ Q material implication P → Q ≡ ¬Q → ¬P contraposition P ↔ Q ≡ (P → Q) ∧ (Q → P) ¬¬P ≡ P double negation P ∧ (Q ∨ R) ≡ (P∧Q) ∨ (P∧R) distribution P ∨ (Q ∧ R) ≡ (P∨Q) ∧ (P∨R) distribution

De Morgan is the one you will use daily. Inverting a compound condition is not a matter of adding a ! at the front and hoping: !(a && b) is !a || !b, and getting this wrong while "simplifying" an if is one of the most common bug sources in refactoring.

Principle

Contraposition (P → Q ≡ ¬Q → ¬P) is a genuine equivalence. The converse (Q → P) and the inverse (¬P → ¬Q) are not. "If it is a square then it is a rectangle" is true; its converse is false. Almost every fallacious argument you will meet is a converse being smuggled in as an equivalence.

Predicates and quantifiers

A predicate P(x) is a proposition with holes. Quantifiers fill them: ∀x P(x) asserts P holds for every x in the domain, ∃x P(x) asserts it holds for at least one. The domain is never optional — ∀x (x² ≥ x) is true over the integers and false over the reals (take x = ½).

Negation pushes through quantifiers by flipping them, which is De Morgan generalised to infinite conjunctions and disjunctions:

¬∀x P(x) ≡ ∃x ¬P(x) ¬∃x P(x) ≡ ∀x ¬P(x)

Reading that in engineering terms: the negation of "every request completes in under 200 ms" is not "every request takes at least 200 ms" — it is "some request takes at least 200 ms". One counterexample refutes a universal claim; refuting an existential claim requires exhausting the domain. That asymmetry is exactly why testing can disprove a specification but not prove it.

Quantifier order is not commutative

This is the single most valuable thing in the chapter. ∀x ∃y and ∃y ∀x say entirely different things.

∀x ∃y (y > x) every integer has something bigger TRUE ∃y ∀x (y > x) one integer beats every integer FALSE

In the first, y may depend on x; in the second it may not. Recognising this distinction is what separates "every user has a session" from "there is a session shared by every user", and it is the formal content of the difference between a per-request lock and a global lock. Same-type quantifiers do commute (∀x∀y ≡ ∀y∀x); mixed ones never do.

Interview angle

Definitions in analysis and algorithms are quantifier sandwiches. Big-O is ∃c ∃n₀ ∀n ≥ n₀; a limit is ∀ε ∃δ ∀x; linearizability is ∀ histories ∃ a serial order. If you can read the quantifier prefix, you can reconstruct the definition; if you cannot, you are memorising.

Normal forms and satisfiability

Every formula can be rewritten in conjunctive normal form (an AND of ORs of literals) or disjunctive normal form (an OR of ANDs). CNF is what SAT solvers eat; DNF is what a truth table's true-rows read off as directly. The conversion is mechanical — eliminate → and ↔, push negations inward with De Morgan, then distribute — but distribution can blow up exponentially, which is why the Tseytin transformation (introducing fresh variables for subformulas) is used in practice to get an equisatisfiable CNF of linear size.

A formula is satisfiable if some assignment makes it true, a tautology if every assignment does, and unsatisfiable if none does. These are linked: φ is a tautology exactly when ¬φ is unsatisfiable, which is why a single SAT solver answers both questions and why validity checking is co-NP-complete while satisfiability is NP-complete.

Truth-table evaluation by bitmask — n variables, 2ⁿ rows in parallel
# Column for variable i is a 2^n-bit integer whose bit r says
# what variable i is in row r.  All 2^n rows evaluate at once.
def columns(n):
    full = (1 << (1 << n)) - 1          # all rows true
    cols = []
    for i in range(n):
        c, block = 0, 1 << i             # run length of the pattern
        for r in range(1 << n):
            if (r >> i) & 1: c |= 1 << r
        cols.append(c)
    return cols, full

# NOT p  -> full ^ p        p AND q -> p & q
# p OR q -> p | q           p -> q  -> (full ^ p) | q
# tautology  <=>  result == full        satisfiable <=> result != 0

That trick — one machine word holding a whole truth-table column — is worth remembering beyond logic. It is the same idea as a bitset DP, and it makes brute-force checking of formulas over n ≤ 5 variables free.

Problem set — 15 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Build the truth table of (P → Q) ∧ (Q → R) → (P → R) and classify it as tautology, contingency or contradiction.

    Solution

    It is a tautology (hypothetical syllogism). Rather than eight rows, argue by contradiction on the outer implication: it can only be false if the antecedent (P→Q)∧(Q→R) is true and P→R is false. The latter forces P = T, R = F. Then P→Q with P = T forces Q = T, and Q→R with Q = T forces R = T — contradicting R = F. No falsifying row exists.

  2. Theory

    Show P → (Q → R) ≡ (P ∧ Q) → R without a truth table.

    Solution

    Apply material implication twice and associate:

    P → (Q → R) ≡ ¬P ∨ (¬Q ∨ R) ≡ (¬P ∨ ¬Q) ∨ R ≡ ¬(P ∧ Q) ∨ R ≡ (P ∧ Q) → R.

    This equivalence is currying: a function of two arguments and a function returning a function are the same thing, and the logical statement is the Curry–Howard image of that fact.

  3. Theory

    Negate and simplify so that no negation stands outside a quantifier or a parenthesis: ∀x ∃y (P(x,y) ∧ ¬Q(y)).

    Solution

    ¬∀x∃y(P ∧ ¬Q) ≡ ∃x ∀y ¬(P(x,y) ∧ ¬Q(y)) ≡ ∃x ∀y (¬P(x,y) ∨ Q(y)) ≡ ∃x ∀y (P(x,y) → Q(y)).

    Rule of thumb: walk left to right, flip every quantifier you pass, then apply De Morgan to the matrix.

  4. Theory

    Over the domain of real numbers, decide the truth of each and justify: (a) ∀x∃y (x·y = 1), (b) ∃y∀x (x·y = 0), (c) ∀x∀y∃z (x < z ∧ z < y).

    Solution
    • (a) False: x = 0 has no multiplicative inverse. It becomes true over the non-zero reals — the domain carries the content.
    • (b) True: take y = 0, which annihilates every x. Note this is an ∃∀ that genuinely holds, because a single witness works uniformly.
    • (c) False as stated: it requires x < y, and fails for x = y or x > y. Restricted to pairs with x < y it is true (density of the reals) with z = (x+y)/2.
  5. Theory

    State the converse, inverse and contrapositive of "if the cache is warm then the request is fast", and say which are equivalent to the original.

    Solution
    • Converse: if the request is fast then the cache is warm.
    • Inverse: if the cache is not warm then the request is not fast.
    • Contrapositive: if the request is not fast then the cache is not warm.

    Only the contrapositive is equivalent to the original. Converse and inverse are equivalent to each other (each is the contrapositive of the other), but not to the original — a fast request could be fast because the payload was tiny.

  6. Theory

    Convert (A ∧ B) ∨ (C ∧ D) to CNF and count the clauses. Generalise to n conjunctive blocks of size 2 and give the growth rate.

    Solution

    Distributing: (A∨C) ∧ (A∨D) ∧ (B∨C) ∧ (B∨D) — 4 clauses. With n blocks of size 2 you must pick one literal from each block, giving 2ⁿ clauses. This exponential blow-up is why solvers use the Tseytin encoding: name each subformula with a fresh variable and assert the definitional equivalences, producing an equisatisfiable CNF of size linear in the original formula.

  7. Theory

    Prove that {¬, ∧} is functionally complete — every truth function can be written with those two alone. Then show NAND alone suffices.

    Solution

    Any truth function on n variables is the disjunction of one conjunctive term per true row (its DNF), so {¬, ∧, ∨} is complete. De Morgan removes ∨: P∨Q ≡ ¬(¬P ∧ ¬Q). Hence {¬,∧} is complete.

    For NAND, write P ↑ Q = ¬(P∧Q). Then ¬P = P↑P and P∧Q = ¬(P↑Q) = (P↑Q)↑(P↑Q). Since {¬,∧} is complete, so is {↑} — which is why a chip fab can build every gate from one cell.

  8. Theory

    An engineer replaces if (!(a > 0 && b != null)) with if (a <= 0 && b == null). Identify the fault and give the correct rewrite, including the case where a is a floating-point NaN.

    Solution

    De Morgan turns a negated AND into an OR, not an AND. The correct rewrite is if (a <= 0 || b == null). The buggy version only fires when both fail, silently skipping the case of a positive a with a null b.

    With floating point there is a second fault: a > 0 and a <= 0 are both false when a is NaN, so ¬(a > 0) is not a ≤ 0. IEEE comparison is not a total order, so classical logic's law of trichotomy does not apply — negate by writing !(a > 0) literally.

  9. Theory

    Express "f is injective" and "f is surjective" as quantified formulas, then write the negation of each in positive form.

    Solution

    Injective: ∀x∀y (f(x) = f(y) → x = y). Negation: ∃x∃y (f(x) = f(y) ∧ x ≠ y) — a collision, which is exactly the object a hash-function attacker looks for.

    Surjective: ∀b ∃a (f(a) = b). Negation: ∃b ∀a (f(a) ≠ b) — an unreachable value.

  10. Program

    Write a function that takes a formula over variables p,q,r,… (up to 20) as an expression tree and returns TAUTOLOGY, SATISFIABLE or UNSATISFIABLE by exhaustive evaluation. State the complexity.

    Solution

    Enumerate all 2ⁿ assignments as bitmasks and evaluate the tree in O(size) each: O(2ⁿ · |φ|) time, O(depth) space. Practical to n ≈ 20–24.

    def classify(node, n):
    sat = taut = True
    any_true = False
    for mask in range(1 << n):
        v = ev(node, mask)
        any_true |= v
        taut &= v
    if taut:      return "TAUTOLOGY"
    if any_true:  return "SATISFIABLE"
    return "UNSATISFIABLE"

def ev(t, m):
    k = t[0]
    if k == 'var': return (m >> t[1]) & 1
    if k == 'not': return 1 - ev(t[1], m)
    a = ev(t[1], m); b = ev(t[2], m)
    if k == 'and': return a & b
    if k == 'or':  return a | b
    if k == 'imp': return (1 - a) | b
    if k == 'iff': return int(a == b)

    Note the bitmask m doubles as the assignment: bit i is the value of variable i. Returning the first satisfying m gives you a model, not just a yes.

  11. Program

    Two boolean expressions over the same n ≤ 16 variables are given as strings. Decide whether they are logically equivalent in O(2ⁿ) total using the bitmask-column technique from the chapter.

    Solution

    Evaluate each formula once, over all rows simultaneously, by making every variable a 2ⁿ-bit integer. The formulas are equivalent iff the two resulting integers are equal.

    FULL = (1 << (1 << n)) - 1
col  = [0]*n
for r in range(1 << n):
    for i in range(n):
        if (r >> i) & 1: col[i] |= 1 << r

def ev(t):
    k = t[0]
    if k == 'var': return col[t[1]]
    if k == 'not': return FULL ^ ev(t[1])
    a, b = ev(t[1]), ev(t[2])
    return {'and': a & b, 'or': a | b,
            'imp': (FULL ^ a) | b, 'iff': FULL ^ (a ^ b)}[k]

equivalent = (ev(f1) == ev(f2))
# differing rows, if you want a counterexample:
diff = ev(f1) ^ ev(f2)
if diff: row = (diff & -diff).bit_length() - 1

    The big-integer operations cost O(2ⁿ/w) machine words each, so this is roughly 64× faster than looping rows, and diff hands you a concrete counterexample for free.

  12. Program

    Implement CNF conversion by the naive method (eliminate →, push negations, distribute) and by Tseytin. Compare output clause counts on (a₁∧b₁) ∨ (a₂∧b₂) ∨ … ∨ (aₙ∧bₙ) for n = 1..12.

    Solution

    Naive produces 2ⁿ clauses; Tseytin produces about 4n. The Tseytin idea: for each gate introduce a variable and assert its definition in CNF.

    # t_i  <->  a_i AND b_i   becomes three clauses:
#   (¬t_i ∨ a_i) (¬t_i ∨ b_i) (t_i ∨ ¬a_i ∨ ¬b_i)
# top  <->  t_1 OR ... OR t_n  becomes:
#   (¬t_i ∨ top) for each i,  and  (¬top ∨ t_1 ∨ ... ∨ t_n)
# finally assert the unit clause (top)

    The two CNFs are not logically equivalent — Tseytin adds variables — but they are equisatisfiable, and any model of the Tseytin form restricted to the original variables is a model of the original. That is all a SAT solver needs, and it is why every real toolchain uses it.

  13. Program

    Given a 2-CNF formula with n ≤ 10⁵ variables and m ≤ 10⁶ clauses, decide satisfiability in linear time and output an assignment. (This is 2-SAT; it is the one tractable island in an NP-complete sea.)

    Solution

    Each clause (x ∨ y) is two implications ¬x → y and ¬y → x. Build the implication graph on 2n literal-nodes, take strongly connected components with Tarjan or Kosaraju. It is unsatisfiable iff some variable and its negation land in the same component. Otherwise set x true when comp[x] > comp[¬x] in reverse topological order.

    // node 2i = x_i, node 2i+1 = ¬x_i
void addClause(int a, bool na, int b, bool nb){
    add(2*a+na,  2*b+!nb);   // ¬a → b
    add(2*b+nb,  2*a+!na);   // ¬b → a
}
// after SCC (Tarjan gives reverse topological order in comp[]):
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++){
    if (comp[2*i] == comp[2*i+1]) return UNSAT;
    val[i] = comp[2*i] < comp[2*i+1];   // Tarjan numbering
}

    Correctness rests on the symmetry of the implication graph: if u → v is an edge then so is ¬v → ¬u — contraposition, appearing as a graph automorphism.

  14. Program

    Model this as logic and solve programmatically: in a 9×9 Sudoku, encode "every cell has exactly one value" in CNF. How many clauses does the exactly-one constraint need, and how would you reduce it?

    Solution

    Variables xₕ,ₖ,ₔ = "cell (r,c) holds value v", 729 in total. At least one: one clause (xₕₖ₁ ∨ … ∨ xₕₖ₉) per cell. At most one: pairwise negation, C(9,2) = 36 clauses per cell, so 81 × 37 = 2,997 clauses just for cells, before rows, columns and boxes.

    The pairwise encoding is O(k²). For large k use a sequential (ladder) encoding with auxiliary variables sᵢ meaning "one of the first i is true", giving O(k) clauses:

    (¬x_i ∨ s_i)   (¬s_{i-1} ∨ s_i)   (¬x_i ∨ ¬s_{i-1})

    At k = 9 pairwise is fine; at k = 1000 it is the difference between running and not.

  15. Program

    Write a checker that, given a claimed logical equivalence between two conditions extracted from source code (say !(a && b) || c and !a || !b || c), reports whether a refactor is safe — and explain why the answer changes if the operands can have side effects or short-circuit.

    Solution

    Treating a, b, c as opaque booleans, evaluate both over all 8 assignments; here both columns agree, so the rewrite is sound in classical logic.

    It is not necessarily sound as a program transformation, for two reasons an interviewer will want you to name:

    • Short-circuit order. In !(a && b), b is evaluated only when a is true. In !a || !b, b is evaluated only when a is true as well — here they match, but reordering the disjuncts to !b || !a changes which expression runs, and if b is list.get(0) guarded by a being a non-empty check, the rewrite throws.
    • Side effects and non-boolean semantics. If evaluating b mutates state or blocks, duplicating or dropping an evaluation changes behaviour even though the truth tables agree. Three-valued logics (SQL's NULL) break the equivalences outright: in SQL, NOT (a AND b) and (NOT a) OR (NOT b) do agree, but the law of excluded middle a OR NOT a is not a tautology when a is NULL.

    The general lesson: logical equivalence licenses a refactor only under the assumption of pure, total, two-valued operands. State the assumption, then use the theorem.

Chapter 02

Direct proof, contraposition, contradiction and cases

Four proof shapes cover the overwhelming majority of arguments in computer science. Knowing which shape a statement wants is most of the work; the algebra afterwards is usually routine.

A proof is a chain of implications from things already accepted to the claim, where each link is either a definition, a previously proved result, or a rule of inference. It is not a computation and not a verification on examples — checking a claim for n = 1..1000 is evidence, not proof, and the history of number theory is littered with conjectures that first fail past 10¹⁰.

What makes proof a practical engineering skill rather than an academic ritual is that the shape of the statement determines the shape of the proof. Learn the mapping and you always know how to start.

Statement shapeHow you start
∀x P(x)"Let x be arbitrary." Prove P(x) using nothing specific about x.
∃x P(x)Construct a witness, or prove one must exist non-constructively.
P → QAssume P, derive Q. Or assume ¬Q, derive ¬P.
P ↔ QTwo proofs, or a chain of equivalences where every step is reversible.
¬PAssume P, derive a contradiction.
A = B for setsA ⊆ B and B ⊆ A.
UniquenessAssume two objects work, prove they are equal.

Direct proof

Assume the hypothesis, unfold the definitions, do algebra, arrive at the conclusion. The step that beginners skip is unfolding the definitions — almost every stuck direct proof is stuck because a word like "divides" or "even" has not been replaced by what it means.

Claim: if a | b and b | c then a | c. Unfold: b = a·k and c = b·m for integers k, m. Substitute: c = a·(km). Since km is an integer, a | c. The whole proof was the substitution; the definition did the work.

Contraposition

Because P → Q ≡ ¬Q → ¬P, you may prove either. Choose whichever hypothesis is more usable. "If n² is even then n is even" is awkward directly — you would have to reason backwards from a square. Contrapositively: "if n is odd then n² is odd", and now you have n = 2k+1 in hand and n² = 4k²+4k+1 = 2(2k²+2k)+1 falls out.

Heuristic

If the hypothesis is a negative or an existential you cannot get hold of, and the conclusion is a positive statement with structure, try the contrapositive. You are trading which side gives you an object to manipulate.

Contradiction

Assume the claim is false, derive something impossible. The classic is the irrationality of √2: assume √2 = p/q in lowest terms, square to get p² = 2q², deduce p is even, write p = 2r, get 2r² = q² so q is even too — contradicting "lowest terms".

Contradiction is the natural tool for impossibility results, which is most of what a computer scientist proves: no comparison sort beats n log n, no algorithm decides halting, no consensus protocol is safe and live under an asynchronous network with one crash failure. Each assumes the thing exists and extracts an absurdity.

A caution worth carrying: contradiction is often used where contraposition would be cleaner. If your "contradiction" proof assumes P ∧ ¬Q and then never uses P, you actually wrote a contrapositive proof with extra steps.

Proof by cases and WLOG

Split the domain into finitely many exhaustive cases and prove each. The obligations are that the cases cover everything and that you actually do all of them. "Without loss of generality" is a legitimate shortcut only when a symmetry genuinely maps one case to the other — if you say WLOG a ≤ b, the statement must be symmetric in a and b, and you should be able to say why.

A case analysis you have written as code without noticing
Claim: for reals a,b:  |a + b| ≤ |a| + |b|   (triangle inequality)

Case 1: a,b ≥ 0     |a+b| = a+b = |a|+|b|          equality
Case 2: a,b ≤ 0     |a+b| = -(a+b) = |a|+|b|       equality
Case 3: opposite signs, WLOG a ≥ 0 > b
        |a+b| ≤ max(a,-b) ≤ a + (-b) = |a|+|b|     strict unless one is 0

WLOG is justified here because swapping a and b leaves both sides unchanged.

Existence, uniqueness, and constructive versus not

To prove ∃x P(x) you may exhibit an x (constructive) or show that no-x leads to contradiction (non-constructive). The distinction is not pedantry for a programmer: a constructive proof is an algorithm. The extended Euclidean algorithm is a constructive proof of Bézout's identity; the pigeonhole principle proves collisions exist without telling you where.

Uniqueness has its own template: suppose x₁ and x₂ both satisfy the property, then show x₁ = x₂. Proving "exactly one" is two separate obligations, and forgetting the second is the most common gap in student proofs.

Interview angle

"Prove your algorithm is correct" is a proof-shape question in disguise. Greedy correctness is an exchange argument (a proof by contradiction: take an optimal solution differing from greedy at the earliest point, exchange, show it is no worse). Dynamic programming correctness is induction on subproblem size. Naming the shape before you start is what makes the answer sound rehearsed rather than improvised.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove that the sum of a rational and an irrational number is irrational.

    Solution

    By contradiction. Let q be rational, i irrational, and suppose q + i = r is rational. Then i = r - q. The rationals are closed under subtraction (a/b - c/d = (ad-cb)/bd, and bd ≠ 0), so i is rational — contradiction. Note that the analogous claim for two irrationals is false: √2 + (-√2) = 0.

  2. Theory

    Prove √3 is irrational, and explain precisely where the same argument fails for √4.

    Solution

    Suppose √3 = p/q in lowest terms, so p² = 3q². Then 3 | p². Because 3 is prime, Euclid's lemma gives 3 | p; write p = 3r, so 9r² = 3q², i.e. q² = 3r², so 3 | q — contradicting lowest terms.

    For √4 the step "4 | p² ⇒ 4 | p" is false (p = 2 is a counterexample), because 4 is not prime. The primality of the radicand is doing all the work, which is why the general theorem is: √n is irrational unless n is a perfect square.

  3. Theory

    Prove there are infinitely many primes. Then adapt the argument to show there are infinitely many primes of the form 4k+3.

    Solution

    Euclid. Suppose p₁,…,pₖ were all of them. Let N = p₁p₂…pₖ + 1. N > 1 so it has a prime factor p; but p cannot be any pᵢ, since each leaves remainder 1. Contradiction. (Note the standard misreading: N itself need not be prime — 2·3·5·7·11·13+1 = 30031 = 59·509.)

    Form 4k+3. Every odd prime is 4k+1 or 4k+3, and a product of numbers all ≡ 1 (mod 4) is ≡ 1. Suppose q₁,…,qₖ are all the 4k+3 primes; put N = 4q₁…qₖ - 1 ≡ 3 (mod 4). N is odd, and if all its prime factors were ≡ 1 (mod 4) then N ≡ 1, false. So some prime factor is ≡ 3 (mod 4), and it is not any qᵢ (each would have to divide 1). Contradiction.

  4. Theory

    Prove: if n is an integer and n³ + 5 is odd, then n is even. Which technique is natural and why?

    Solution

    Contraposition: assume n is odd and show n³+5 is even. If n = 2k+1 then n³ is odd (product of odds), so n³+5 = odd + odd = even. Direct proof would require reasoning from the parity of a cube back to the parity of n, which is the same work done backwards; contraposition hands you the algebraic object (2k+1) immediately.

  5. Theory

    Prove that among any two consecutive integers exactly one is even, using a proof by cases on n mod 2. Then state what could go wrong if you omitted a case.

    Solution

    By the division algorithm, n = 2q or n = 2q+1, exhaustively. Case A: n even, then n+1 = 2q+1 odd. Case B: n odd, then n+1 = 2q+2 = 2(q+1) even. In both cases exactly one of the pair is even.

    Exhaustiveness is the hidden obligation: the proof is valid only because the division algorithm guarantees the two cases cover ℤ. Omitting a case yields a proof of a statement restricted to a subdomain — which is precisely the bug pattern where an if/else if chain lacks a final else and silently does nothing on the uncovered input.

  6. Theory

    Show that for any 5 points placed in a 2×2 square, some two are at distance at most √2. Is your proof constructive?

    Solution

    Cut the square into four 1×1 quadrants. Five points into four quadrants means some quadrant holds two (pigeonhole), and the diameter of a unit square is √2. So those two are within √2.

    It is non-constructive in the pigeonhole step: it proves a crowded quadrant exists without naming it. It becomes constructive the moment you actually bucket the points, which is exactly what the grid method for the closest-pair problem does — the proof turns into an O(n) algorithm.

  7. Theory

    Prove that if a₁,…,aₙ are positive reals with product 1, then their sum is at least n. Identify the proof technique.

    Solution

    This is AM–GM: (a₁+…+aₙ)/n ≥ (a₁…aₙ)^(1/n) = 1. A self-contained argument is induction on n with a case split: for n = 1 trivial; for the step, since the product is 1 some aᵢ ≥ 1 and some a₃ ≤ 1. Replace the pair by their product aᵢa₃, reducing to n-1 terms; the inequality (aᵢ-1)(1-a₃) ≥ 0 rearranges to aᵢ + a₃ ≥ aᵢa₃ + 1, so the sum did not increase when we merged. Apply the induction hypothesis.

    The technique is induction plus a smoothing/exchange step — the same shape as a greedy exchange argument.

  8. Theory

    A greedy algorithm schedules jobs by earliest finishing time to maximise the count of non-overlapping intervals. Write the correctness proof as a formal exchange argument.

    Solution

    Let G = g₁,g₂,… be greedy's choices in order and O = o₁,o₂,… an optimal solution sorted by finish time, chosen among all optima to agree with G on the longest prefix. Suppose |O| > |G| and let k be the first index where they differ. Greedy picked the compatible interval with the smallest finish time, so finish(gₖ) ≤ finish(oₖ). Replace oₖ by gₖ in O: everything after oₖ starts at or after finish(oₖ) ≥ finish(gₖ), so the result is still feasible and the same size — hence still optimal, but it agrees with G on a longer prefix, contradicting the choice of O.

    Therefore no such k exists, G is a prefix of an optimum, and since greedy stops only when nothing compatible remains, |G| = |O|. Note the structure: contradiction on the outside, construction on the inside.

  9. Program

    Write a program that searches for a counterexample to "n² + n + 41 is prime for all natural n". Find the smallest one and explain what the experiment does and does not establish.

    Solution

    The smallest counterexample is n = 40: 40²+40+41 = 1681 = 41². (n = 41 also fails, visibly, since every term is divisible by 41.)

    def is_prime(x):
    if x < 2: return False
    d = 2
    while d*d <= x:
        if x % d == 0: return False
        d += 1
    return True

n = 0
while is_prime(n*n + n + 41): n += 1
print(n)          # 40

    The search disproves a universal claim with one witness — that is logically complete. Running it to n = 10⁶ and finding nothing would not prove the claim: verifying ∀ over an infinite domain by enumeration is impossible. This asymmetry (tests refute, proofs establish) is the whole reason correctness arguments exist alongside test suites.

  10. Program

    Implement a checker for the claim "every even integer 4 ≤ n ≤ 10⁶ is the sum of two primes" (Goldbach) in near-linear time overall, and report the pair with the largest smaller-prime.

    Solution

    Sieve once to 10⁶, then for each even n scan primes upward until the complement is prime. Empirically the first prime works almost immediately, so the total cost is close to O(N log log N) for the sieve plus a small constant per n.

    N = 10**6
comp = bytearray(N+1)
for i in range(2, int(N**0.5)+1):
    if not comp[i]:
        comp[i*i::i] = bytearray(len(comp[i*i::i]))
        for j in range(i*i, N+1, i): comp[j] = 1
primes = [i for i in range(2, N+1) if not comp[i]]

worst = (0, 0, 0)
for n in range(4, N+1, 2):
    for p in primes:
        if p > n // 2: break
        if not comp[n-p]:
            if p > worst[0]: worst = (p, n-p, n)
            break
print(worst)

    Again: exhaustive verification to 10⁶ is strong evidence and zero proof. Goldbach remains open.

  11. Program

    Given a set of intervals, implement the earliest-finish-time greedy and, separately, an O(n²) DP that computes the true optimum. Randomly generate 10⁵ instances and assert the two agree — a computational cross-check of the exchange argument.

    Solution
    def greedy(iv):
    iv = sorted(iv, key=lambda t: t[1])
    end, k = float('-inf'), 0
    for s, f in iv:
        if s >= end: end, k = f, k + 1
    return k

def dp(iv):                       # exact, O(n^2)
    iv = sorted(iv, key=lambda t: t[1])
    n = len(iv); best = [1]*n
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i):
            if iv[j][1] <= iv[i][0]:
                best[i] = max(best[i], best[j] + 1)
    return max(best) if n else 0

import random
for _ in range(100000):
    iv = [tuple(sorted(random.sample(range(20), 2))) for _ in range(random.randint(0,8))]
    assert greedy(iv) == dp(iv), iv

    This is stress testing, the competitive-programming discipline that corresponds to proof-checking: a brute force you trust, a fast solution you do not, and random small inputs. It finds counterexamples fast when the greedy is wrong (try sorting by start time instead and watch it fail within a few hundred iterations).

  12. Program

    Write a program to find the smallest positive integer n such that n is expressible as a sum of two positive cubes in two different ways, and describe why brute force needs a bound argument to terminate.

    Solution

    The answer is 1729 = 1³+12³ = 9³+10³, the Hardy–Ramanujan number.

    from collections import defaultdict
LIM = 100                              # a^3 with a <= LIM
seen = defaultdict(list)
for a in range(1, LIM+1):
    for b in range(a, LIM+1):
        seen[a*a*a + b*b*b].append((a,b))
ans = min(k for k,v in seen.items() if len(v) > 1 and k <= LIM**3)
print(ans)   # 1729

    The subtlety: a search over a,b ≤ LIM only certifies minimality up to LIM³, because a smaller n could in principle use a cube larger than LIM³ — except it cannot, since each cube is at most n. So the correct bound is a,b ≤ n^(1/3), and restricting the reported answer to k ≤ LIM³ is what makes the brute force a proof rather than a guess. Stating such a bound is the difference between "I searched" and "I searched exhaustively".

  13. Program

    Implement a verifier for uniqueness: given a function solve(instance) and a brute-force enumerator of all valid answers, check on random instances that the answer is unique, and explain how non-uniqueness changes what a judge can accept.

    Solution
    for _ in range(TRIALS):
    inst = random_instance()
    all_valid = [a for a in enumerate_candidates(inst) if valid(inst, a)]
    assert all_valid, "no solution but solve() returned one"
    if len(all_valid) > 1:
        print("NOT UNIQUE:", inst, all_valid[:3])
    assert solve(inst) in all_valid

    The last assertion is the point. When answers are not unique, comparing your output byte-for-byte with a reference is wrong — the checker must verify the property (feasibility plus optimal value), which is exactly why contest judges use special checkers for "print any valid answer" problems. Formally, you have separated proving ∃x P(x) from proving ∃!x P(x), and only the second licenses exact-match judging.

  14. Program

    Prove computationally and then mathematically: the equation x² ≡ 2 (mod 5) has no solution. Generalise your program to report, for each prime p < 100, which residues are squares.

    Solution

    Computationally, square every residue mod 5: 0,1,4,4,1 — the set of squares is {0,1,4}, and 2 is absent. That is a complete proof, because a solution would have to be one of the 5 residue classes; exhaustive search over a finite domain is a proof, unlike the infinite case in problem 9.

    for p in primes_below(100):
    qr = sorted({(x*x) % p for x in range(p)})
    print(p, len(qr), qr[:10])
# for odd p you always get exactly (p+1)/2 values, including 0

    Mathematically: the non-zero squares mod an odd prime p form the image of x ↦ x², which is 2-to-1 (since x and -x collide and nothing else does), so there are exactly (p-1)/2 non-zero quadratic residues. For p = 5 that is 2 residues, {1,4}, and 2 is a non-residue. Chapter 24 turns this counting fact into Euler's criterion.

Chapter 03

Induction, strong induction and well-ordering

Induction is the proof rule that mirrors recursion, and strong induction is the one that mirrors divide and conquer. If you can write the recursion you can write the proof, because they are the same object read in opposite directions.

The principle of mathematical induction says: if P(0) holds, and P(n) → P(n+1) for every n, then P(n) holds for all n ≥ 0. It is not a trick — it is one of the Peano axioms, the axiom that pins down what "the natural numbers" means and excludes stray elements not reachable by counting.

Every induction proof has three named parts and a discipline about each:

Base case prove P(b) outright for the smallest b you claim Hypothesis assume P(k) for a fixed but arbitrary k ≥ b Step prove P(k+1), using the hypothesis somewhere

If the step never uses the hypothesis you have not written an induction, you have written a direct proof with decoration. If the base case is wrong you have proved nothing, however impeccable the step — the classic joke proof that all horses are the same colour has a flawless step and a broken n = 2 case.

A worked example, and where the step really happens

Claim: 1 + 2 + … + n = n(n+1)/2.

Base n = 1: LHS = 1, RHS = 1·2/2 = 1. ✓ Step assume Σ_{i=1..k} i = k(k+1)/2. Then Σ_{i=1..k+1} i = [k(k+1)/2] + (k+1) ← hypothesis used here = (k+1)(k/2 + 1) = (k+1)(k+2)/2 ← the claim at k+1 ✓

Notice the shape: you write the k+1 quantity, split off the last term so the hypothesis applies to the rest, substitute, and simplify to the target form. That splitting move is 90% of routine inductions.

Strong induction

Sometimes P(k) alone is not enough and you need all of P(b), …, P(k). That is strong induction, and it is logically equivalent to ordinary induction (apply ordinary induction to Q(n) = P(b) ∧ … ∧ P(n)), but far more convenient whenever the recursion splits the problem non-uniformly.

Every integer n ≥ 2 has a prime factorisation: if n is prime, done; otherwise n = ab with 1 < a,b < n, and the hypothesis applies to a and b — which are not n-1, so ordinary induction cannot reach them. Mergesort's correctness has the same shape: the recursive calls are on ⌊n/2⌋ and ⌈n/2⌉.

Principle

Match the induction to the recursion. Loop → ordinary induction on the iteration count. Divide and conquer → strong induction on size. Tree recursion → structural induction on the tree. Mutual recursion → simultaneous induction on a pair of statements. When a proof feels impossible, it is usually because the induction is not shaped like the algorithm.

Well-ordering, and why termination is a proof obligation

The well-ordering principle — every non-empty set of non-negative integers has a least element — is equivalent to induction, and it is the standard way to prove termination. Find a quantity (a variant or monovariant) that is a non-negative integer and strictly decreases every iteration. It cannot decrease forever, so the loop stops.

Termination and correctness of Euclid's algorithm, in full
while b != 0:
    a, b = b, a % b
return a

VARIANT   b is a non-negative integer and strictly decreases:
          the new b is a mod b < b whenever b > 0.
          By well-ordering the loop cannot run forever.

INVARIANT gcd(a, b) is unchanged by the body, because
          gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, a mod b).
          True before the loop; preserved by each iteration.

ON EXIT   b = 0, so the invariant reads gcd(a₀, b₀) = gcd(a, 0) = a.
          Hence the returned a is the gcd.               ∎

That template — invariant for correctness, variant for termination — proves every loop you will ever write, and it is Floyd–Hoare logic in miniature. A while (true) with a break deep inside is hard to reason about precisely because the variant is hidden.

Strengthening the hypothesis

A counter-intuitive but essential technique: when an induction will not close, prove something stronger. A stronger claim gives you a stronger hypothesis in the step, and the extra assumption is often exactly what was missing.

Try proving ∑ 1/i² ≤ 2 by induction and it stalls — the hypothesis Sₖ ≤ 2 gives Sₖ₊₁ ≤ 2 + 1/(k+1)², which overshoots. Prove instead Sₖ ≤ 2 - 1/k. Now Sₖ₊₁ ≤ 2 - 1/k + 1/(k+1)² ≤ 2 - 1/(k+1), because 1/(k+1)² ≤ 1/k - 1/(k+1) = 1/(k(k+1)). The stronger statement proves itself while the weaker one does not.

Interview angle

"Why is your DP correct?" wants an induction on subproblem order: state the invariant that dp[i] equals the true optimum for the subproblem, show it for the base indices, and show the transition preserves it given that all indices it reads are smaller in the DP order. Two sentences, and it is the difference between "I've seen this pattern" and "I know why it works".

Problem set — 15 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove by induction that ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ i² = n(n+1)(2n+1)/6.

    Solution

    Base n = 1: 1 = 1·2·3/6 = 1. ✓

    Step: assume Sₖ = k(k+1)(2k+1)/6. Then

    Sₖ₊₁ = k(k+1)(2k+1)/6 + (k+1)² = (k+1)[k(2k+1) + 6(k+1)]/6 = (k+1)(2k²+7k+6)/6 = (k+1)(k+2)(2k+3)/6,

    which is the formula at n = k+1. ∎ The factoring of 2k²+7k+6 into (k+2)(2k+3) is the only non-mechanical step; pulling (k+1) out first is what makes it appear.

  2. Theory

    Prove 2ⁿ > n² for all n ≥ 5. Why does the base case have to be 5?

    Solution

    Base n = 5: 32 > 25. ✓ (At n = 2,3,4 the claim is false or equal: 4 = 4, 8 < 9, 16 = 16 — so 5 is genuinely the first valid base.)

    Step: assume 2ᵏ > k² for some k ≥ 5. Then 2ᵏ⁺¹ = 2·2ᵏ > 2k². It suffices that 2k² ≥ (k+1)², i.e. k² - 2k - 1 ≥ 0, which holds for k ≥ 1+√2 ≈ 2.41, so certainly for k ≥ 5. ∎

    The step is valid from k = 3 onward, but the chain has to start somewhere true — this is the standard trap where a correct step is anchored to a false base.

  3. Theory

    Prove that any 2ⁿ × 2ⁿ board with one square removed can be tiled by L-shaped trominoes.

    Solution

    Base n = 1: a 2×2 board minus one square is an L-tromino. ✓

    Step: given a 2ᵏ⁺¹ × 2ᵏ⁺¹ board with one square missing, cut it into four 2ᵏ×2ᵏ quadrants. The missing square lies in one of them. Place a single tromino at the centre covering one cell of each of the other three quadrants. Now every quadrant is a 2ᵏ×2ᵏ board with exactly one cell unavailable, so the hypothesis tiles each. ∎

    This proof is an algorithm: recursing gives an O(4ⁿ) constructive tiling, optimal since that is the output size.

  4. Theory

    Prove by strong induction that every integer n ≥ 12 can be written as 4a + 5b with a,b ≥ 0.

    Solution

    Bases n = 12 = 4·3, 13 = 4·2+5, 14 = 4+5·2, 15 = 5·3. ✓ (Four bases, because the step reaches back by 4.)

    Step: for n ≥ 16, n-4 ≥ 12, so by the strong hypothesis n-4 = 4a+5b; then n = 4(a+1)+5b. ∎

    The count of base cases must equal the reach-back of the step. This is the Chicken McNugget / Frobenius setting: for coprime p,q the largest unrepresentable value is pq-p-q, here 20-4-5 = 11, which is exactly why 12 is the threshold.

  5. Theory

    Prove that the Fibonacci numbers satisfy F₁+F₂+…+Fₙ = Fₙ₊₂ - 1, and that gcd(Fₔ, Fₙ) = F₋ᶜᵔ₃ₓᵢ₋ₙ₋ is plausible by checking small cases.

    Solution

    Base n = 1: F₁ = 1 and F₃ - 1 = 2-1 = 1. ✓

    Step: ∑ᵢ₋₁ᵏ⁺¹ Fᵢ = (Fₖ₊₂ - 1) + Fₖ₊₁ = Fₖ₊₃ - 1 by the Fibonacci recurrence. ∎

    For the gcd identity: gcd(F₁₂,F₁₈) = gcd(144,1597) = 1 = F₆? No — gcd(12,18) = 6 and F₆ = 8, and indeed gcd(144, 2584) = 8 once you index consistently. The lesson is to fix an indexing convention (F₁=F₂=1) before testing identities; off-by-one in Fibonacci indexing invalidates more "counterexamples" than any actual error.

  6. Theory

    Find the flaw: "Claim: all horses have the same colour. Base: one horse, trivially. Step: given k+1 horses, the first k share a colour and the last k share a colour, so all k+1 do."

    Solution

    The step fails at k+1 = 2. The argument needs the two groups of size k to overlap in at least one horse to chain the colours; with k = 1 the sets {h₁} and {h₂} are disjoint and nothing links them. So P(1) → P(2) is not established, and the chain breaks at its first link even though every later link is fine.

    The general moral: a step that is valid "for large k" must still be valid at the specific k where the chain starts. This is the same failure mode as an off-by-one in a loop that only manifests on inputs of size 1.

  7. Theory

    State a loop invariant for insertion sort and use it to prove correctness.

    Solution

    Invariant: at the start of the iteration with index i, the subarray A[0..i-1] contains the original elements of A[0..i-1], in sorted order.

    • Initialisation: at i = 1 the subarray is a single element, trivially sorted and a permutation of itself.
    • Maintenance: the body shifts every element of A[0..i-1] greater than A[i] one position right and inserts A[i] into the gap. The result is sorted (the inserted key is ≥ everything to its left and < everything to its right) and is a permutation of the old contents plus A[i]. So the invariant holds at i+1.
    • Termination: the loop ends at i = n, where the invariant states that A[0..n-1] is a sorted permutation of the input. ∎

    This is induction on i, dressed in the vocabulary CLRS uses; the "maintenance" clause is the inductive step.

  8. Theory

    Prove that a binary tree with n internal nodes (each with exactly 2 children) has n+1 leaves, by structural induction.

    Solution

    Structural induction on the tree. Base: a single leaf has n = 0 internal nodes and 1 leaf; 0+1 = 1. ✓

    Step: a non-leaf tree is a root with subtrees T₁, T₂ having n₁, n₂ internal nodes and, by hypothesis, n₁+1, n₂+1 leaves. The whole tree has n₁+n₂+1 internal nodes and (n₁+1)+(n₂+1) = (n₁+n₂+1)+1 leaves. ∎

    This is why a Huffman tree with k symbols has exactly k-1 merges, and why a segment tree over n leaves needs < 2n nodes — the same counting identity twice.

  9. Theory

    Prove that strong induction and the well-ordering principle are equivalent.

    Solution

    WO → SI. Suppose the strong-induction premises hold but P(n) fails for some n. Let S = {n : ¬P(n)}, non-empty, so by well-ordering it has a least element m. Every j < m satisfies P(j), so the strong-induction step yields P(m) — contradicting m ∈ S. Hence S is empty.

    SI → WO. Let S be a set of naturals with no least element; we show S is empty by strong induction on P(n) = "n ∉ S". Assume 0,…,n-1 ∉ S. If n ∈ S it would be the least element of S (everything smaller is excluded), contradiction; so n ∉ S. By strong induction no n is in S. ∎

    This is why "take a minimal counterexample" and "induct" are interchangeable proof openings.

  10. Program

    Implement Towers of Hanoi and prove by induction that your solution uses exactly 2ⁿ - 1 moves; verify computationally for n ≤ 20.

    Solution
    def hanoi(n, a, b, c, out):
    if n == 0: return
    hanoi(n-1, a, c, b, out)
    out.append((a, c))
    hanoi(n-1, b, a, c, out)   # note the source/aux swap

for n in range(21):
    out = []
    hanoi(n, 'A', 'B', 'C', out)
    assert len(out) == (1 << n) - 1

    Proof. Let M(n) be the move count. M(0) = 0 = 2⁰-1. The code gives M(n) = 2M(n-1) + 1, so by induction M(n) = 2(2ⁿ⁻¹-1)+1 = 2ⁿ-1. ∎

    Optimality is a separate induction: the largest disc must move at least once, and immediately before it does, all n-1 smaller discs must be stacked on the spare peg — costing at least M(n-1) before and M(n-1) after, giving M(n) ≥ 2M(n-1)+1.

  11. Program

    Write mergesort and annotate it with the invariant and variant that prove it correct and terminating. Then write a stress test against the language's built-in sort.

    Solution
    def msort(a):
    # VARIANT: len(a) strictly decreases on every recursive call
    #          and is a non-negative integer -> recursion terminates.
    if len(a) <= 1: return a[:]                 # BASE of the strong induction
    m = len(a) // 2
    L, R = msort(a[:m]), msort(a[m:])           # HYPOTHESIS: both are sorted
    out, i, j = [], 0, 0
    # INVARIANT: out is sorted, and every element of out is <= every
    #            remaining element of L[i:] and R[j:].
    while i < len(L) and j < len(R):
        if L[i] <= R[j]: out.append(L[i]); i += 1
        else:            out.append(R[j]); j += 1
    out += L[i:]; out += R[j:]
    return out                                   # STEP: hence out is sorted

import random
for _ in range(20000):
    a = [random.randrange(-9, 10) for _ in range(random.randrange(0, 12))]
    assert msort(a) == sorted(a)

    Note that the induction is strong: the calls are on ⌊n/2⌋ and ⌈n/2⌉, not n-1. Note also that <= rather than < in the comparison is what makes the sort stable — an invariant about equal elements that the correctness proof above does not capture, and therefore has to be stated separately.

  12. Program

    Given n ≤ 10⁵, count the ways to tile a 2×n strip with 1×2 dominoes. Derive the recurrence, prove it by induction, and compute the answer mod 10⁹+7.

    Solution

    Let T(n) be the count. Consider the leftmost column: either one vertical domino covers it (leaving T(n-1)) or two horizontal dominoes cover the first two columns (leaving T(n-2)). These cases are exhaustive and disjoint, so T(n) = T(n-1) + T(n-2), T(0)=1, T(1)=1 — the Fibonacci numbers.

    MOD = 10**9 + 7
a, b = 1, 1                      # T(0), T(1)
for _ in range(n - 1):
    a, b = b, (a + b) % MOD
print(b if n >= 1 else 1)

    The induction that makes this a proof rather than a pattern-match is the claim "every tiling of the 2×n strip has a unique leftmost-column configuration from those two", which needs the observation that a horizontal domino in row 1 forces one in row 2 (nothing else can cover the cell below it without leaving a hole). For n up to 10¹⁸ use matrix exponentiation from Chapter 48.

  13. Program

    Implement a function that, given a recurrence aₙ = c₁aₙ₋₁ + … + cₖaₙ₋ₖ and initial terms, verifies a claimed closed form for n = 0..10⁵ — a computational check of an inductive claim.

    Solution
    def verify(c, init, closed, N=10**5, mod=None):
    a = list(init)
    k = len(c)
    for n in range(N + 1):
        if n >= k:
            v = sum(c[i] * a[n-1-i] for i in range(k))
            if mod: v %= mod
            a.append(v)
        if a[n] != closed(n):
            return ("MISMATCH", n, a[n], closed(n))
    return "OK"

# e.g. a_n = a_{n-1} + a_{n-2}, a_0=0,a_1=1 vs Binet rounded
print(verify([1,1], [0,1], lambda n: round(((1+5**.5)/2)**n / 5**.5), N=70))

    Two lessons this experiment teaches directly. First, Binet's formula fails past n ≈ 70 in double precision — a closed form correct in ℝ is not correct in float64, which is Chapter 66's subject. Second, agreement on 10⁵ terms is not proof; the actual proof is a two-line induction verifying that the closed form satisfies both the recurrence and the initial conditions, and that these two facts determine the sequence uniquely.

  14. Program

    Prove and implement: the number of binary strings of length n with no two consecutive 1s is Fₙ₊₂. Then generate all such strings for n = 20 without generating and filtering all 2²⁰.

    Solution

    Proof. Let S(n) be the count. Strings ending in 0 are any valid string of length n-1 with 0 appended: S(n-1). Strings ending in 1 must have 0 before it, so they are a valid string of length n-2 with "01" appended: S(n-2). Hence S(n) = S(n-1)+S(n-2), with S(0)=1, S(1)=2, giving S(n) = Fₙ₊₂ under F₁=F₂=1. ∎

    def gen(n):
    out = []
    def rec(pos, last, cur):
        if pos == n: out.append(''.join(cur)); return
        cur.append('0'); rec(pos+1, 0, cur); cur.pop()
        if last == 0:
            cur.append('1'); rec(pos+1, 1, cur); cur.pop()
    rec(0, 0, [])
    return out
# |gen(20)| == 17711 == F_22, and the recursion visits only valid prefixes

    The point of the generator is that pruning at the branch (if last == 0) means the search tree has exactly O(S(n)) leaves rather than 2ⁿ — the proof and the pruning are the same fact, which is the general relationship between counting arguments and backtracking efficiency.

  15. Program

    Write a general "variant checker" harness: instrument a loop with a claimed variant expression and assert on every iteration that it is a non-negative integer and strictly decreased. Apply it to binary search and to the Collatz iteration; explain the different outcomes.

    Solution
    def run_with_variant(step, state, variant, limit=10**6):
    prev = variant(state); assert prev >= 0
    for _ in range(limit):
        if done(state): return "terminated", state
        state = step(state)
        v = variant(state)
        assert 0 <= v < prev, f"variant failed: {prev} -> {v}"
        prev = v
    return "no proof of termination within limit", state

    Binary search: the variant hi - lo is a non-negative integer that at least halves each iteration, so it strictly decreases while positive — the harness passes, and this is the termination proof (plus the ⌈log₂n⌉ bound, since halving a value in [0,n] reaches 0 in that many steps).

    Collatz: no known variant exists. The value itself increases on odd inputs (3n+1), and no monotone measure has ever been found. The harness reports "no proof" forever, which is the honest state of mathematics: Collatz terminates for every integer anyone has tested past 2⁶⁸, and remains unproved. The contrast is the punchline of the chapter — termination is not observable, it is provable or it is open.

Chapter 04

Sets, relations and functions

Sets are the substrate everything else is built on, relations are what tables and graphs actually are, and functions are relations with a determinism constraint. Getting the vocabulary exact removes an entire class of modelling mistakes.

A set is an unordered collection of distinct objects. That definition contains two engineering facts: no duplicates and no order. A HashSet honours both; a List honours neither. Choosing the wrong one is choosing the wrong mathematical object, and the bugs that follow (duplicate emails, order-dependent test failures) are the model leaking.

The standard operations and their identities:

A ∪ B union {x : x ∈ A or x ∈ B} A ∩ B intersection {x : x ∈ A and x ∈ B} A \ B difference {x : x ∈ A and x ∉ B} A △ B symmetric diff (A\B) ∪ (B\A) -- "changed" set; XOR A × B Cartesian prod {(a,b) : a ∈ A, b ∈ B} |A×B| = |A|·|B| P(A) power set all subsets of A |P(A)| = 2^|A| (A ∪ B)ᶜ = Aᶜ ∩ Bᶜ De Morgan again, now on sets A ∩ (B ∪ C) = (A∩B) ∪ (A∩C)

Those are the same laws as Chapter 1 because a set is a predicate in disguise: x ∈ A ∩ B unfolds to x ∈ A ∧ x ∈ B. Any set identity can be proved by unfolding to logic, applying a propositional equivalence, and folding back. That is the standard technique and it never fails.

Proving set equality

To show A = B you show A ⊆ B and B ⊆ A. Each direction starts "let x ∈ A be arbitrary" and ends "hence x ∈ B". When both directions are the same chain read backwards, you may write a single chain of ↔ — but only if every step is genuinely reversible, which is a real obligation people skip.

Relations

A binary relation from A to B is simply a subset of A × B. That is the entire definition, and it is worth sitting with: a database table is a relation (hence "relational"), an adjacency matrix is a relation, ≤ is a relation, and so is "follows on social media". The properties a relation on a single set may have:

PropertyDefinitionExample / non-example
Reflexive∀a: aRa≤ yes; < no
Irreflexive∀a: ¬aRa< yes; "is a sibling of" yes
SymmetricaRb → bRa"same connected component" yes; "parent of" no
AntisymmetricaRb ∧ bRa → a=b≤ yes; "same age" no
TransitiveaRb ∧ bRc → aRc≤ yes; "friend of" famously no
Total∀a,b: aRb ∨ bRa≤ on ℤ yes; ⊆ no

Symmetric and antisymmetric are not opposites: equality is both, "divides" on positive integers is antisymmetric only, and a relation can be neither.

Principle

A comparator you pass to a sort must define a strict weak ordering: irreflexive, transitive, and with transitive incomparability. Violate it — return true for both cmp(a,b) and cmp(b,a), or compare with <= where < is required — and C++'s std::sort has undefined behaviour that reads out of bounds, while Java throws "Comparison method violates its general contract". The exception is a relation-theory violation reported at runtime.

Closures

The transitive closure R⁺ is the smallest transitive relation containing R — reachability, if R is an edge set. Reflexive-transitive closure R* adds the diagonal. Computing it is Floyd–Warshall on booleans in O(n³), or O(n³/64) with bitsets, and knowing that "reachability = transitive closure" lets you move between graph and relational phrasings of the same problem freely.

Functions

A function f : A → B is a relation where every a ∈ A relates to exactly one b. "At most one" makes it a partial function; "at least one" makes it total. A Map is a partial function; a total function on a finite domain is an array.

injective (one-to-one) f(x) = f(y) → x = y no collisions surjective (onto) ∀b ∃a f(a) = b nothing unreachable bijective both invertible |A| = |B| finite ⟹ injective ⟺ surjective ⟺ bijective (this fails for infinite sets — see Chapter 6)

Composition (g ∘ f)(x) = g(f(x)) is associative but not commutative, and it preserves injectivity and surjectivity in the directions you would expect. The formal inverse f⁻¹ exists exactly when f is a bijection — which is precisely why a hash function has no inverse (not injective) and why an encryption function must be one (a bijection on the message space for a fixed key).

Interview angle

"Design a URL shortener" is a question about functions. You need an injective map from long URLs to short codes (else two URLs collide and one breaks), and you must decide whether it is a computed function (hash, needing collision handling because it is not injective) or a stored relation (a counter plus a table, injective by construction). Saying that out loud reframes the whole design in one sentence.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove A ∖ (B ∪ C) = (A ∖ B) ∩ (A ∖ C) by the unfold-to-logic method.

    Solution

    x ∈ A∖(B∪C) ↔ x∈A ∧ ¬(x∈B ∨ x∈C) ↔ x∈A ∧ (¬x∈B ∧ ¬x∈C) (De Morgan) ↔ (x∈A ∧ ¬x∈B) ∧ (x∈A ∧ ¬x∈C) (idempotence of ∧ on x∈A) ↔ x ∈ (A∖B) ∩ (A∖C). Every step is a biconditional, so both inclusions are proved at once. ∎

  2. Theory

    Show |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| - |A ∩ B| and extend to three sets. What is the pattern for n sets?

    Solution

    Each element of A∪B is counted once on the right if it lies in exactly one of A, B, and twice-minus-once = once if it lies in both. For three sets, elements in all three are counted 3 - 3 + 1 = 1 time:

    |A∪B∪C| = |A|+|B|+|C| - |A∩B| - |A∩C| - |B∩C| + |A∩B∩C|.

    The general pattern is inclusion–exclusion: alternate signs over all non-empty subsets of the index set, |∪Aᵢ| = ∑∅≠S (-1)^(|S|+1) |∩i∈S Aᵢ|, proved in Chapter 30. The binomial identity that makes it work is ∑k=1..m (-1)^(k+1) C(m,k) = 1 for every m ≥ 1.

  3. Theory

    Classify each relation on ℤ as reflexive / symmetric / antisymmetric / transitive: (a) a ≡ b (mod 5), (b) a | b, (c) a ≠ b, (d) a · b > 0.

    Solution
    • (a) reflexive, symmetric, transitive — an equivalence relation. Not antisymmetric (2 ≡ 7 and 7 ≡ 2 but 2 ≠ 7).
    • (b) reflexive, transitive, antisymmetric on positive integers (a partial order); on all of ℤ antisymmetry fails since 2 | -2 and -2 | 2. The domain matters.
    • (c) symmetric only. Not reflexive, not transitive (1≠2, 2≠1, but 1=1).
    • (d) symmetric and transitive, but not reflexive because 0·0 = 0. A relation that is symmetric and transitive but not reflexive is the standard trap: the "proof" that aRb → bRa → aRa silently assumes some b exists related to a.
  4. Theory

    How many binary relations are there on an n-element set? How many are reflexive? Symmetric? Functions?

    Solution
    • All relations: subsets of n² pairs, so 2^(n²).
    • Reflexive: the n diagonal pairs are forced, the other n²-n are free: 2^(n²-n).
    • Symmetric: choose freely on the diagonal (n) and on unordered off-diagonal pairs (C(n,2)): 2^(n(n+1)/2).
    • Functions A→A: each of n inputs picks one of n outputs: nⁿ. Of these, n! are bijections.

    The gap between 2^(n²) and nⁿ is the price of determinism, and it is why a lookup table is exponentially cheaper to represent than an arbitrary relation.

  5. Theory

    Prove that if g ∘ f is injective then f is injective, and give a counterexample showing g need not be.

    Solution

    Suppose f(x) = f(y). Applying g, g(f(x)) = g(f(y)), and injectivity of the composite gives x = y. So f is injective. ∎

    Counterexample for g: let f : {1} → {1,2} with f(1)=1, and g : {1,2} → {1} constant. Then g∘f is injective on a one-element domain, but g is not injective. Dually, if g∘f is surjective then g is, but f need not be.

  6. Theory

    Show |P(A)| = 2^|A| by a bijection with binary strings, and explain the connection to bitmask enumeration.

    Solution

    Fix an ordering a₀,…,aₙ₋₁ of A. Map each subset S to the length-n string whose i-th bit is 1 iff aᵢ ∈ S. This is injective (different subsets differ on some element, hence on some bit) and surjective (every string names the subset of positions where it is 1), so it is a bijection, and there are 2ⁿ strings. ∎

    That bijection is subset enumeration by bitmask: for (int m = 0; m < (1<<n); m++) iterates over P(A) exactly once each, and m & (1<<i) tests membership in O(1). Enumerating submasks of m is the same bijection restricted: for (int s = m; s; s = (s-1) & m).

  7. Theory

    Define the image and preimage of a set under f. Prove f⁻¹(X ∩ Y) = f⁻¹(X) ∩ f⁻¹(Y) but that images only satisfy f(X ∩ Y) ⊆ f(X) ∩ f(Y).

    Solution

    f(X) = {f(x) : x ∈ X} and f⁻¹(Y) = {x : f(x) ∈ Y} (defined for any f, not just bijections).

    Preimage: x ∈ f⁻¹(X∩Y) ↔ f(x) ∈ X ∧ f(x) ∈ Y ↔ x ∈ f⁻¹(X) ∧ x ∈ f⁻¹(Y). ∎ Preimages commute with all of union, intersection and complement — which is exactly why filtering by a predicate on the input composes cleanly.

    Image: ⊆ is clear. For strictness take f(x)=x², X = {1}, Y = {-1}. Then X∩Y = ∅ so the left side is empty, while f(X)∩f(Y) = {1}. Equality holds for all X,Y precisely when f is injective.

  8. Program

    Implement set operations over sorted integer arrays in O(n+m) and over bitsets in O(n/64). Benchmark both for n = 10⁶.

    Solution
    // sorted two-pointer intersection
vector<int> inter(const vector<int>& a, const vector<int>& b){
    vector<int> r; size_t i = 0, j = 0;
    while (i < a.size() && j < b.size()){
        if (a[i] < b[j]) i++;
        else if (b[j] < a[i]) j++;
        else { r.push_back(a[i]); i++; j++; }
    }
    return r;
}
// bitset: universe of size N, membership by bit
bitset<1000000> A, B;
auto I = A & B;      // intersection      ~N/64 word ops
auto U = A | B;      // union
auto D = A & ~B;     // difference
auto S = A ^ B;      // symmetric difference

    The bitset wins by a factor near 64 when the universe is dense and small enough to fit in memory; the sorted-array version wins when the sets are sparse relative to the universe (a 10⁶-bit set costs 125 KB whether it holds 3 elements or 10⁶). The crossover is roughly when |A|+|B| > N/64 — a genuinely useful rule when choosing a representation.

  9. Program

    Given a directed graph with n ≤ 1000 nodes, compute the transitive closure. Give both the O(n³) Floyd–Warshall version and a bitset version, and state the practical speed-up.

    Solution
    // bitset transitive closure: O(n^3 / 64)
bitset<1000> reach[1000];
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) reach[i][i] = 1;      // reflexive-transitive
for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (reach[i][k]) reach[i] |= reach[k];

    The inner loop over j collapses into one |= over 1000 bits — about 16 machine words. At n = 1000 that is 10⁹/64 ≈ 1.6·10⁷ word operations, well under a second, where the scalar triple loop is 10⁹ and takes seconds. The if (reach[i][k]) guard is not an optimisation detail: it is the observation that if i cannot reach k, routing through k adds nothing.

  10. Program

    Write a comparator-contract validator: given a comparator and a small array, check irreflexivity, antisymmetry and transitivity by brute force over all triples, and report the violating triple.

    Solution
    def check(cmp_lt, xs):
    for a in xs:
        if cmp_lt(a, a): return ("not irreflexive", a)
    for a in xs:
        for b in xs:
            if cmp_lt(a,b) and cmp_lt(b,a): return ("not antisymmetric", a, b)
    for a in xs:
        for b in xs:
            for c in xs:
                if cmp_lt(a,b) and cmp_lt(b,c) and not cmp_lt(a,c):
                    return ("not transitive", a, b, c)
                # transitivity of incomparability (strict weak ordering):
                eq = lambda x,y: not cmp_lt(x,y) and not cmp_lt(y,x)
                if eq(a,b) and eq(b,c) and not eq(a,c):
                    return ("incomparability not transitive", a, b, c)
    return "ok"

    Run it on the classic broken comparator lambda a,b: a.score <= b.score and it reports "not antisymmetric" on any two equal scores. Run it on a comparator that ranks by a.score but treats a missing score as 0 in one branch and as infinity in another, and it reports an intransitive triple — the exact input that makes std::sort read out of bounds. This O(k³) check on a 20-element sample is cheap insurance in a test suite.

  11. Program

    Given n ≤ 20 items, enumerate every subset and, for each, every one of its submasks. Prove the total work is 3ⁿ rather than 4ⁿ.

    Solution
    for (int m = 0; m < (1 << n); m++)
    for (int s = m; ; s = (s - 1) & m){
        process(m, s);
        if (s == 0) break;
    }

    Count. The pair (m, s) with s ⊆ m is determined by assigning each of the n elements one of three states: in s (hence in m), in m but not s, or in neither. Three independent choices per element gives exactly 3ⁿ pairs. ∎

    At n = 20 that is 3.5·10⁹ — too slow; at n = 16 it is 4.3·10⁷ and fine. This is the workhorse loop for subset-sum DP over subsets (SOS DP is the O(2ⁿ n) refinement when you only need sums, not all pairs).

  12. Program

    Implement a bijection between the set of all finite subsets of ℕ and ℕ itself, in both directions, and verify round-tripping for the first 10⁵ naturals.

    Solution

    The bijection is the binary-representation one: subset S maps to ∑i∈S 2ᵢ.

    def encode(S):  return sum(1 << i for i in S)
def decode(n):  return {i for i in range(n.bit_length()) if (n >> i) & 1}
assert all(encode(decode(n)) == n for n in range(10**5))

    It is a bijection because binary representation is unique. The consequence is important for Chapter 6: the set of finite subsets of ℕ is countable, while P(ℕ) — all subsets, including infinite ones — is not. The finiteness restriction is exactly what makes the encoding a finite integer.

  13. Program

    Model a "user has role" relation and answer: which users have all of a required role set? Implement with bitmasks and state the complexity against a nested-loop approach.

    Solution
    // roles[u] is a bitmask of u's roles; need is the required mask
vector<int> who(const vector<int>& roles, int need){
    vector<int> r;
    for (int u = 0; u < (int)roles.size(); u++)
        if ((roles[u] & need) == need) r.push_back(u);   // superset test
    return r;
}

    O(n) with a one-instruction subset test, versus O(n·k) scanning a list of roles per user. The mathematical content is that "need ⊆ roles[u]" is (roles[u] & need) == need — subset as a bit operation. Note the direction: (a & b) == b means b ⊆ a, and reversing it is the single most common bug in permission code.

  14. Program

    Given two large files of records, compute the symmetric difference by key without loading both fully into memory. What set-theoretic property makes a streaming solution possible?

    Solution

    Sort both files by key externally, then do a single merge pass emitting keys present in exactly one stream:

    i = j = 0
while i < len(A) or j < len(B):
    if   j == len(B) or (i < len(A) and A[i] < B[j]): emit(A[i], 'only-A'); i += 1
    elif i == len(A) or B[j] < A[i]:                  emit(B[j], 'only-B'); j += 1
    else: i += 1; j += 1        # present in both: not in the symmetric difference

    The property that licenses streaming is that ▵ is decomposable over an ordering: whether a key belongs to A ▵ B depends only on that key's membership in each set, not on any other key. So a merge that sees each key's full multiplicity in one place suffices, and memory is O(1) beyond the sort. Union, intersection and difference are decomposable too; something like "the 10 most common keys in A but not B" is not, and needs a second pass or a sketch.

Chapter 05

Equivalence relations, partial orders and lattices

Equivalence relations partition; partial orders arrange. Union-find is the first, topological sort is the second, and a surprising number of algorithm design questions are really asking which of the two structures your data has.

Two special kinds of relation carry most of the weight in computer science, and they are near-mirror images. An equivalence relation is reflexive, symmetric and transitive. A partial order is reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive. Swapping symmetry for antisymmetry is the whole difference between "these things are interchangeable" and "these things are ranked".

Equivalence relations and partitions

The central theorem: equivalence relations on A correspond exactly to partitions of A. Given ~, the equivalence class [a] = {x : x ~ a}, and the classes are non-empty, cover A, and are pairwise disjoint. Conversely any partition defines an equivalence by "in the same block".

The disjointness proof is the one to know: if [a] ∩ [b] ≠ ∅, take c in both, so c~a and c~b; symmetry and transitivity give a~b, and then [a] = [b]. Classes are never partially overlapping — they are equal or disjoint, and there is no third case.

Equivalence Classes are Algorithm ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── ≡ (mod n) residue classes modular arithmetic same component components union-find / DFS same hash bucket buckets hash tables bisimilar states minimal DFA states Hopcroft's algorithm same sorted string anagram groups group-by on a key

Notice the last row: every group-by is an equivalence relation, and the key function is a witness. Two items are equivalent iff key(x) = key(y), and relations of that form ("kernel of a function") are automatically equivalences. This gives a fast test: if you can produce a canonical form, you have an equivalence, and you can group in O(n) with a hash map instead of comparing pairs in O(n²).

Principle

To group by an equivalence, find a canonical form. Anagrams → sorted letters. Congruent triangles → sorted side lengths. Isomorphic rooted trees → a canonical string encoding of the sorted children encodings. Whenever grouping looks quadratic, the missing piece is a canonical form.

Union-find: an equivalence relation you can grow

Disjoint-set union maintains an equivalence relation under incremental merges, with O(α(n)) amortised operations — inverse Ackermann, effectively constant.

Union-find with path compression and union by size
int p[N], sz[N];
void init(int n){ for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) p[i] = i, sz[i] = 1; }
int find(int x){ return p[x] == x ? x : p[x] = find(p[x]); }   // compress
bool unite(int a, int b){
    a = find(a); b = find(b);
    if (a == b) return false;                 // already equivalent
    if (sz[a] < sz[b]) swap(a, b);            // attach smaller to larger
    p[b] = a; sz[a] += sz[b];
    return true;
}

The three relation axioms map onto the code: reflexivity is p[i] = i, symmetry is the fact that unite is order-insensitive, and transitivity is the tree structure — sharing a root is transitive by construction.

Partial orders

A poset (P, ≤) is reflexive, antisymmetric, transitive. The word partial means some pairs are simply incomparable: under ⊆, the sets {1} and {2} are neither. A total order additionally compares everything.

Posets are drawn as Hasse diagrams: the transitive reduction, edges only for covering relations, everything implied by transitivity left out. A DAG is exactly a poset's Hasse diagram plus orientation, and this is why:

poset ⟷ DAG reachability = the order relation linear extension ⟷ topological sort any total order consistent with ≤ antichain ⟷ set of mutually incomparable tasks (schedulable in parallel) chain ⟷ a dependency path (a lower bound on makespan)

Every finite poset has at least one linear extension (that is what topological sort computes), and counting them is #P-complete — which is why "how many valid orderings" problems are hard while "give me one" is linear time.

Lattices, and why they matter for static analysis

A lattice is a poset where every pair has a least upper bound (join, ∨) and a greatest lower bound (meet, ∧). Subsets under ⊆ form a lattice with union and intersection; divisors under divisibility form one with lcm and gcd.

Lattices are the backbone of dataflow analysis. The compiler's abstract state lives in a finite-height lattice, transfer functions are monotone, and the fixed-point iteration terminates because a monotone function on a finite-height lattice reaches a fixed point in bounded steps (Knaster–Tarski). Type inference joins types the same way; the "common supertype" of a ternary expression is a join in the subtyping lattice.

Interview angle

"Given task dependencies, find the minimum time with unlimited workers" is a poset question: the answer is the longest chain, computable by DP over a topological order. "Maximum tasks in parallel" is the largest antichain, which by Dilworth's theorem equals the minimum number of chains covering the poset — solvable as bipartite matching. Recognising which of the two you were asked is the whole problem.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove that equivalence classes are either identical or disjoint.

    Solution

    Suppose c ∈ [a] ∩ [b], so c~a and c~b. By symmetry a~c, and with transitivity a~b. Now take any x ∈ [a]: x~a~b gives x ∈ [b], so [a] ⊆ [b]; the symmetric argument gives the reverse. Hence [a] = [b]. Therefore if the classes meet at all, they coincide. ∎

  2. Theory

    How many equivalence relations are there on a set of size 4? Of size n?

    Solution

    Equivalence relations correspond to partitions, so the count is the Bell number Bₙ. B₀..B₅ = 1, 1, 2, 5, 15, 52, so the answer for n = 4 is 15. The recurrence is Bₙ₊₁ = ∑ₖ C(n,k) Bₖ — choose the k elements sharing a block with a distinguished element, partition the rest. Chapter 33 develops this.

    Contrast with the 2^(n²) relations overall: the three axioms are enormously restrictive.

  3. Theory

    Is "|a - b| ≤ 1" on the integers an equivalence relation? Justify precisely.

    Solution

    No. It is reflexive and symmetric but not transitive: |1-2| ≤ 1 and |2-3| ≤ 1 but |1-3| = 2. This is the classic "approximately equal" trap, and it has a direct engineering consequence: floating-point comparison by epsilon (abs(a-b) < eps) is not an equivalence relation, so you cannot use it as a hash-map key or a sort key without producing inconsistent results. The standard fix is snapping to a grid (round(x/eps)), which is an equivalence because it is the kernel of a function.

  4. Theory

    Draw the Hasse diagram of the divisors of 36 under divisibility. Identify the join and meet, the maximal and minimal elements, and the longest chain.

    Solution

    Divisors: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36. Covering edges: 1→2, 1→3; 2→4, 2→6; 3→6, 3→9; 4→12; 6→12, 6→18; 9→18; 12→36; 18→36.

    Join = lcm, meet = gcd. Unique minimum 1, unique maximum 36 (so it is a bounded lattice). The longest chain is 1 | 2 | 4 | 12 | 36, length 5 — equal to Ω(36)+1 = 4+1 where Ω counts prime factors with multiplicity, since each covering step multiplies by exactly one prime.

    Structurally this poset is {0,1,2} × {0,1} ordered componentwise (exponents of 2 and 3) — a product of chains, which is the general shape of any divisor lattice.

  5. Theory

    Prove that every finite non-empty poset has a maximal element, and explain why "maximal" and "maximum" differ.

    Solution

    Take any x₀. If it is not maximal, some x₁ > x₀ exists; repeat. Antisymmetry and transitivity make the sequence strictly increasing, so no element repeats, and finiteness forces the process to stop — at a maximal element. ∎ (For infinite posets this needs Zorn's lemma and can fail without it.)

    Maximal means nothing is strictly above it; maximum means it is above everything. In the poset {a, b} with no relations, both a and b are maximal and there is no maximum. In scheduling terms: several tasks can be "last possible" without one being "the last".

  6. Theory

    State Dilworth's theorem and use it to solve: given n tasks with a dependency partial order, what is the minimum number of workers each of whom executes a chain of dependent tasks?

    Solution

    Dilworth: in a finite poset, the minimum number of chains needed to cover all elements equals the maximum size of an antichain.

    So the answer is the size of the largest set of mutually independent tasks. Computationally: build the transitive closure, then a bipartite graph with left copy and right copy of each element, edge u_L - v_R when u < v. The minimum chain cover is n - (\text{maximum matching}) (this is minimum path cover on a DAG). By König's theorem the matching equals the minimum vertex cover, and Dilworth follows.

    Complexity: O(n³/64) for the closure plus Hopcroft–Karp at O(E√V).

  7. Theory

    Show that the set of all partitions of a set, ordered by refinement, forms a lattice. What are the meet and join?

    Solution

    Order: P ≤ Q iff every block of P is contained in a block of Q (P is finer). The meet P ∧ Q is the partition whose blocks are the non-empty intersections Bᴵ ∩ B_Q — the coarsest partition refining both. The join P ∨ Q is the transitive closure of the union of the two equivalence relations — the finest partition that both refine.

    The join is exactly what union-find computes when you feed it the edges of both partitions, which is why merging two groupings is a linear-time operation rather than a search. The bottom element is all singletons; the top is one block.

  8. Program

    Implement union-find with path compression and union by size, and use it to count connected components of a graph with 10⁶ nodes and 10⁶ edges. Then extend it to support "number of nodes in my component" and "number of edges in my component".

    Solution
    struct DSU {
    vector<int> p, sz, ecnt;
    DSU(int n): p(n), sz(n,1), ecnt(n,0) { iota(p.begin(), p.end(), 0); }
    int find(int x){ while (p[x] != x) x = p[x] = p[p[x]]; return x; }  // halving
    void unite(int a, int b){
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) { ecnt[ra]++; return; }        // internal edge
        if (sz[ra] < sz[rb]) swap(ra, rb);
        p[rb] = ra; sz[ra] += sz[rb];
        ecnt[ra] += ecnt[rb] + 1;
    }
    int compSize(int x){ return sz[find(x)]; }
    int compEdges(int x){ return ecnt[find(x)]; }
};

    Any value aggregated at the root works as long as it is mergeable and you only ever query it at the root. A component is a tree iff compEdges == compSize - 1, which is a one-line cycle detector.

  9. Program

    Group 10⁶ strings into anagram classes. Give an O(total length · logσ) solution and explain why comparing pairs is not viable.

    Solution
    from collections import defaultdict
groups = defaultdict(list)
for s in words:
    key = tuple(sorted(s))            # canonical form; or a 26-tuple of counts
    groups[key].append(s)

    Pairwise comparison is Θ(n²) = 10¹² comparisons — hopeless. The canonical form converts the equivalence test into an equivalence key, and hashing does the grouping in one pass. For lowercase words a 26-element count tuple is faster than sorting: O(|s| + 26) per word with no comparison sort.

    The general principle from the chapter: an equivalence relation given by a canonical form is the kernel of a function, and kernels are groupable in linear time.

  10. Program

    Implement topological sort (Kahn's algorithm), detect cycles, and additionally output the lexicographically smallest linear extension.

    Solution
    import heapq
def toposort(n, adj):
    indeg = [0]*n
    for u in range(n):
        for v in adj[u]: indeg[v] += 1
    h = [u for u in range(n) if indeg[u] == 0]
    heapq.heapify(h)                       # min-heap => lexicographically smallest
    order = []
    while h:
        u = heapq.heappop(h); order.append(u)
        for v in adj[u]:
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0: heapq.heappush(h, v)
    return order if len(order) == n else None      # None => a cycle exists

    O((V+E) log V) with the heap, O(V+E) with a plain queue if any extension will do. The cycle test is exact: Kahn's algorithm emits every vertex iff the relation is antisymmetric-and-acyclic, i.e. iff it really is a partial order. A graph with a cycle is not a poset, because a ≤ b ≤ a with a ≠ b violates antisymmetry.

  11. Program

    Given a DAG of n ≤ 10⁵ tasks with durations, compute the minimum makespan with unlimited parallelism, and list one critical path.

    Solution
    order = toposort(n, adj)
finish = [0]*n; pred = [-1]*n
for u in order:
    finish[u] = max((finish[p] for p in rev[u]), default=0) + dur[u]
    pred[u]   = max(rev[u], key=lambda p: finish[p], default=-1)
makespan = max(finish)

# reconstruct the critical path backwards
v = max(range(n), key=lambda i: finish[i]); path = []
while v != -1: path.append(v); v = pred[v]
path.reverse()

    O(V+E). Mathematically the makespan is the maximum weight of a chain in the poset, and the critical path is that chain.
