Every task off the critical path has slack; reducing any single non-critical task's duration cannot reduce the makespan, which is the scheduling fact this identity makes obvious.

  12. Program

    Implement DFA minimisation by partition refinement (Hopcroft or Moore) — a direct application of computing the coarsest equivalence relation satisfying a stability condition.

    Solution
    # Moore's algorithm: O(n^2 * |Σ|), simple and adequate for n ≤ few thousand
part = [1 if accepting[s] else 0 for s in range(n)]     # initial 2 blocks
while True:
    sig = [(part[s], tuple(part[delta[s][c]] for c in alphabet)) for s in range(n)]
    remap, new = {}, []
    for s in range(n):
        if sig[s] not in remap: remap[sig[s]] = len(remap)
        new.append(remap[sig[s]])
    if new == part: break
    part = new

    Each round splits blocks whose members behave differently on some symbol, so the partition only ever refines; since it cannot refine past singletons, it terminates in at most n rounds. The fixed point is the coarsest equivalence that is stable under the transition function — the Myhill–Nerode relation — and the quotient automaton is the unique minimal DFA. Hopcroft's refinement of this is O(n |Σ| log n) by always processing the smaller half of a split block.

  13. Program

    Given n intervals, define a < b iff a ends before b starts. Find the longest chain and the largest antichain, and interpret both.

    Solution

    Under this order, a chain is a set of pairwise non-overlapping intervals, and an antichain is a set of pairwise overlapping intervals.

    # longest chain = maximum non-overlapping set: greedy by end time, O(n log n)
iv.sort(key=lambda t: t[1]); end = -inf; chain = 0
for s, f in iv:
    if s >= end: end, chain = f, chain + 1

# largest antichain = maximum simultaneous overlap: sweep line, O(n log n)
ev = sorted([(s, +1) for s, f in iv] + [(f, -1) for s, f in iv])
cur = best = 0
for x, d in ev:
    cur += d; best = max(best, cur)

    Interpretation: the chain is the maximum number of non-conflicting meetings one room can host; the antichain is the minimum number of rooms needed. By Dilworth's dual (Mirsky's theorem) the poset can be covered by exactly that many antichains, which is precisely the greedy room-assignment algorithm. Interval orders are the case where both quantities are computable greedily instead of by matching.

  14. Program

    Write a fixed-point iterator over a finite lattice for a reaching-definitions dataflow analysis, and argue termination.

    Solution
    IN  = [set() for _ in blocks]
OUT = [set() for _ in blocks]
changed = True
while changed:
    changed = False
    for b in blocks:
        newin = set().union(*(OUT[p] for p in preds[b])) if preds[b] else set()
        newout = (newin - kill[b]) | gen[b]
        if newin != IN[b] or newout != OUT[b]:
            IN[b], OUT[b], changed = newin, newout, True

    Termination. The lattice is P(D) for the finite set D of definitions, ordered by ⊆, of height |D|. The transfer function f(X) = (X ∖ kill) ∪ gen is monotone, and the join is union, so each variable's value only grows. A strictly increasing chain in a lattice of height |D| has at most |D|+1 elements, so across all |B| blocks the loop runs at most |B|·|D| times and the least fixed point is reached — Knaster–Tarski, made concrete. Replacing union with intersection (available expressions) flips the lattice and requires initialising to top rather than bottom; getting that initialisation backwards is the classic dataflow bug.

Chapter 06

Cardinality, countability and the pigeonhole principle

The pigeonhole principle is the most useful trivial theorem in computer science, and Cantor's diagonal argument is the proof template behind undecidability. Both are statements about comparing sizes without counting.

Two sets have the same cardinality when a bijection exists between them. For finite sets this recovers ordinary counting. For infinite sets it produces results that look paradoxical until you accept the definition and stop appealing to intuition.

The pigeonhole principle

If n items go into m boxes and n > m, some box holds at least two items. The proof is one line by contraposition: if every box held at most one, the total would be at most m. The generalised form: some box holds at least ⌈n/m⌉ items — because if every box held at most ⌈n/m⌉ - 1, the total would be at most m(⌈n/m⌉-1) < n.

Trivial to state, and it proves an astonishing range of things:

Any 2^k+1 distinct values hashed into 2^k buckets → a collision exists Any n+1 numbers from 1..2n → one divides another Any sequence of n²+1 distinct reals → a monotone subsequence of length n+1 Any 5 points in a 2×2 square → two within √2 Lossless compression cannot shrink every input (counting argument) Floyd's cycle detection terminates: n+1 states in an n-state space
Principle

Pigeonhole proofs are non-constructive: they say a collision exists without saying where. The engineering consequence is that they give lower bounds and impossibility results for free, but never algorithms. When a problem says "show that two of these must…", the answer is almost always pigeonhole, and the work is choosing the right boxes.

Choosing the boxes is the entire skill. For "among n+1 numbers from 1..2n, one divides another": write each number as 2ᵃ · q with q odd. There are only n odd values in 1..2n, so two numbers share the same q — and then the one with the smaller exponent divides the other. The boxes were the odd parts, and finding that is the proof.

Countable sets

A set is countable if it is finite or has a bijection with ℕ. Surprises follow immediately:

  • ℤ is countable: 0,1,-1,2,-2,… — zigzag enumeration.
  • ℕ × ℕ is countable: enumerate along anti-diagonals. The explicit bijection is the Cantor pairing function π(x,y) = (x+y)(x+y+1)/2 + y.
  • ℚ is countable: each rational is a pair of integers, and a countable union of countable sets is countable.
  • The set of all finite strings over a finite alphabet is countable: enumerate by length, then lexicographically.
  • Therefore the set of all programs is countable, since a program is a finite string.

Cantor's diagonal argument

The reals are not countable. Suppose they were, and list the reals in [0,1) as r₁, r₂, r₃, … with decimal expansions. Build a new number d whose k-th digit differs from the k-th digit of rₖ (say, 5 unless that digit is 5, else 6 — avoiding the 0.999… ambiguity). Then d differs from every rₖ in position k, so d is not in the list, contradicting completeness of the list. ∎

This is the single most reused proof template in theoretical computer science. Since programs are countable and functions ℕ → {0,1} are not, most functions are not computable — a cardinality argument that gives an impossibility result before any machine model is defined. The halting problem's proof is the diagonal argument applied to a table of "does program i halt on input j", and Russell's paradox, Gödel's incompleteness and the Recursion theorem are all the same move.

The halting problem as a diagonal
Suppose H(p, x) decides whether program p halts on input x.
Build:
    D(p):  if H(p, p) then loop forever   else halt

Now ask: does D(D) halt?
    If D(D) halts, then H(D,D) was true, so D loops.       contradiction
    If D(D) loops, then H(D,D) was false, so D halts.      contradiction

So no such H exists.  The "diagonal" is the entry H(p, p).

Cantor's theorem and the hierarchy

For any set A, |A| < |P(A)|. Proof: given any f : A → P(A), consider D = {a : a ∉ f(a)}. If D = f(b) for some b, then b ∈ D ↔ b ∉ f(b) = D — contradiction. So no f is surjective. ∎ The same diagonal, one level up, and it gives an infinite hierarchy of infinities.

Interview angle

"Can you write a program that detects infinite loops in any program?" is asking whether you know the halting problem. The strong answer names the diagonal construction in three lines, then adds the engineering reality: undecidable in general, but decidable for restricted languages (loop bounds, terminating type systems), and approximable in practice by timeouts and static analysers that are allowed to answer "don't know". Rice's theorem generalises: every non-trivial semantic property of programs is undecidable.

Problem set — 15 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove that among any 51 numbers chosen from 1..100, two are consecutive.

    Solution

    Box the integers into the 50 pairs {1,2}, {3,4}, …, {99,100}. With 51 chosen numbers and 50 boxes, some box contributes both its members — and those two are consecutive. ∎ The bound is tight: the 50 odd numbers contain no consecutive pair, so 51 is exactly the threshold.

  2. Theory

    Prove that among any n+1 numbers chosen from 1..2n, one divides another.

    Solution

    Write each chosen number uniquely as 2ᵃq with q odd. Each q lies in {1,3,5,…,2n-1}, only n possible values. With n+1 numbers, two share the same odd part: 2ᵃq and 2ᵇq with a < b. Then the first divides the second. ∎

    Tight: {n+1, n+2, …, 2n} has n elements and no such pair, since the ratio of any two is less than 2.

  3. Theory

    Prove the Erdős–Szekeres theorem: any sequence of n²+1 distinct reals has a monotone subsequence of length n+1.

    Solution

    For each position i, let (aᵢ, dᵢ) be the lengths of the longest increasing and longest decreasing subsequences ending at i. If no monotone subsequence of length n+1 exists, then aᵢ, dᵢ ∈ {1,…,n}, giving only n² possible pairs for n²+1 positions. By pigeonhole two positions i < j share a pair. But comparing xᵢ and x₃: if xᵢ < x₃ then a₃ ≥ aᵢ+1; otherwise d₃ ≥ dᵢ+1. Either way the pairs differ — contradiction. ∎

    Note the boxes were pairs of DP values, which is why this proof is essentially the correctness argument for the O(n log n) LIS algorithm read backwards.

  4. Theory

    Prove that no lossless compression algorithm can shrink every input, and quantify how many inputs can shrink.

    Solution

    A lossless compressor is injective on the set of inputs. There are 2ⁿ strings of length exactly n, but only 2ⁿ - 1 strings of length less than n in total (1 + 2 + 4 + … + 2ⁿ⁻¹). By pigeonhole an injective map from 2ⁿ inputs into 2ⁿ-1 shorter outputs is impossible, so at least one length-n input does not shrink. ∎

    Quantitatively: at most 2ⁿ⁻ᵏ - 1 inputs of length n can shrink by k or more bits, so the fraction shrinking by 10 bits is under 2⁻¹⁰ ≈ 0.1%. Compression works only because real data is concentrated on a tiny, highly non-uniform subset of the input space — Chapter 68 makes this precise with entropy.

  5. Theory

    Prove ℚ is countable and ℝ is not, and state exactly which step of the diagonal argument fails if you try it on ℚ.

    Solution

    ℚ: each rational is p/q in lowest terms with q > 0, so it injects into ℤ × ℕ, which is countable by the Cantor pairing function; an infinite subset of a countable set is countable.

    ℝ: Cantor's diagonal as in the chapter.

    Applying the diagonal to a list of rationals does construct a real number differing from every listed rational — but that number need not be rational (its digit sequence is not eventually periodic, in general), so no contradiction arises. The diagonal argument only refutes a listing when the diagonal object is guaranteed to be in the set being listed, and that is the step that fails.

  6. Theory

    Prove that the set of computable functions ℕ → ℕ is countable, and conclude that uncomputable functions exist.

    Solution

    Every computable function is computed by at least one program; every program is a finite string over a finite alphabet; the set of finite strings is countable (enumerate by length, then lexicographically). So the computable functions are the image of a countable set under a surjection, hence countable.

    But the set of all functions ℕ → {0,1} has cardinality |P(ℕ)| > |ℕ| by Cantor's theorem. A countable set cannot cover an uncountable one, so uncomputable functions exist — in fact "almost all" functions are uncomputable. ∎

    This is a pure cardinality proof: it names no specific uncomputable function and requires no machine model. Producing an explicit one (the halting predicate) needs the diagonal construction instead.

  7. Theory

    You hash n keys into m buckets uniformly. Give the pigeonhole bound on the maximum load, and contrast it with the probabilistic bound.

    Solution

    Pigeonhole (worst case, deterministic): some bucket holds at least ⌈n/m⌉ keys — and an adversary who knows your hash function can force all n keys into one bucket, since the function is fixed and its preimages are infinite. This is exactly the hash-flooding DoS attack, and the mitigation is a keyed hash chosen at startup so the adversary cannot compute preimages.

    Probabilistic (random hash, n = m): the maximum load is Θ(log n / log log n) with high probability — the balls-in-bins result of Chapter 40. With two hash functions and placement in the lesser-loaded bucket it drops to Θ(log log n) ("the power of two choices").

    The contrast is the whole point: pigeonhole gives an unavoidable worst case, randomness gives a typical case, and neither substitutes for the other.

  8. Theory

    Prove that in any group of 6 people, there are 3 mutual acquaintances or 3 mutual strangers (R(3,3) ≤ 6).

    Solution

    Fix a person v. Of the 5 others, by pigeonhole at least ⌈5/2⌉ = 3 share the same relation to v — say three are acquaintances of v (the stranger case is symmetric). If any two of those three know each other, they plus v form a mutual-acquaintance triangle. Otherwise all three are mutual strangers. ∎

    Tightness: on 5 vertices, the 5-cycle and its complement (also a 5-cycle) contain no triangle, so R(3,3) = 6 exactly. In graph terms: every 2-colouring of K₆'s edges contains a monochromatic triangle.

  9. Program

    Implement the Cantor pairing function and its inverse; verify it is a bijection ℕ² → ℕ for all pairs up to 500.

    Solution
    def pair(x, y):
    s = x + y
    return s * (s + 1) // 2 + y

def unpair(z):
    import math
    w = (math.isqrt(8*z + 1) - 1) // 2      # largest w with w(w+1)/2 <= z
    t = w * (w + 1) // 2
    y = z - t
    return (w - y, y)

seen = set()
for x in range(500):
    for y in range(500):
        z = pair(x, y)
        assert unpair(z) == (x, y)
        assert z not in seen; seen.add(z)
assert min(seen) == 0                     # and the image has no gaps below max

    Use math.isqrt, not int(sqrt()) — the floating-point square root is wrong for z near 2⁵² and the bijection silently breaks. That is a Chapter 66 issue appearing inside a Chapter 6 construction, and it is exactly the kind of failure that survives a 500×500 test and dies in production.

  10. Program

    Given n ≤ 10⁵ integers, decide in O(n) whether two of them are congruent mod n-1, without a hash set. Justify with pigeonhole.

    Solution

    There are only n-1 residue classes mod n-1, and n numbers, so by pigeonhole a collision is guaranteed — the answer is always yes, and the work is only to exhibit the pair.

    seen = [-1] * (n - 1)             # direct-address array, not a hash set
for i, v in enumerate(a):
    r = v % (n - 1)
    if seen[r] != -1:
        print(seen[r], i); break
    seen[r] = i

    O(n) time and memory with no hashing, because the key space is bounded by n-1 and can be direct-addressed. The pigeonhole guarantee also means the loop provably breaks before exhausting the array, so no "not found" branch is needed — a rare case where a counting argument removes a code path.

  11. Program

    Implement Floyd's tortoise-and-hare cycle detection for a functional graph f : {0..n-1} → {0..n-1}, returning the cycle start and length in O(1) memory. Explain why pigeonhole guarantees a cycle.

    Solution

    Iterating f from any start produces an infinite sequence in an n-element state space; by pigeonhole some state repeats within the first n+1 steps, and once a state repeats the sequence is eventually periodic. So a cycle always exists — this is why the algorithm never needs a "no cycle" branch.

    def floyd(f, x0):
    slow, fast = f(x0), f(f(x0))
    while slow != fast: slow, fast = f(slow), f(f(fast))
    mu, slow = 0, x0                       # find cycle start
    while slow != fast: slow, fast, mu = f(slow), f(fast), mu + 1
    lam, cur = 1, f(slow)                  # find cycle length
    while cur != slow: cur, lam = f(cur), lam + 1
    return mu, lam

    Why the meeting point works: if the tail has length μ and the cycle length λ, the pointers meet at a position ≡ 0 (mod λ) steps into the cycle, so restarting one pointer at x₀ and advancing both one step at a time makes them meet exactly at the cycle entrance. This is the basis of Pollard's rho in Chapter 23.

  12. Program

    Empirically find the birthday-paradox threshold: for a 32-bit hash, how many random keys before a collision is more likely than not? Compare to the theoretical ≈ 1.177√m.

    Solution
    import random
def trial(bits):
    m, seen, k = 1 << bits, set(), 0
    while True:
        h = random.randrange(m); k += 1
        if h in seen: return k
        seen.add(h)
trials = sorted(trial(24) for _ in range(200))   # 24 bits keeps memory sane
print("median first collision:", trials[100])
print("theory 1.177*sqrt(m) =", 1.177 * (1 << 12))

    For 24 bits the median lands near 4,800 versus the predicted 1.177·4096 ≈ 4,820. Extrapolating to 32 bits gives ≈ 77,000 keys for a coin-flip chance of collision — which is why a 32-bit hash is unusable as a unique identifier at any real scale, and why UUIDs carry 122 random bits (collision-safe past 10¹⁹ ids).

    Note the contrast with pigeonhole: pigeonhole says a collision is certain at m+1 keys; the birthday bound says it is likely at √m. The square-root gap is the difference between a guarantee and a risk, and security proofs live in that gap.

  13. Program

    Write a program that enumerates all finite binary strings in a well-defined order (a bijection with ℕ) and, given a string, returns its index. Use it to argue that the set of programs is countable.

    Solution
    def index(s):                 # "" -> 0, "0" -> 1, "1" -> 2, "00" -> 3, ...
    return (1 << len(s)) - 1 + int(s or '0', 2) * (len(s) > 0)

def at(n):
    L = 0
    while (1 << (L + 1)) - 1 <= n: L += 1
    off = n - ((1 << L) - 1)
    return format(off, '0%db' % L) if L else ''
assert all(index(at(n)) == n for n in range(10000))

    This is a explicit bijection ℕ → {0,1}*, so the set of finite binary strings is countable. A program is a finite string in some encoding, so the set of programs injects into {0,1}* and is countable. Combined with problem 6, that settles the existence of uncomputable functions — and the enumeration above is exactly the "list all programs" device used in proofs of the Recursion theorem and in Kolmogorov complexity arguments.

  14. Program

    Implement a busy-beaver-style bounded halting checker: run each of the 2ᵏ programs of length k for at most T steps and report how many halt. Explain what this experiment cannot tell you.

    Solution
    halted = 0
for code in range(1 << k):
    if run_with_step_limit(decode(code), steps=T) is not TIMEOUT:
        halted += 1
print(halted, "of", 1 << k, "halted within", T, "steps")

    What it gives you: a lower bound on the number of halting programs of length k, and a sound over-approximation of the non-halting set. Bounded halting is decidable — that is the whole point of the step limit.

    What it cannot tell you: whether any timed-out program halts eventually. Raising T never resolves this in general, because the maximum runtime of a halting k-bit program (the busy beaver function BB(k)) grows faster than any computable function, so no computable choice of T is ever sufficient. That is the halting problem in quantitative form: not merely unsolved, but with a provably non-computable resource requirement.

  15. Program

    Given n points with integer coordinates in [0,10⁹]², prove and detect: among any 5, two have a midpoint with integer coordinates. Implement the detection in O(n).

    Solution

    Proof. The midpoint of (x₁,y₁) and (x₂,y₂) is integral iff x₁ ≡ x₂ and y₁ ≡ y₂ (mod 2). There are only 2×2 = 4 parity classes, so among 5 points two share a class. ∎

    buckets = {}
for i, (x, y) in enumerate(pts):
    key = (x & 1, y & 1)
    if key in buckets:
        print("pair:", buckets[key], i); break
    buckets[key] = i

    O(n), and by pigeonhole it terminates within the first 5 points regardless of n. In d dimensions the same argument needs 2ᵈ+1 points — the parity vector is the box, and the box count is what generalises.

Chapter 07

Invariants, monovariants and the extremal principle

Three problem-solving tools that answer "is this reachable?", "does this terminate?" and "what does the best case look like?". They are the mathematical form of loop invariants, termination measures and greedy exchange arguments.

These three techniques are the working core of olympiad-style problem solving, and each has an exact counterpart in program reasoning. They are grouped here because in practice you try them in order: what stays the same, what steadily decreases, what does the extreme case look like.

Invariants: proving something is unreachable

An invariant is a quantity or property preserved by every legal move. If the start and the target differ in an invariant, the target is unreachable — and this is the only clean way to prove a negative about a process.

The classic: a mutilated 8×8 chessboard with two opposite corners removed cannot be tiled by dominoes. Colour it normally; the removed corners share a colour, so 30 squares of one colour and 32 of the other remain. Every domino covers exactly one of each. The difference (\#\text{black} - \#\text{white}) is invariant at 0 for any tiling, but the board has difference 2. Unreachable. ∎

Note what this proof does not do: it does not search. A brute force over placements would take exponential time to conclude the same thing, and would not generalise to 2n × 2n. The invariant is a proof of impossibility for every board size at once.

Common invariants worth trying ────────────────────────────── parity of a sum, count, or position (most common by far) a sum or product taken modulo k colouring / 2-colouring argument number of inversions in a permutation sum of coordinates mod something the multiset of values (if moves only permute) a monotone quantity that is preserved exactly

The 15-puzzle, in full

Why half of all 15-puzzle configurations are unsolvable
State: a permutation of 15 tiles + blank, on a 4×4 grid.
Move : slide a tile into the blank = transpose that tile with the blank.

Each move is a single transposition, so it flips the parity of the
permutation.  Each move also changes the blank's row by 0 or ±1.

Define  I = (number of inversions among the 15 tiles)
            + (row index of the blank, 0-based from the top)

A horizontal move: permutation unchanged in the "reading order" sense
  → inversions unchanged, blank row unchanged  → I unchanged mod 2.
A vertical move: the blank jumps over 3 tiles → inversions change by
  an odd amount (±1 or ±3); blank row changes by 1 → I unchanged mod 2.

So I mod 2 is INVARIANT.  The solved state has I even.
Any start with I odd is provably unreachable — exactly half of them.

Monovariants: proving a process ends

A monovariant changes in one direction only. If it is a non-negative integer that strictly decreases, well-ordering forces termination — this is the loop variant of Chapter 3, used on processes rather than code.

Example: n people sit in a circle; repeatedly pick two adjacent people who are out of order by height and swap them. Does this stop? Yes: each swap removes exactly one inversion, inversions are a non-negative integer, so at most C(n,2) swaps occur. That is simultaneously a termination proof and a tight bound on bubble sort.

Monovariants need not be integers, but they must not approach a limit without reaching it. "The total energy decreases" proves nothing if it decreases by 1/2ᵏ each step. Either bound the decrease from below by a positive constant, or use a well-founded ordering (lexicographic on a tuple of naturals is the standard trick for nested loops).

Principle

For nested or interleaved processes, use a lexicographic variant: a tuple of non-negative integers, ordered lexicographically, that strictly decreases. This proves termination of algorithms where no single counter decreases — Knuth–Bendix completion, the simplex method with Bland's rule, and the potential-function proofs of Chapter 12 all rely on it.

The extremal principle

Consider the largest, the smallest, the leftmost, the closest pair. In a finite non-empty set, an extremum exists (well-ordering again), and the extremal object often cannot behave the way the problem's negation requires.

Proof that a finite graph with all degrees ≥ 2 contains a cycle: take a longest path v₀ … vₖ. The endpoint vₖ has degree at least 2, so it has a neighbour besides vₖ₋₁. That neighbour cannot be outside the path (the path would be longer, contradicting maximality), so it is some vᵢ on the path — giving a cycle. ∎ Nothing was constructed; the extremal choice did the work.

Interview angle

Extremal arguments are how greedy proofs and exchange arguments are written: "consider an optimal solution agreeing with greedy on the longest prefix" is an extremal choice, and the contradiction comes from showing you could extend the agreement. If you can state that sentence, you can prove most greedy algorithms correct on the spot.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    A blackboard holds the numbers 1..20. Repeatedly erase two numbers a, b and write |a-b|. What is the parity of the final number, and why?

    Solution

    Invariant: the parity of the sum. Replacing a, b by |a-b| changes the sum by a+b-|a-b| = 2min(a,b), an even amount. So the sum's parity never changes.

    Initially 1+2+…+20 = 210, which is even. The final single number therefore must be even. (Every even value from 0 to 20 is achievable, so the invariant is exactly the obstruction and nothing more.)

  2. Theory

    Prove that a 8×8 board with two opposite corners removed cannot be tiled by 1×2 dominoes, and generalise: which pairs of removed squares can be tiled?

    Solution

    The colouring argument is in the chapter: opposite corners share a colour, leaving 30/32, while every domino covers one of each.

    Generalisation (Gomory's theorem): removing any two squares of opposite colours always leaves a tileable board. Proof: draw a closed Hamiltonian cycle through all 64 cells (easy to construct on any even board). Removing two cells of opposite colour splits the cycle into two paths, each of even length — because along the cycle colours alternate, so the two removed cells are an odd distance apart in both directions. An even path is trivially tiled by consecutive dominoes. ∎

    So the colour invariant is not merely necessary here — it is sufficient, which is unusual and worth noting.

  3. Theory

    Three piles hold 10, 15 and 20 stones. A move takes one stone from each of two piles and adds two to the third. Can you reach 15, 15, 15?

    Solution

    Look at the pile sizes mod 3. A move changes a pair by -1,-1 and the third by +2. Since +2 ≡ -1 (mod 3), every pile decreases by 1 mod 3 — so the pairwise differences mod 3 are invariant.

    Start: (10,15,20) ≡ (1,0,2) (mod 3), all distinct. Target: (15,15,15) ≡ (0,0,0), all equal. Differences mod 3 cannot change from "all distinct" to "all equal". Unreachable. ∎

  4. Theory

    Prove that bubble sort performs exactly inv(A) swaps, where inv is the number of inversions, and that this is optimal for adjacent-transposition sorting.

    Solution

    Each bubble-sort swap exchanges an adjacent out-of-order pair, which removes exactly that inversion and changes no other pair's relative order. So swaps and inversions decrease in lockstep, and the process ends when inversions reach 0 — after exactly inv(A) swaps.

    Optimality: any algorithm restricted to adjacent transpositions changes the inversion count by exactly ±1 per operation. Going from inv(A) to 0 therefore needs at least inv(A) operations. ∎ The maximum, C(n,2), occurs for a reversed array — which is why insertion sort is Θ(n + inv) and genuinely linear on nearly-sorted data.

  5. Theory

    Prove that any 15-puzzle position with an odd inversion-plus-blank-row parity is unsolvable, and compute what fraction of the 16! arrangements are solvable.

    Solution

    The invariant argument is in the chapter's code block. Since the invariant partitions the state space into two classes and every move preserves it, no position in the odd class reaches the solved (even) position.

    Fraction: exactly half, 16!/2 = 10{,}461{,}394{,}944{,}000 solvable arrangements. That the split is exactly even follows because transposing any two non-blank tiles flips the invariant, giving a bijection between the two classes. The converse (every even position is solvable) is true but needs a separate constructive argument.

  6. Theory

    n points in the plane, no three collinear, each joined to its nearest neighbour. Prove no point has more than 5 edges pointing at it.

    Solution

    Suppose points a₁,…,aₖ all have p as their nearest neighbour. Take any two, aᵢ, a₃. Since p is nearest to aᵢ, we have |aᵢa₃| > |aᵢp|, and symmetrically |aᵢa₃| > |a₃p|. In triangle aᵢ p a₃, the largest side is opposite the largest angle, so the angle at p exceeds both others, hence ∠aᵢpa₃ > 60°.

    The angles at p around a full turn sum to 360°, and each pair of consecutive rays subtends more than 60°, so k < 360/60 = 6, i.e. k ≤ 5. ∎ This bounds the in-degree of a nearest-neighbour graph by 5, which is why such graphs have O(n) edges and can be built and traversed in linear time once computed.

  7. Theory

    Use the extremal principle to prove that every finite DAG has a vertex with in-degree 0.

    Solution

    Take a longest directed path v₀ → v₁ → … → vₖ (one exists: the graph is finite, and path lengths are bounded by n since acyclicity forbids repeats). If v₀ had an incoming edge from u, then either u is off the path — giving a longer path, contradiction — or u is some vᵢ on the path, giving a cycle, contradicting acyclicity. So v₀ has in-degree 0. ∎

    This is the existence proof underlying Kahn's algorithm: the queue is never empty while vertices remain, so topological sort always succeeds on a DAG.

  8. Program

    Implement a 15-puzzle solvability checker in O(n²) or O(n log n), generalised to k × k boards.

    Solution
    def solvable(board, k):          # board: flat list, 0 = blank
    tiles = [t for t in board if t != 0]
    inv = sum(1 for i in range(len(tiles))
                for j in range(i+1, len(tiles)) if tiles[i] > tiles[j])
    if k % 2 == 1:
        return inv % 2 == 0                       # odd width: parity of inv alone
    blank_row_from_bottom = k - (board.index(0) // k)
    return (inv + blank_row_from_bottom) % 2 == 1

    The width parity matters because a vertical move jumps the blank over k-1 tiles: when k is odd that is an even number of inversions changed (invariant is inversions alone), when k is even it is odd (so the blank's row must be folded in). Replace the O(n²) inversion count with a BIT for O(n log n) on large boards.

  9. Program

    Count inversions in an array of up to 10⁶ elements in O(n log n), two ways: merge sort and a Fenwick tree.

    Solution
    # merge sort: count pairs crossing the split
def count(a):
    if len(a) < 2: return a, 0
    m = len(a)//2
    L, x = count(a[:m]); R, y = count(a[m:])
    out, i, j, c = [], 0, 0, x + y
    while i < len(L) and j < len(R):
        if L[i] <= R[j]: out.append(L[i]); i += 1
        else: out.append(R[j]); j += 1; c += len(L) - i   # L[i:] all beat R[j]
    return out + L[i:] + R[j:], c

# Fenwick: sweep right-to-left, count already-seen smaller values
def count_bit(a):
    r = {v:i+1 for i,v in enumerate(sorted(set(a)))}      # coordinate compress
    bit = [0]*(len(r)+1); total = 0
    for v in reversed(a):
        k = r[v]-1
        while k: total += bit[k]; k -= k & -k             # query prefix
        k = r[v]
        while k <= len(r): bit[k] += 1; k += k & -k       # update
    return total

    Both are O(n log n). The merge version needs no compression and is cache-friendlier; the Fenwick version generalises to weighted counts and to online queries. The key line in the merge version is c += len(L) - i: when R[j] is emitted, every remaining element of L is larger and to its left, so all of them are inversions — the whole algorithm is that observation.

  10. Program

    Given a chocolate bar of m × n squares, prove that breaking it into unit squares always takes exactly mn - 1 breaks, and write a verifier that plays random break sequences.

    Solution

    Monovariant / invariant: each break increases the number of pieces by exactly 1. Starting at 1 piece and ending at mn, the number of breaks is mn - 1 regardless of strategy. ∎ No search needed, and no strategy is better than another — which is the surprising part.

    import random
def play(m, n):
    pieces, breaks = [(m, n)], 0
    while any(p != (1,1) for p in pieces):
        i = random.choice([i for i,p in enumerate(pieces) if p != (1,1)])
        a, b = pieces.pop(i); breaks += 1
        if a > 1 and (b == 1 or random.random() < .5):
            k = random.randint(1, a-1); pieces += [(k,b), (a-k,b)]
        else:
            k = random.randint(1, b-1); pieces += [(a,k), (a,b-k)]
    return breaks
assert all(play(m,n) == m*n - 1 for m in range(1,7) for n in range(1,7))

    This is the cleanest possible illustration that an invariant can settle a question that looks like it needs game analysis.

  11. Program

    Implement a solver for the "water jug" problem (jugs of capacity a and b, target c) using BFS, and separately decide reachability in O(log) using an invariant.

    Solution

    Invariant: every reachable amount is an integer combination of a and b, so it is a multiple of g = gcd(a,b). Conversely every multiple of g up to max(a,b) is reachable (Bézout, Chapter 15). So:

    def reachable(a, b, c):
    from math import gcd
    return c == 0 or (c <= max(a, b) and c % gcd(a, b) == 0)

    The BFS over states (x,y) with 0 ≤ x ≤ a, 0 ≤ y ≤ b and six moves (fill either, empty either, pour either way) explores O(ab) states and confirms this empirically. For a,b ≤ 10⁹ only the invariant version is viable — the classic case where a number-theoretic invariant collapses an exponential search to a gcd.

  12. Program

    Write a stress test that searches for an invariant automatically: given a small state space and a move relation, compute the reachable set from a start state by BFS and report which simple functions (sum mod k, parity of a coordinate, …) are constant on it.

    Solution
    from itertools import product
reach = bfs(start, moves)                 # set of reachable states
cands = {}
for k in range(2, 13):
    vals = {sum(s) % k for s in reach}
    if len(vals) == 1: cands[f"sum mod {k}"] = vals.pop()
for i in range(dim):
    vals = {s[i] % 2 for s in reach}
    if len(vals) == 1: cands[f"coord {i} parity"] = vals.pop()
print(cands)

    This is a genuinely useful contest technique: brute-force the reachable set for small parameters, look for a constant statistic, then prove it preserved by each move and generalise. It converts "guess the invariant" from inspiration into search. The caveat is that a statistic constant on a small reachable set may not be invariant in general — the program suggests, the proof decides.

  13. Program

    Prove and implement: a knight on an infinite board cannot return to its start in an odd number of moves. Verify by BFS on an 8×8 board.

    Solution

    Invariant: colour the board like a chessboard. Every knight move changes colour (a (1,2) displacement changes x+y by an odd amount). So after k moves the knight is on its starting colour iff k is even. Returning to the start requires the starting colour, hence an even k. ∎

    from collections import deque
dist = {(0,0): 0}; q = deque([(0,0)])
D = [(1,2),(2,1),(-1,2),(-2,1),(1,-2),(2,-1),(-1,-2),(-2,-1)]
while q:
    x, y = q.popleft()
    for dx, dy in D:
        n = (x+dx, y+dy)
        if 0 <= n[0] < 8 and 0 <= n[1] < 8 and n not in dist:
            dist[n] = dist[(x,y)] + 1; q.append(n)
assert all((x+y) % 2 == d % 2 for (x,y), d in dist.items())

    The assertion is the invariant, checked on every reachable square: shortest-path parity equals coordinate-sum parity. It also proves BFS distances on a knight graph are never both even and odd for the same square — the graph is bipartite, which is the general statement of this colouring argument.

  14. Program

    Implement a lexicographic termination checker: given a multi-counter loop, assert that a tuple variant strictly decreases lexicographically each iteration. Apply it to the Euclidean algorithm on pairs and to a nested loop with a reset.

    Solution
    def lex_lt(a, b): return a < b            # tuples compare lexicographically
prev = variant(state)
while not done(state):
    state = step(state)
    cur = variant(state)
    assert all(v >= 0 for v in cur), "variant left the well-founded domain"
    assert lex_lt(cur, prev), f"not decreasing: {prev} -> {cur}"
    prev = cur

    Euclid on pairs: variant (a, b) with a ≥ b — the pair strictly decreases lexicographically even though a alone does not on the swap step.

    Nested loop with reset: an inner counter that resets to n whenever the outer counter drops. No single counter decreases (the inner one jumps up), but (outer, inner) decreases lexicographically every iteration, and lexicographic order on ℕᵏ is well-founded — so it terminates. This is exactly the pattern that defeats naive termination checkers and exactly what the tuple variant is for.

II. Asymptotics and the Analysis of Algorithms
Chapter 08

Asymptotic notation, formally

Big-O is a set of functions defined by a quantifier sandwich, not a synonym for "roughly". Getting the definition exact fixes the three mistakes almost everyone makes: confusing O with Θ, asserting tightness that was never proved, and reasoning about constants that the notation deliberately discarded.

The definitions, stated as the quantified formulas they are:

f = O(g) ⟺ ∃c > 0 ∃n₀ ∀n ≥ n₀ : 0 ≤ f(n) ≤ c·g(n) upper bound f = Ω(g) ⟺ ∃c > 0 ∃n₀ ∀n ≥ n₀ : 0 ≤ c·g(n) ≤ f(n) lower bound f = Θ(g) ⟺ f = O(g) and f = Ω(g) tight bound f = o(g) ⟺ ∀c > 0 ∃n₀ ∀n ≥ n₀ : 0 ≤ f(n) < c·g(n) strictly smaller f = ω(g) ⟺ ∀c > 0 ∃n₀ ∀n ≥ n₀ : 0 ≤ c·g(n) < f(n) strictly larger

Two structural facts are visible in the quantifiers. First, O and Ω begin with ∃c, so one constant suffices; o and ω begin with ∀c, which is far stronger. Second, everything is eventual: behaviour below n₀ is irrelevant, so no finite amount of measurement can establish or refute an asymptotic claim.

The = sign is an abuse of notation. O(g) is a set of functions and the honest statement is f ∈ O(g). The abuse is harmless in one direction and dangerous in the other: n = O(n²) and n² = O(n²) are both true, but you may not conclude n = n². Read = as "is", never as "equals".

The three mistakes

1. Saying O when you mean Θ. "Quicksort is O(n log n)" is true but weak; it is also O(n²) and O(2ⁿ), all correct upper bounds. The precise statements are: worst case Θ(n²), expected case Θ(n log n).

2. Confusing case with bound. Best/average/worst case selects which function of n you are describing; O/Θ/Ω describes how tightly you are describing it. They are orthogonal, and "the worst case is Ω(n log n)" is a perfectly sensible sentence that people wrongly correct.

3. Believing asymptotics predict runtime. An O(n log n) algorithm with a 500× constant loses to an O(n²) one until n is large. Galactic algorithms — matrix multiplication at O(n²·³⁷²), for example — have constants so large they are never run.

Principle

Asymptotics answer "how does cost scale?" not "how long does it take?". In competitive programming the useful translation is the operations budget: roughly 10⁸ simple operations per second. At n = 10⁵, an O(n²) solution is 10¹⁰ and fails; O(n log n) is 1.7·10⁶ and passes comfortably. That translation, not the notation, is what you actually use under time pressure.

The growth hierarchy

Classn=10⁶Typical source
O(1)1hash lookup, arithmetic
O(α(n))< 5union-find
O(log n)20binary search, balanced tree
O(√n)1,000trial division, sqrt decomposition
O(n)10⁶one pass
O(n log n)2·10⁷sorting, divide and conquer
O(n√n)10⁹Mo's algorithm
O(n²)10¹²all pairs — dead above n ≈ 10⁴
O(2ⁿ)—subsets — dead above n ≈ 25
O(n!)—permutations — dead above n ≈ 11

The reverse reading of this table is the one that wins contests: given the constraint, infer the intended complexity. n ≤ 20 means bitmask DP. n ≤ 500 means O(n³). n ≤ 10⁵ means O(n log n). n ≤ 10¹⁸ means O(log n) or a closed form.

Comparing growth rates

The limit test decides most cases: if lim f/g = 0 then f = o(g); if the limit is a positive constant, f = Θ(g); if it is ∞, then f = ω(g). If the limit does not exist, the functions may still be comparable, or genuinely incomparable — f(n) = n^(1+sin n) is neither O(n) nor Ω(n²).

Facts worth memorising
log^k n  =  o(n^ε)      for every k and every ε > 0     (logs beat nothing)
n^k      =  o(c^n)      for every k and every c > 1     (polys lose to exps)
c^n      =  o(n!)                                        n! ≈ (n/e)^n √(2πn)
n!       =  o(n^n)
log(n!)  =  Θ(n log n)                                   Stirling
Σ_{i=1..n} 1/i = ln n + γ + o(1) = Θ(log n)              harmonic
a^log b  =  b^log a                                      same base throughout
log_a n  =  Θ(log_b n)     ⟹ the base of a log inside Θ is irrelevant
                             BUT 2^log₂n = n while 2^log₁₀n = n^0.30
                             — the base matters in an exponent
Interview angle

When asked for a complexity, give the tight bound, name the case, and say what dominates: "Θ(n log n) expected, dominated by the sort; worst case Θ(n²) if the pivot is adversarial, which randomising the pivot removes." Three clauses, and it demonstrates you know what the notation is hiding.

Problem set — 15 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove from the definition that 3n² + 10n + 7 = Θ(n²), giving explicit constants.

    Solution

    Upper: for n ≥ 1, 10n ≤ 10n² and 7 ≤ 7n², so f(n) ≤ 20n². Take c₂ = 20, n₀ = 1.

    Lower: all terms are non-negative for n ≥ 0, so f(n) ≥ 3n². Take c₁ = 3, n₀ = 1.

    Hence 3n² ≤ f(n) ≤ 20n² for n ≥ 1, so f = Θ(n²). ∎ Note the constants are not unique and no smallest one is required — only existence.

  2. Theory

    Is 2ⁿ⁺¹ = O(2ⁿ)? Is 2²ⁿ = O(2ⁿ)? Justify both.

    Solution

    2ⁿ⁺¹ = 2·2ⁿ, so with c = 2 it is O(2ⁿ) — yes. A constant factor in the exponent's offset is a constant factor overall.

    2²ⁿ = (2ⁿ)², and 2²ⁿ/2ⁿ = 2ⁿ → ∞, so no constant c works — no, it is ω(2ⁿ). A constant factor in the exponent's coefficient is an exponential factor overall.

    This is why "O(2ⁿ) so the constant does not matter" is wrong: halving the exponent (meet-in-the-middle, O(2^{n/2})) is the difference between n = 20 and n = 40.

  3. Theory

    Order by growth rate, grouping ties: n log n, n^1.01, 2^{log₂ n}, n / log n, (log n)^{log n}, n^{1/log n}, 2^{√(log n)}.

    Solution

    Take logs of everything: log n + log log n; 1.01 log n; log n (since 2^{log₂n} = n); log n - log log n; (log n)(log log n); (log n)/(log n) = 1, i.e. the function is the constant 2; √(log n).

    n^{1/log n} = 2 constant 2^{√log n} sub-polynomial, super-polylog n/log n 2^{log₂ n} = n (tie with nothing else here) n log n n^{1.01} beats n log n since 0.01 log n → ∞ beats log log n (log n)^{log n} = 2^{(log log n)(log n)}, super-polynomial

    The technique — compare logarithms, since log is strictly increasing — is the general method and it handles every case above mechanically.

  4. Theory

    Prove or disprove: if f = O(g) then 2^{f} = O(2^{g}).

    Solution

    False. Take f(n) = 2n and g(n) = n. Then f = O(g) with c = 2, but 2^{f} = 4ⁿ and 2^{g} = 2ⁿ, and 4ⁿ/2ⁿ = 2ⁿ → ∞. ∎

    The reason: O hides multiplicative constants, and exponentiation turns a multiplicative constant into an exponential one. The correct statement needs f ≤ g + O(1), not f = O(g). The same trap kills "f = O(g) ⇒ f! = O(g!)".

  5. Theory

    Prove that O(f) + O(g) = O(max(f,g)) and use it to justify why analysing a sequence of phases means taking the maximum.

    Solution

    Let h₁ ≤ c₁f for n ≥ n₁ and h₂ ≤ c₂g for n ≥ n₂. For n ≥ max(n₁,n₂), h₁+h₂ ≤ c₁f + c₂g ≤ (c₁+c₂)·max(f,g). ∎

    Consequence: an algorithm that sorts (Θ(n log n)) then does a linear scan (Θ(n)) is Θ(n log n) overall — the phases do not add up in the notation, the largest simply absorbs the rest. This is also why micro-optimising a linear pass inside an O(n²) algorithm is wasted effort, and why max and + are interchangeable in asymptotic sums.

  6. Theory

    Give functions f, g with neither f = O(g) nor g = O(f).

    Solution

    Let f(n) = n and g(n) = n^{1 + sin(n)}, so g oscillates between n⁰ and n² infinitely often. Then f/g is unbounded and g/f is unbounded, so neither bound holds.

    A cleaner discrete version: f(n) = n, and g(n) = n² for even n, 1 for odd n. ∎

    The point is that asymptotic comparison is a partial order (Chapter 5), not a total one. Every real algorithm's cost function is comparable in practice, which is why this is easy to forget until a proof needs the general case.

  7. Theory

    Prove log(n!) = Θ(n log n) without invoking Stirling's formula.

    Solution

    Upper: n! ≤ nⁿ, so log(n!) ≤ n log n.

    Lower: the top half of the factors are each at least n/2, so n! ≥ (n/2)^{n/2}, giving log(n!) ≥ (n/2)(log n - 1) = Ω(n log n).

    Hence Θ(n log n). ∎ This is the bound behind the Ω(n log n) comparison-sort lower bound of Chapter 13: a decision tree with n! leaves has depth at least log₂(n!).

  8. Theory

    An algorithm runs in T(n) = 10⁶ n and another in T(n) = n². For which n is the first faster? Discuss what this means for asymptotic reasoning in practice.

    Solution

    10⁶n < n² iff n > 10⁶. So the "better" linear algorithm is slower for every input below a million.

    What it means: asymptotics are a statement about the limit, and the crossover point is invisible to them. Real engineering answers this with hybrid algorithms — std::sort switches to insertion sort below ~16 elements, Karatsuba multiplication reverts to schoolbook below ~32 limbs, and Timsort has a whole minrun heuristic. Each is an explicit acknowledgement that the constant beats the exponent in the range that actually occurs.

  9. Program

    Write a program that empirically estimates the exponent k in T(n) ≈ c nᵏ by timing an algorithm at several sizes and fitting log T against log n. Apply it to bubble sort and to sorted().

    Solution
    import time, random, math
def timeit(f, n, reps=3):
    best = float('inf')
    for _ in range(reps):
        a = [random.random() for _ in range(n)]
        t = time.perf_counter(); f(a); best = min(best, time.perf_counter()-t)
    return best

def exponent(f, sizes):
    xs = [math.log(n) for n in sizes]
    ys = [math.log(timeit(f, n)) for n in sizes]
    mx, my = sum(xs)/len(xs), sum(ys)/len(ys)
    num = sum((x-mx)*(y-my) for x, y in zip(xs, ys))
    den = sum((x-mx)**2 for x in xs)
    return num/den                       # slope = estimated k

print(exponent(bubble, [200, 400, 800, 1600]))     # ≈ 2.0
print(exponent(sorted, [10**5, 2*10**5, 4*10**5, 8*10**5]))   # ≈ 1.05

    The sort measures ~1.05 rather than 1.0 because n log n looks like n^{1+log log n/log n} over any finite range — a genuine limitation of the method: it cannot distinguish n log n from n^{1.05} empirically. That is the experimental face of the fact that asymptotic claims are not falsifiable by measurement.

  10. Program

    Given a problem constraint, output the intended complexity class. Write the lookup as code and justify each threshold with the 10⁸ ops/second budget.

    Solution
    def intended(n):
    if n <= 11:      return "O(n!)      permutations / brute force"
    if n <= 25:      return "O(2^n)     subset enumeration, meet-in-middle at 40"
    if n <= 100:     return "O(n^4) or O(n^3 log n)"
    if n <= 500:     return "O(n^3)     Floyd-Warshall, matrix ops"
    if n <= 5000:    return "O(n^2)     DP over pairs"
    if n <= 10**5:   return "O(n sqrt n) or O(n log^2 n)"
    if n <= 10**6:   return "O(n log n)  sort, heap, divide and conquer"
    if n <= 10**7:   return "O(n)        single pass, no allocation per element"
    if n <= 10**12:  return "O(sqrt n)   trial division, Pollard rho"
    return "O(log n) or closed form"

    The thresholds come from 10⁸ operations/second and a typical 1–2 s limit: n² at n = 5000 is 2.5·10⁷ (fine), at n = 10⁵ is 10¹⁰ (hopeless). Reading the constraint first is the single most useful contest habit, because it tells you which algorithms are even candidates before you think about the problem.

  11. Program

    Implement three algorithms for maximum subarray sum — O(n³), O(n²) and Kadane's O(n) — and measure the largest n each handles in one second.

    Solution
    def kadane(a):                      # O(n)
    best = cur = a[0]
    for x in a[1:]:
        cur = max(x, cur + x)       # extend or restart
        best = max(best, cur)
    return best

def quad(a):                        # O(n^2)
    best = a[0]
    for i in range(len(a)):
        s = 0
        for j in range(i, len(a)):
            s += a[j]; best = max(best, s)
    return best

    Rough limits at 10⁸ ops/s in a compiled language: cubic ~ n = 500, quadratic ~ n = 10⁴, Kadane ~ n = 10⁸. In Python divide each by roughly 50.

    Kadane's correctness is the invariant "cur is the maximum sum of a subarray ending exactly at the current index" — proved by induction, since such a subarray either extends the previous one or starts fresh. The whole n³ → n improvement is that one observation, which is typical: complexity classes are crossed by finding structure, not by optimising code.

  12. Program

    Write a benchmark demonstrating that an O(n) algorithm with poor cache behaviour can lose to an O(n log n) one with good locality. Explain what the model omits.

    Solution
    // A: O(n) — random access into a large array (cache-hostile)
long long a = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) a += data[perm[i]];        // random order

// B: O(n log n) — sort a copy, then a sequential scan
sort(copy.begin(), copy.end());
long long b = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) b += copy[i];              // sequential

    At n = 10⁸ with 4-byte ints (400 MB), loop A incurs a cache miss on nearly every access — about 100 ns each, so ~10 s — while the sort plus sequential scan streams at memory bandwidth and finishes in ~2 s despite doing log n = 27× more "operations".

    What the RAM model omits: it charges 1 for every memory access, but real hardware has a 100× spread between L1 and DRAM. The external-memory (cache-oblivious) model fixes this by counting block transfers instead, and it is why B-trees beat binary search trees on disk and why array-of-structs versus struct-of-arrays changes runtime without changing complexity.

  13. Program

    Implement a function that counts the exact number of basic operations an algorithm performs, and use it to verify a claimed Θ bound by checking that T(2n)/T(n) approaches the expected ratio.

    Solution
    class Counter:
    def __init__(self): self.n = 0
    def tick(self, k=1): self.n += k

def count_ops(alg, n):
    c = Counter(); alg(list(range(n)), c); return c.n

for n in [1000, 2000, 4000, 8000, 16000]:
    print(n, count_ops(alg, n), count_ops(alg, 2*n) / count_ops(alg, n))

    Expected ratios T(2n)/T(n): 2 for linear, slightly above 2 for n log n (precisely 2 + 2/log₂n), 4 for quadratic, 8 for cubic. Counting operations rather than timing removes constant factors, JIT warm-up and cache effects, so the ratio converges cleanly — this is the right way to check an implementation matches its claimed complexity in a test suite. Assert 1.9 < ratio < 2.3 and a future refactor that accidentally makes the algorithm quadratic fails CI.

  14. Program

    Given n ≤ 40 integers, find whether a subset sums to S. Naive is O(2ⁿ) — implement meet-in-the-middle at O(2^{n/2} · n) and explain the asymptotic significance.

    Solution
    def subset_sum(a, S):
    n = len(a); h = n // 2
    def sums(xs):
        r = [0]
        for x in xs: r += [v + x for v in r]
        return r
    L, R = sums(a[:h]), sorted(sums(a[h:]))
    import bisect
    for v in L:
        i = bisect.bisect_left(R, S - v)
        if i < len(R) and R[i] == S - v: return True
    return False

    At n = 40: naive is 2⁴⁰ ≈ 10¹² (hours); meet-in-the-middle is 2²⁰ = 10⁶ per half plus a sort, well under a second. The improvement is not a constant factor and not a change of complexity class in the usual sense — it halves the exponent, which is exactly the distinction problem 2 makes. This is the standard escape hatch when n sits in the 30–45 window: too big for 2ⁿ, too small for anything polynomial to exist.

  15. Program

    Write a script that, given two implementations, finds the crossover input size where the asymptotically better one starts winning, using binary search over n.

    Solution
    def crossover(fast, slow, lo=1, hi=10**7):
    # invariant: slow wins at lo, fast wins at hi (verify before searching)
    while lo < hi:
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        if timeit(fast, mid) < timeit(slow, mid): hi = mid
        else: lo = mid + 1
    return lo

    Two caveats that make this harder than it looks. First, the predicate must be monotone for binary search to be valid — timing noise breaks monotonicity, so take the minimum of several runs and require a margin (say fast < 0.9·slow) before deciding. Second, the crossover moves with the machine, the allocator and the data distribution, so the number is an observation about one environment, not a property of the algorithms.

    Used properly this is how you pick the cutoff for a hybrid algorithm: measure once, hard-code the threshold, and re-measure when the hardware changes.

Chapter 09

Summations and closed forms

Every loop nest is a summation, and every summation you can close is a complexity you can state exactly. A dozen closed forms and three manipulation techniques cover essentially all of algorithm analysis.

Analysing an algorithm means writing its cost as a sum and then evaluating or bounding that sum. The translation is mechanical: a loop is a ∑, nesting is nesting, and the bounds come from the loop bounds.

for i in 1..n: Σ_{i=1..n} ( ... ) for j in i..n: Σ_{j=i..n} ( ... ) O(1) work 1 cost = Σ_{i=1..n} Σ_{j=i..n} 1 = Σ_{i=1..n} (n-i+1) = Σ_{k=1..n} k = n(n+1)/2

Note the reindexing in the last step: substituting k = n-i+1 turned a decreasing sum into the standard triangular one. Reindexing, splitting and bounding are the three moves, and they suffice for nearly everything.

The closed forms to know cold

Σ_{i=1..n} 1 = n Σ_{i=1..n} i = n(n+1)/2 ≈ n²/2 Σ_{i=1..n} i² = n(n+1)(2n+1)/6 ≈ n³/3 Σ_{i=1..n} i³ = [n(n+1)/2]² ≈ n⁴/4 Σ_{i=1..n} i^k = Θ(n^{k+1}/(k+1)) Faulhaber Σ_{i=0..n} rⁱ = (r^{n+1} - 1)/(r - 1) geometric, r ≠ 1 Σ_{i=0..∞} rⁱ = 1/(1-r) |r| < 1 Σ_{i=0..∞} i·rⁱ = r/(1-r)² |r| < 1 Σ_{i=1..n} 1/i = H_n = ln n + γ + 1/(2n) + O(1/n²) γ ≈ 0.5772 Σ_{i=1..n} 1/i² → π²/6 ≈ 1.6449 converges! Σ_{i=1..n} log i = log(n!) = n log n - n + O(log n) Σ_{i=0..n} C(n,i) = 2ⁿ Σ_{i=0..n} i·C(n,i) = n·2^{n-1} Σ_{i=0..n} C(i,k) = C(n+1,k+1) hockey stick

The geometric sum deserves emphasis because it is the one that most often collapses an analysis. 1 + 2 + 4 + … + 2ᵏ = 2ᵏ⁺¹ - 1 < 2·2ᵏ: a geometric series is dominated by its largest term, up to a constant. That single fact explains why doubling a dynamic array is amortised O(1), why the total work in a divide-and-conquer with geometrically shrinking levels is dominated by one level, and why a geometric-backoff retry loop has bounded total delay.

Principle

If the terms grow or shrink geometrically, the sum is Θ(\text{largest term}). If they grow polynomially, the sum is Θ(n · \text{largest term}) up to the degree shift. Recognising which regime you are in answers most summation questions without evaluating anything.

Three techniques

1. Splitting. To bound ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ 1/i from above, split into blocks of size 1, 2, 4, 8, …; each block sums to at most 1, and there are log₂n blocks, giving Hₙ ≤ 1 + log₂n. Blocking with the same trick from below gives Hₙ ≥ 1 + (log₂n)/2. Hence Hₙ = Θ(log n) with no calculus.

2. Integral comparison. For monotone f, ∫₁ⁿ⁺¹ f ≤ ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ f(i) ≤ f(1) + ∫₁ⁿ f (increasing f; reverse for decreasing). This gives ∑ iᵏ ≈ n^{k+1}/(k+1) and Hₙ ≈ ln n immediately, and is the fastest route to the leading term of almost any sum.

3. Perturbation. To evaluate S = ∑ᵢ₋₀ⁿ i·2ᵢ, write 2S, shift the index, subtract. The telescoping leaves a geometric sum you already know:

Perturbation, worked
S  = Σ_{i=0..n} i·2ⁱ
2S = Σ_{i=0..n} i·2^{i+1} = Σ_{j=1..n+1} (j-1)·2ʲ

2S - S = (n)·2^{n+1} - Σ_{j=1..n} 2ʲ        ← after aligning indices
S      = n·2^{n+1} - (2^{n+1} - 2)
       = (n-1)·2^{n+1} + 2

Check n = 3:  0 + 2 + 8 + 24 = 34,  and (3-1)·16 + 2 = 34.  ✓

Always check a closed form at a small n. Off-by-one errors in summation bounds are the single most common analysis mistake, and a 5-second check at n = 1, 2, 3 catches nearly all of them.

Sums that appear in real analyses

SumValueWhere it appears
∑ n/2ᵢ< 2nheapify is O(n), not O(n log n)
∑ 2ᵢ to n< 2ndynamic array doubling
∑ n/in Hₙ ≈ n ln nsieve of Eratosthenes, harmonic loops
∑ log iΘ(n log n)building a BST by n insertions
∑ iΘ(n²)insertion sort, naive string concat
∑ √iΘ(n¹·⁵)sqrt decomposition rebuilds

The first and third rows are the ones people get wrong. for i in 1..n: for j in i..n step i runs ∑ n/i = Θ(n log n) times, not Θ(n²) — the harmonic series is why the sieve is nearly linear.

Interview angle

"Why is building a heap O(n) when each sift-down is O(log n)?" The answer is a summation: nodes at height h number about n/2Ḥ⁺¹ and cost O(h), so the total is n ∑ h/2Ḥ ≤ 2n. The naive n · log n multiplies the worst case by the count instead of summing the actual costs, and spotting that difference is the whole question.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Evaluate ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ i(i+1) in closed form.

    Solution

    Split: ∑ i² + ∑ i = n(n+1)(2n+1)/6 + n(n+1)/2 = n(n+1)[(2n+1)+3]/6 = n(n+1)(n+2)/3.

    Check n = 2: 1·2 + 2·3 = 8, and 2·3·4/3 = 8. ✓ The result n(n+1)(n+2)/3 = 2·C(n+2,3) is not a coincidence — i(i+1) = 2·C(i+1,2) and hockey stick gives the sum directly, which is the slicker derivation.

  2. Theory

    Compute ∑ᵢ₋₀ⁿ i²·2ᵢ using perturbation.

    Solution

    Let S = ∑ i²2ᵢ. Then 2S = ∑₃₋₁ⁿ⁺¹ (j-1)²2₌, so

    S = 2S - S = n²2ⁿ⁺¹ + ∑₃₋₁ⁿ[(j-1)² - j²]2₌ = n²2ⁿ⁺¹ - ∑₃₋₁ⁿ(2j-1)2₌.

    Using ∑ j2₌ = (n-1)2ⁿ⁺¹+2 and ∑ 2₌ = 2ⁿ⁺¹-2:

    S = n²2ⁿ⁺¹ - 2[(n-1)2ⁿ⁺¹+2] + (2ⁿ⁺¹-2) = (n²-2n+3)2ⁿ⁺¹ - 6.

    Check n = 2: 0 + 2 + 16 = 18, and (4-4+3)·8 - 6 = 18. ✓

  3. Theory

    Prove Hₙ = Θ(log n) by the blocking argument, giving explicit constants.

    Solution

    Upper: group terms as 1, (1/2+1/3), (1/4+…+1/7), … The k-th block has 2ᵏ terms each at most 2⁻ᵏ, so it sums to at most 1. With ⌈log₂(n+1)⌉ blocks, Hₙ ≤ 1 + log₂n.

    Lower: the same blocks each have 2ᵏ terms each at least 2^{-(k+1)}, so each sums to at least 1/2, giving Hₙ ≥ 1 + (log₂n)/2 for n = 2ᵏ.

    So (1/2)log₂n ≤ Hₙ ≤ 1 + log₂n and Hₙ = Θ(log n). ∎ The true constant is ln n = 0.693 log₂n, comfortably inside these bounds.

  4. Theory

    Show that the sieve of Eratosthenes performs Θ(n log log n) operations, not Θ(n log n).

    Solution

    The work is ∑p ≤ n, p prime n/p = n ∑p ≤ n 1/p. Mertens' second theorem gives ∑p ≤ n 1/p = ln ln n + M + o(1) with M ≈ 0.2615. Hence Θ(n log log n). ∎

    The naive bound ∑i ≤ n n/i = n Hₙ = Θ(n log n) is what you get if you cross out multiples of every integer rather than only primes — the difference between log n and log log n is exactly the sparsity of the primes. At n = 10⁸, log log n ≈ 2.9 versus log n ≈ 18, a 6× difference that is very visible in practice.

  5. Theory

    Prove that heapify is O(n) by summing the actual sift-down costs.

    Solution

    In a heap of n elements, the number of nodes at height h is at most ⌈n/2^{h+1}⌉, and sifting down from height h costs O(h). Total:

    ∑h=0..⌊log n⌋ (n/2^{h+1})·O(h) = O(n ∑h≥0 h/2Ḥ) = O(n · 2) = O(n),

    using ∑ h xḤ = x/(1-x)² at x = 1/2, which is 2. ∎

    The intuition to state aloud: half the nodes are leaves and cost nothing, a quarter cost 1, an eighth cost 2 — the expensive nodes are exponentially rare, and the geometric series converges. Inserting n elements one at a time is genuinely Θ(n log n) because there the expensive operations are the common ones.

  6. Theory

    What is ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ ∑₃₋₁ᵢ 1/j? Give the asymptotic.

    Solution

    Swap the order of summation. The pair (i,j) is counted when j ≤ i ≤ n, so

    ∑₃₋₁ⁿ (1/j)·|{i : j ≤ i ≤ n}| = ∑₃₋₁ⁿ (n-j+1)/j = (n+1)Hₙ - n.

    So the value is (n+1)Hₙ - n = Θ(n log n). ∎

    Exchanging the order of summation is the most reliable technique for double sums: rewrite the index constraint as a region, then integrate over it in the other order. It is the discrete Fubini theorem, and it turns many analyses that look quadratic into n log n.

  7. Theory

    An algorithm at each step of size k does k work and recurses on k/2. Write the total as a sum and evaluate. Repeat when it does k² work, and when it does √k.

    Solution

    Work k: n + n/2 + n/4 + … = n∑2⁻ᵢ < 2n = Θ(n) — geometric, dominated by the top.

    Work k²: n² + n²/4 + n²/16 + … = n²·(4/3) = Θ(n²) — even more strongly top-dominated.

    Work √k: √n(1 + 1/√2 + 1/2 + …) = √n · 1/(1-1/√2) ≈ 3.41√n = Θ(√n) — still geometric, still top-dominated.

    All three are Θ(\text{top-level work}), and that is the general rule for a single recursive call on a constant fraction: the levels form a geometric series with ratio < 1. It changes only when the branching factor multiplies the work back up, which is the master theorem of Chapter 10.

  8. Program

    Write a program that verifies a claimed closed form against direct summation for n = 1..10⁵, using exact integer arithmetic. Test it on the four Faulhaber formulas.

    Solution
    def check(term, closed, N=100000):
    s = 0
    for n in range(1, N+1):
        s += term(n)
        if s != closed(n):
            return ("mismatch at n =", n, s, closed(n))
    return "ok"

print(check(lambda i: i,      lambda n: n*(n+1)//2))
print(check(lambda i: i*i,    lambda n: n*(n+1)*(2*n+1)//6))
print(check(lambda i: i**3,   lambda n: (n*(n+1)//2)**2))
print(check(lambda i: i**4,   lambda n: n*(n+1)*(2*n+1)*(3*n*n+3*n-1)//30))

    Use integer division only where divisibility is guaranteed — n(n+1) is always even, n(n+1)(2n+1) is always divisible by 6 — otherwise // silently truncates and the check "passes" on a wrong formula for some n. In a language with fixed-width integers this loop also overflows around n = 10⁵ for the cube formula (n⁴/4 ≈ 2.5·10¹⁹ exceeds 2&sup6;³ only past n ≈ 2·10⁴… but the intermediate (n(n+1)/2)² does not) — worth checking deliberately.

  9. Program

    Count exactly how many times the inner statement runs for for i in 1..n: for j in 1..n step i. Compare the measured count to n Hₙ.

    Solution
    def count(n):
    c = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        j = 1
        while j <= n: c += 1; j += i
    return c

import math
for n in [10**3, 10**4, 10**5]:
    c = count(n)
    H = sum(1.0/i for i in range(1, n+1))
    print(n, c, n*H, c / (n*H))         # ratio → 1

    The exact count is ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ ⌊n/i⌋, which equals n Hₙ - O(n) because each floor loses less than 1. The ratio approaches 1 from below. This same sum ∑⌊n/i⌋ is also the total number of divisors of all integers up to n, and it can be computed in O(√n) by the divisor-block trick — grouping equal values of ⌊n/i⌋, which is Chapter 19's technique.

  10. Program

    Implement ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ ⌊n/i⌋ in O(√n) for n ≤ 10¹².

    Solution

    ⌊n/i⌋ takes only O(√n) distinct values, and the i giving each value form a contiguous block.

    def divisor_sum(n):
    total, i = 0, 1
    while i <= n:
        q = n // i
        j = n // q                     # largest index with the same quotient
        total += q * (j - i + 1)
        i = j + 1
    return total

    Why blocks: if q = ⌊n/i⌋ then every i' ≤ ⌊n/q⌋ gives the same quotient, and ⌊n/q⌋ is the exact right endpoint. There are at most 2√n blocks because for i ≤ √n there are √n indices, and for i > √n the quotient is below √n so there are at most √n distinct values. At n = 10¹² that is 2 million iterations — instant, versus a trillion for the naive loop.

  11. Program

    Empirically demonstrate that repeated string concatenation is Θ(n²) while joining a list is Θ(n), and explain the summation behind each.

    Solution
    def quadratic(n):
    s = ""
    for i in range(n): s += "x"       # copies the whole prefix each time
    return s

def linear(n):
    parts = []
    for i in range(n): parts.append("x")
    return "".join(parts)             # one allocation, one pass

    Concatenation: step i copies i characters, so the total is ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ i = Θ(n²). (CPython has an in-place optimisation that sometimes hides this for str; the quadratic behaviour is immediate and unavoidable in Java with String in a loop, which is why StringBuilder exists.)

    Join: appends are amortised O(1) by array doubling — total copy work ∑ 2ᵢ < 2n — and the final join allocates once and copies n characters. Total Θ(n).

    The two summations, ∑i versus ∑2ᵢ, are exactly the difference between the arithmetic and geometric regimes from the chapter's Principle box.

  12. Program

    Given q queries each asking for ∑ᵢ₋₂₋ᵣ aᵢ on a static array, answer all in O(n+q). Extend to 2D and to a single point update.

    Solution
    // 1D prefix sums
pre[0] = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) pre[i+1] = pre[i] + a[i];
auto query = [&](int l, int r){ return pre[r+1] - pre[l]; };   // O(1)

// 2D: inclusion-exclusion over four corners
P[i+1][j+1] = A[i][j] + P[i][j+1] + P[i+1][j] - P[i][j];
sum(r1,c1,r2,c2) = P[r2+1][c2+1] - P[r1][c2+1] - P[r2+1][c1] + P[r1][c1];

    With updates, prefix sums cost O(n) per update, so switch to a Fenwick tree: O(log n) both ways. The choice is a summation trade-off — prefix sums pay O(n) once for O(1) queries, Fenwick pays O(log n) always. With u updates and q queries, prefix sums win when u·n < (u+q)log n, i.e. essentially when updates are rare.

    The 2D formula is inclusion–exclusion (Chapter 30) applied to rectangles, and the sign pattern is the same alternation.

  13. Program

    Write a function computing ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ iᵏ mod p for n ≤ 10¹⁸ and k ≤ 100. State the method.

    Solution

    Sᵏ(n) = ∑ iᵏ is a polynomial in n of degree k+1 (Faulhaber). So evaluate it at k+2 points and use Lagrange interpolation on consecutive points, which is O(k) after O(k) precomputation.

    def power_sum(n, k, p):
    m = k + 2
    y, s = [], 0
    for i in range(1, m+1):
        s = (s + pow(i, k, p)) % p; y.append(s)     # S_k(1..m)
    if n <= m: return y[n-1]
    pre = [1]*(m+1); suf = [1]*(m+2)
    for i in range(1, m+1): pre[i] = pre[i-1] * ((n - i) % p) % p
    for i in range(m, 0, -1): suf[i] = suf[i+1] * ((n - i) % p) % p
    fact = [1]*(m+1)
    for i in range(1, m+1): fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i % p
    res = 0
    for i in range(1, m+1):
        num = pre[i-1] * suf[i+1] % p
        den = fact[i-1] * fact[m-i] % p
        term = y[i-1] * num % p * pow(den, p-2, p) % p
        if (m - i) % 2: term = p - term
        res = (res + term) % p
    return res

    O(k log p) per query. The mathematical content is that a degree-(k+1) polynomial is determined by k+2 values — Chapter 52 develops the interpolation; here it is the tool that makes an n = 10¹⁸ summation instant.

  14. Program

    Analyse and implement: a dynamic array that grows by a factor g > 1 when full. Empirically measure total copy work for g = 1.1, 1.5, 2, 4 and compare to the predicted n·g/(g-1).

    Solution
    def total_copies(n, g):
    cap, copies = 1, 0
    for i in range(n):
        if i == cap:
            copies += cap                    # copy the whole old array
            cap = max(cap + 1, int(cap * g))
    return copies
for g in (1.1, 1.5, 2.0, 4.0):
    n = 10**6
    print(g, total_copies(n, g), n * g / (g - 1))

    Derivation: the copies form a geometric series ending at n with ratio 1/g, so the total is n(1 + 1/g + 1/g² + …) = n·g/(g-1). Amortised cost per push is therefore g/(g-1) copies — 2 for g = 2, 3 for g = 1.5, 11 for g = 1.1, and 1.33 for g = 4.

    The trade-off is memory: growth factor g wastes up to (g-1)/g of the allocation. That is why std::vector uses 2 (fast, up to 50% waste) while some allocators prefer 1.5 — a factor below 2 allows reusing previously freed blocks, since 1+g > g² only when g < φ ≈ 1.618. Both choices are defensible and the summation is what makes the trade-off explicit.

Chapter 10

Solving recurrences: trees, master theorem, Akra-Bazzi

A recursive algorithm's cost is a recurrence, and there are exactly three ways to solve one: draw the tree, apply the master theorem, or guess and prove by induction. Knowing which regime you are in — leaf-heavy, root-heavy, or balanced — answers most cases in ten seconds.

A divide-and-conquer algorithm that makes a recursive calls on inputs of size n/b and does f(n) work outside the calls has cost

T(n) = a·T(n/b) + f(n), T(1) = Θ(1)

Everything follows from comparing the work at the top of the recursion tree to the work at the leaves. There are log_b n levels, level i has aᵢ nodes each doing f(n/bᵢ) work, and the leaf count is a^{log_b n} = n^{log_b a}. Write c = log_b a — the critical exponent.

The recursion tree, which is the real method

Mergesort: T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n
level 0        1 node   × n           = n
level 1        2 nodes  × n/2         = n
level 2        4 nodes  × n/4         = n
...
level log n    n nodes  × 1           = n
                                       ─────
                      log n levels × n = Θ(n log n)

Every level costs the same → BALANCED case, multiply by the depth.
Two contrasting shapes
T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n²     ROOT-HEAVY   n² + n²/2 + n²/4 + ... = Θ(n²)
                                     geometric, top dominates

T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n      LEAF-HEAVY   n + 2n + 4n + ... + n² = Θ(n²)
                                     geometric, bottom dominates
                                     (leaves = n^{log₂4} = n²)

Those three pictures — flat, decreasing, increasing — are the entire content of the master theorem. Draw two levels and the leaf count, see which way the series runs, and you have the answer.

The master theorem

T(n) = a·T(n/b) + f(n), a ≥ 1, b > 1, c = log_b a Case 1 (leaf-heavy) f(n) = O(n^{c-ε}) ⟹ T(n) = Θ(n^c) Case 2 (balanced) f(n) = Θ(n^c log^k n) ⟹ T(n) = Θ(n^c log^{k+1} n) Case 3 (root-heavy) f(n) = Ω(n^{c+ε}) ⟹ T(n) = Θ(f(n)) and a·f(n/b) ≤ d·f(n) for some d < 1 (regularity)
Recurrencec = log_b aCaseSolutionAlgorithm
2T(n/2)+n12Θ(n log n)mergesort
2T(n/2)+111Θ(n)tree traversal
T(n/2)+102Θ(log n)binary search
T(n/2)+n03Θ(n)quickselect (balanced)
8T(n/2)+n²31Θ(n³)naive matrix mult
7T(n/2)+n²2.8071Θ(n^{2.807})Strassen
3T(n/2)+n1.5851Θ(n^{1.585})Karatsuba
2T(n/2)+n log n12 (k=1)Θ(n log² n)closest pair (naive merge)
Principle

The gaps in the master theorem are real. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n/log n falls between cases 1 and 2 — n/log n is not O(n^{1-ε}) and not Θ(n logᵏ n) — and the true answer is Θ(n log log n), obtainable only by summing the tree. Case 3 also requires the regularity condition, which real recurrences almost always satisfy but pathological ones do not.

Uneven splits and Akra–Bazzi

When the pieces have different sizes, the master theorem does not apply. Akra–Bazzi handles T(n) = ∑ aᵢT(n/bᵢ) + f(n): find p with ∑ aᵢ/bᵢ^p = 1, then T(n) = Θ(n^p (1 + ∫₁ⁿ f(u)/u^{p+1} du)).

For the median-of-medians selection recurrence T(n) = T(n/5) + T(7n/10) + n: since 1/5 + 7/10 = 9/10 < 1, we get p = 1 and the integral ∫ du/u² converges, giving T(n) = Θ(n). The intuition without machinery: the subproblem sizes shrink by a constant factor 9/10 per level, so the total is a geometric series n(1 + 0.9 + 0.81 + …) = 10n.

That intuition is worth more than the formula. If the child sizes sum to less than n, the recursion is root-heavy and T = Θ(f(n)). If they sum to exactly n, it is balanced and you pay a log. If they sum to more, it is leaf-heavy.

Substitution: guess and prove

For anything the theorems miss, guess a form and verify by induction — including the constants, which is where it goes wrong. To prove T(n) = 2T(⌊n/2⌋) + n is O(n log n), assume T(k) ≤ ck log k for k < n:

T(n) ≤ 2c(n/2)log(n/2) + n = cn log n - cn + n ≤ cn log n whenever c ≥ 1 ✓

The classic failure is proving T(n) = O(n) for the same recurrence by writing T(n) ≤ 2c(n/2) + n = cn + n = O(n). That last step is wrong: you must reach ≤ cn exactly, not cn + n, because the induction requires the same constant at every level. Sloppiness about constants is how n log n gets "proved" linear.

Interview angle

When you propose a divide-and-conquer, state the recurrence before the complexity: "T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(n) for the merge, so Θ(n log n)." It shows the analysis came from the structure rather than recall. If asked to improve it, the recurrence tells you where: reduce a (Strassen, Karatsuba), increase b, or reduce f.

Problem set — 15 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Solve by the master theorem: (a) T(n)=3T(n/2)+n², (b) T(n)=4T(n/2)+n², (c) T(n)=5T(n/2)+n².

    Solution

    All have b = 2 and f = n²; only c = log₂a changes.

    • (a) c = log₂3 ≈ 1.585 < 2: root-heavy, Case 3, Θ(n²).
    • (b) c = 2: balanced, Case 2 with k = 0, Θ(n² log n).
    • (c) c = log₂5 ≈ 2.322 > 2: leaf-heavy, Case 1, Θ(n^{log₂5}).

    The three cases from one parameter change — this triple is the cleanest illustration that the answer is decided by comparing f(n) with n^{log_b a} and nothing else.

  2. Theory

    Solve T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n/log n by summing the recursion tree, and explain why the master theorem fails.

    Solution

    Level i has 2ᵢ nodes of size n/2ᵢ, each costing (n/2ᵢ)/log(n/2ᵢ). Level total: n/(log n - i). Summing i = 0..log n - 1:

    ∑ᵢ n/(log n - i) = n ∑₌₋₁^{log n} 1/j = n H_{log n} = Θ(n log log n).

    The master theorem fails because n/log n is polynomially smaller than n^{c} = n by no fixed ε — for any ε > 0, n/log n is not O(n^{1-ε}) — so Case 1 does not apply, and it is not Θ(n logᵏ n) for k ≥ 0 so Case 2 does not either. This is the standard example of the gap.

  3. Theory

    Solve T(n) = T(n-1) + n and T(n) = T(n-1) + 1/n and T(n) = 2T(n-1) + 1.

    Solution

    These are subtract-and-conquer, not divide-and-conquer, so the master theorem is inapplicable; unroll instead.

    • T(n) = ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ i = Θ(n²) — insertion sort, quicksort worst case.
    • T(n) = Hₙ = Θ(log n) — surprisingly slow-growing.
    • T(n) = 2ⁿ - 1 = Θ(2ⁿ) — Towers of Hanoi. Unroll: T(n)+1 = 2(T(n-1)+1), so T(n)+1 = 2ⁿ(T(0)+1).

    The general rule for T(n) = aT(n-1) + f(n): it is Θ(aⁿ) when a > 1, and Θ(∑ f) when a = 1.

  4. Theory

    Prove by substitution that T(n) = 2T(⌊n/2⌋) + n is O(n log n), handling the floor rigorously.

    Solution

    Claim: T(n) ≤ cn log₂n for n ≥ 2 and suitable c.

    Step: T(n) ≤ 2c⌊n/2⌋log₂⌊n/2⌋ + n ≤ 2c(n/2)log₂(n/2) + n = cn(log₂n - 1) + n = cn log₂n - cn + n, which is ≤ cn log₂n provided c ≥ 1.

    Base: n = 2 gives T(2) = 2T(1)+2, a constant; choose c large enough that c·2·1 ≥ T(2). Note n = 1 cannot be the base because c·1·log 1 = 0 — a real subtlety, resolved by starting the induction at n = 2 and treating n = 1 as part of the constant. ∎

    The floor was handled by ⌊n/2⌋ ≤ n/2 and monotonicity of x log x, which is the standard move; the Akra–Bazzi theorem's tolerance for ±O(n/log²n) perturbations formalises why floors never change the answer.

  5. Theory

    Find the flaw: "T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n. Assume T(k) ≤ ck. Then T(n) ≤ 2c(n/2) + n = cn + n = O(n)."

    Solution

    The induction requires proving T(n) ≤ cn with the same c, and the derivation reaches cn + n, which exceeds cn for every n > 0 and every c. Writing "= O(n)" at the end hides the failure: the extra n per level accumulates over log n levels, which is exactly the log n factor the false proof lost.

    The rule: in a substitution proof, the induction hypothesis must be re-established exactly, with no slack absorbed into asymptotic notation. If you need slack, strengthen the hypothesis (e.g. T(n) ≤ cn - d), which is Chapter 3's technique applied here.

  6. Theory

    Analyse the median-of-medians recurrence T(n) = T(n/5) + T(7n/10) + O(n) and prove linearity by substitution.

    Solution

    Claim T(n) ≤ cn. Then T(n) ≤ c(n/5) + c(7n/10) + an = cn(1/5 + 7/10) + an = 0.9cn + an, and this is ≤ cn iff an ≤ 0.1cn, i.e. c ≥ 10a. ∎

    The 7n/10 comes from the guarantee: the median of medians exceeds at least 3(n/10) elements and is exceeded by at least 3(n/10), so the recursive side has at most 7n/10. The reason group size 5 is chosen: with groups of 3 the recurrence becomes T(n/3)+T(2n/3)+O(n) whose child sizes sum to exactly n — balanced, hence Θ(n log n), not linear. The whole design hinges on 1/5 + 7/10 < 1.

  7. Theory

    Karatsuba multiplies two n-digit numbers with 3 half-size multiplications. Write and solve the recurrence, and give the crossover reasoning against schoolbook.

    Solution

    T(n) = 3T(n/2) + O(n). Here c = log₂3 ≈ 1.585 > 1, so Case 1: T(n) = Θ(n^{1.585}).

    The trick: with x = x₁B + x₀ and y = y₁B + y₀, compute z₂ = x₁y₁, z₀ = x₀y₀, and z₁ = (x₁+x₀)(y₁+y₀) - z₂ - z₀ — three multiplications instead of four, at the cost of extra additions.

    Crossover: schoolbook is n² with a tiny constant; Karatsuba is ≈ 3n^{1.585} with recursion and allocation overhead. In practice GMP switches around 20–40 limbs (roughly 600–1200 bits), then to Toom-Cook, then to FFT-based multiplication above a few thousand limbs. Each transition is a crossover computed exactly as in Chapter 8 problem 15.

  8. Theory

    Use Akra–Bazzi on T(n) = T(n/3) + T(2n/3) + n.

    Solution

    Solve (1/3)^p + (2/3)^p = 1. By inspection p = 1 works. Then

    T(n) = Θ(n(1 + ∫₁ⁿ u/u² du)) = Θ(n(1 + ln n)) = Θ(n log n).

    This matches the tree: the child sizes sum to exactly n, so every level costs n, and the depth is between log₃n and log_{3/2}n — both Θ(log n). It is the quicksort-with-a-guaranteed-1/3-split recurrence, and it explains why even a badly unbalanced but constant-ratio pivot still gives n log n.

  9. Program

    Write a program that numerically solves an arbitrary recurrence T(n) = aT(n/b)+f(n) by memoised evaluation, then estimates the exponent by fitting log T vs log n. Verify against the master theorem on five examples.

    Solution
    from functools import lru_cache
import math

def solver(a, b, f):
    @lru_cache(None)
    def T(n):
        if n <= 1: return 1.0
        return a * T(n // b) + f(n)
    return T

def fit(T, lo=1<<10, hi=1<<22):
    xs, ys = [], []
    n = lo
    while n <= hi:
        xs.append(math.log(n)); ys.append(math.log(T(n))); n *= 2
    mx, my = sum(xs)/len(xs), sum(ys)/len(ys)
    return (sum((x-mx)*(y-my) for x,y in zip(xs,ys))
