            / sum((x-mx)**2 for x in xs))

print(fit(solver(2, 2, lambda n: n)))        # ~1.06  (n log n)
print(fit(solver(4, 2, lambda n: n)))        # ~2.00  (n^2)
print(fit(solver(7, 2, lambda n: n*n)))      # ~2.81  (Strassen)
print(fit(solver(3, 2, lambda n: n)))        # ~1.58  (Karatsuba)
print(fit(solver(1, 2, lambda n: 1)))        # ~0.02  (log n)

    The fitted exponent matches log_b a in the leaf-heavy cases exactly; in balanced cases it comes out slightly above the polynomial exponent, because a log n factor looks like a small positive exponent over a bounded range — the same limitation as Chapter 8's empirical fit, and a good reminder that the theorem tells you what measurement cannot.

  10. Program

    Implement Strassen's matrix multiplication and measure the crossover against the naive O(n³) algorithm.

    Solution
    def strassen(A, B, cutoff=64):
    n = len(A)
    if n <= cutoff: return naive(A, B)
    m = n // 2
    a11,a12,a21,a22 = split(A, m);  b11,b12,b21,b22 = split(B, m)
    M1 = strassen(add(a11,a22), add(b11,b22), cutoff)
    M2 = strassen(add(a21,a22), b11,          cutoff)
    M3 = strassen(a11,          sub(b12,b22), cutoff)
    M4 = strassen(a22,          sub(b21,b11), cutoff)
    M5 = strassen(add(a11,a12), b22,          cutoff)
    M6 = strassen(sub(a21,a11), add(b11,b12), cutoff)
    M7 = strassen(sub(a12,a22), add(b21,b22), cutoff)
    c11 = add(sub(add(M1,M4),M5), M7)
    c12 = add(M3, M5)
    c21 = add(M2, M4)
    c22 = add(sub(add(M1,M3),M2), M6)
    return join(c11,c12,c21,c22)

    7 multiplications instead of 8, at the cost of 18 additions instead of 4 — so T(n) = 7T(n/2) + Θ(n²) gives Θ(n^{2.807}). In practice the crossover is around n = 512–1024 for a careful implementation, because the naive version's inner loop is dense, contiguous and vectorisable while Strassen's additions destroy locality and allocate. It is also numerically less stable for floating point — the subtractions cancel — which is why BLAS libraries do not use it by default.

  11. Program

    Implement quickselect and median-of-medians selection. Measure the constant factor difference and explain when the O(n) worst-case guarantee is worth paying for.

    Solution
    def quickselect(a, k):                       # expected O(n), worst O(n^2)
    import random
    while True:
        if len(a) == 1: return a[0]
        p = random.choice(a)
        lo = [x for x in a if x < p]
        eq = [x for x in a if x == p]
        hi = [x for x in a if x > p]
        if k < len(lo): a = lo
        elif k < len(lo)+len(eq): return p
        else: k -= len(lo)+len(eq); a = hi

    Expected comparisons for quickselect: ≈ 3.4n. Median-of-medians: ≈ 24n in a straightforward implementation — a 5–10× constant, which is why introselect (quickselect, falling back to median-of-medians only after too many bad pivots) is what std::nth_element actually does.

    Pay for the guarantee when the input is adversarial (a service exposed to untrusted data, where a chosen input triggering Θ(n²) is a DoS) or when a hard latency bound matters. Otherwise randomisation gives the same expectation with a far better constant, and the worst case has probability n^{-Θ(1)}.

  12. Program

    Implement the closest-pair-of-points divide and conquer in O(n log n) and state precisely why the strip check is O(1) per point.

    Solution
    def closest(pts):
    P = sorted(pts)                              # by x
    def rec(P):
        n = len(P)
        if n <= 3: return brute(P), sorted(P, key=lambda p: p[1])
        m = n // 2; mid_x = P[m][0]
        dl, Ly = rec(P[:m]); dr, Ry = rec(P[m:])
        d = min(dl, dr)
        Y = merge_by_y(Ly, Ry)                   # keeps the whole thing O(n log n)
        strip = [p for p in Y if abs(p[0]-mid_x) < d]
        for i in range(len(strip)):
            for j in range(i+1, min(i+8, len(strip))):   # at most 7 ahead
                d = min(d, dist(strip[i], strip[j]))
        return d, Y
    return rec(P)[0]

    Why 7 suffices: consider the d × 2d rectangle above a strip point. Each half of it (left and right of the dividing line) is d × d and contains points pairwise at distance ≥ d, so at most 4 points fit in each — 8 total including the point itself, hence at most 7 others to check. That geometric packing bound is what makes the merge step linear and the whole recurrence T(n) = 2T(n/2)+O(n) = Θ(n log n).

    Sorting by y inside the recursion instead of merging gives T(n) = 2T(n/2)+O(n log n) = Θ(n log²n) — the Case 2 with k = 1 row of the chapter's table, and a good illustration that a log in f(n) costs you a log overall.

  13. Program

    Write a tool that draws the recursion tree of a given recurrence: level, node count, per-node cost, level total. Use it to identify visually which master case applies.

    Solution
    def tree(a, b, f, n):
    print(f"{'lvl':>4} {'nodes':>12} {'size':>12} {'per-node':>14} {'total':>14}")
    lvl, nodes, size, grand = 0, 1, n, 0
    while size >= 1:
        per = f(size); tot = nodes * per; grand += tot
        print(f"{lvl:>4} {nodes:>12} {size:>12} {per:>14.1f} {tot:>14.1f}")
        lvl += 1; nodes *= a; size //= b
    print("leaves:", nodes, " grand total:", grand)

tree(2, 2, lambda s: s,     1<<10)   # flat column  → balanced → n log n
tree(2, 2, lambda s: s*s,   1<<10)   # decreasing   → root-heavy → f(n)
tree(4, 2, lambda s: s,     1<<10)   # increasing   → leaf-heavy → n^{log_b a}

    The "total" column is the whole diagnosis: constant means multiply by depth, geometrically decreasing means take the first row, geometrically increasing means take the last. Printing it for an unfamiliar recurrence is faster and more reliable than trying to recall which master case has which inequality direction.

  14. Program

    Given T(n) = T(n-1) + T(n-2)-style tree recursion (naive Fibonacci), count the exact number of calls, then fix it with memoisation and with an iterative loop. Quantify all three.

    Solution
    calls = 0
def fib_naive(n):
    global calls; calls += 1
    return n if n < 2 else fib_naive(n-1) + fib_naive(n-2)

    Exact call count: C(n) = 2Fₙ₊₁ - 1. So computing F₄₀ makes 331,160,281 calls — about Θ(φⁿ) with φ = 1.618. Note the base of the exponential is φ, not 2, because the tree is lopsided; this is the general solution of T(n)=T(n-1)+T(n-2)+O(1).

    Memoisation makes it Θ(n) calls and Θ(n) space; the iterative two-variable loop is Θ(n) time and Θ(1) space; matrix exponentiation (Chapter 48) is Θ(log n). Four algorithms for one function, differing by φⁿ → n → log n — the clearest demonstration that a recurrence's shape, not its formula, determines cost.

  15. Program

    Implement an iterative-deepening search whose cost is ∑d=0..D bᵈ and demonstrate that the repeated work is a constant factor, not a blow-up.

    Solution
    def ids(start, goal, succ, max_depth):
    for depth in range(max_depth + 1):
        found = dls(start, goal, succ, depth)     # depth-limited DFS
        if found is not None: return found, depth
    return None

    Cost: ∑d=0..D bᵈ = (b^{D+1}-1)/(b-1), versus bᵈ for a single depth-D search. The ratio is b/(b-1): 2× for b = 2, 1.5× for b = 3, 1.1× for b = 11. A geometric series dominated by its largest term, exactly the Chapter 9 principle.

    So iterative deepening gets BFS's optimality and DFS's O(D) memory for a constant-factor time penalty that shrinks as the branching factor grows. That is why it is the standard choice for game-tree search, where b is large and memory is the binding constraint — and it is a case where an analysis that looks wasteful is refuted by summing the series properly.

Chapter 11

Logarithms, harmonic numbers and Stirling's approximation

Three tools for turning products into sums, estimating factorials, and recognising the log that appears whenever something halves or when you sum reciprocals. They show up in nearly every analysis, and estimating them mentally to one significant figure is a genuinely useful skill.

Logarithms convert multiplication into addition, and that is why they appear wherever a quantity halves, doubles, or is measured in bits. The identities:

log(xy) = log x + log y log(x/y) = log x − log y log(xᵏ) = k log x log_b x = ln x / ln b a^{log_b c} = c^{log_b a} x = b^{log_b x} log₂ 10 ≈ 3.322 ln 2 ≈ 0.693 log₂ e ≈ 1.443 log₂ n for n = 10⁶ ≈ 20 10⁹ ≈ 30 10¹² ≈ 40 10¹⁸ ≈ 60

The last row is worth memorising outright: every factor of 1000 adds about 10 to log₂. That single fact lets you estimate any logarithmic cost in your head — a balanced tree over a billion keys is 30 levels deep, a binary search over 10¹⁸ takes 60 steps.

Inside Θ, the base of a logarithm is irrelevant (log_a n = log_b n / log_b a, a constant factor). Inside an exponent it is not: 2^{log₂n} = n but 2^{log₃n} = n^{0.63}. Confusing these is a common error, and the fix is always to rewrite a^{log_b c} as c^{log_b a} where the exponent is a plain constant.

Harmonic numbers

H_n = 1 + 1/2 + 1/3 + ... + 1/n = ln n + γ + 1/(2n) − 1/(12n²) + O(n⁻⁴) γ ≈ 0.5772156649 Euler–Mascheroni constant H_n grows without bound, but slowly: H_{10⁶} ≈ 14.39, H_{10⁹} ≈ 21.3

The harmonic series diverges — famously proved by the blocking argument of Chapter 9 — but so slowly that it is effectively a logarithm for every practical purpose. It appears in:

  • Coupon collector: expected draws to see all n coupons is nHₙ ≈ n ln n.
  • Quicksort: expected comparisons ≈ 2n ln n ≈ 1.39 n log₂n — the constant 2 ln 2 comes straight from Hₙ.
  • Sieve loops: ∑ n/i = nHₙ.
  • Random BST depth: expected depth ≈ 2 ln n ≈ 1.39 log₂n, only 39% worse than a perfectly balanced tree.
  • Records in a random sequence: expected number of new maxima is exactly Hₙ.
Principle

Whenever an analysis produces "the i-th item has probability 1/i of something", the answer is Hₙ = Θ(log n) by linearity of expectation. Recognising that pattern — the 1/i term — is faster than deriving the sum, and it covers a surprising number of randomised-algorithm analyses.

Stirling's approximation

n! = √(2πn) · (n/e)ⁿ · (1 + 1/(12n) + 1/(288n²) + ...) log₂(n!) = n log₂ n − n log₂ e + (1/2)log₂(2πn) + O(1/n) = n log₂ n − 1.4427n + O(log n) Relative error of the leading form: 0.8% at n = 10, 0.08% at n = 100.

Stirling is what makes factorials tractable. Two immediate consequences:

The comparison-sort lower bound. A decision tree distinguishing n! permutations needs depth ≥ log₂(n!) = n log₂n - 1.44n + O(log n). So no comparison sort beats n log₂n comparisons, and the -1.44n term says the achievable constant is 1, not something smaller.

The central binomial coefficient. C(2n,n) ≈ 4ⁿ/√(πn). This is why a random walk returns to the origin with probability Θ(1/√n), why the number of balanced bracket sequences (Catalan, Chapter 32) is Θ(4ⁿ/n^{1.5}), and why the binomial distribution's peak has height Θ(1/√n) — the √n in all three is the same √(2πn) from Stirling.

Estimating factorials and binomials without overflow
# log-space: never compute n! directly for large n
import math
def log_fact(n):        return math.lgamma(n + 1)          # exact, no overflow
def log_binom(n, k):    return log_fact(n) - log_fact(k) - log_fact(n-k)
def binom_float(n, k):  return math.exp(log_binom(n, k))

# 100! has 158 digits; 1000! has 2568.  Digit count:
def digits(n): return int(log_fact(n) / math.log(10)) + 1

# Stirling by hand, order of magnitude:
#   log10(n!) ≈ n log10(n/e) + 0.5 log10(2πn)
#   log10(70!) ≈ 70·log10(25.75) + 1.3 ≈ 70·1.411 + 1.3 ≈ 100  → 70! ≈ 10^100
Interview angle

"How many comparisons does sorting need?" expects Θ(n log n); the strong answer adds "at least log₂(n!) ≈ n log₂n - 1.44n by the decision-tree argument, so mergesort's n log₂n is within a lower-order term of optimal — and radix sort beats it only because it is not a comparison sort." Naming why the lower bound does not apply to radix sort is the part that lands.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Without a calculator, estimate log₂(10⁹), log₂(3&sup5;⁰) and the number of decimal digits in 2¹⁰⁰₀.

    Solution
    • log₂10⁹ = 9 log₂10 ≈ 9 × 3.322 ≈ 29.9, so about 30.
    • log₂3⁵⁰ = 50 log₂3 ≈ 50 × 1.585 = 79.2.
    • Digits of 2¹⁰²⁴: 1024 × log₁₀2 = 1024 × 0.30103 ≈ 308.25, so 309 digits. (The famous 2¹⁰²⁴ overflow threshold in double is exactly this.)

    The two constants to keep: log₂10 ≈ 3.32 and log₁₀2 ≈ 0.301. Everything else follows.

  2. Theory

    Prove a^{log_b c} = c^{log_b a} and use it to simplify n^{log₂3} versus 3^{log₂n}.

    Solution

    Take log_b of both sides: LHS gives log_b c · log_b a, RHS gives log_b a · log_b c. Equal, and log_b is injective on positives. ∎

    So 3^{log₂n} = n^{log₂3} = n^{1.585} — they are the same function, which surprises people. This identity is how you recognise Karatsuba's n^{log₂3} and a recursion producing 3^{depth} as identical growth rates.

  3. Theory

    Prove Hₙ = ln n + γ + O(1/n) at least to the level of ln n + O(1), using the integral comparison.

    Solution

    Since 1/x is decreasing, for each i ≥ 2: ∫ᵢ^{i+1} dx/x ≤ 1/i ≤ ∫_{i-1}^{i} dx/x. Summing i = 1..n:

    ln(n+1) = ∫₁^{n+1} dx/x ≤ Hₙ ≤ 1 + ∫₁ⁿ dx/x = 1 + ln n.

    So ln n ≤ Hₙ ≤ 1 + ln n, i.e. Hₙ - ln n ∈ [0,1]. That difference is decreasing and bounded below, hence convergent — its limit is γ ≈ 0.5772. ∎ The O(1/n) refinement comes from the Euler–Maclaurin formula.

  4. Theory

    Compute the expected number of "record" values (new running maxima) in a uniformly random permutation of n distinct elements.

    Solution

    Let Xᵢ = 1 if position i is a record. Position i is a record iff the maximum of the first i elements sits at position i, which by symmetry has probability 1/i. By linearity of expectation (no independence needed):

    E[records] = ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ 1/i = Hₙ ≈ ln n + γ.

    For n = 10⁶ that is about 14.4 records — strikingly few. This is exactly the expected number of times a "best so far" variable is updated in a linear scan, and the same computation gives the expected number of times a randomised incremental algorithm rebuilds.

  5. Theory

    Use Stirling to show C(2n,n) = Θ(4ⁿ/√n) and give the constant.

    Solution

    C(2n,n) = (2n)!/(n!)². Substituting Stirling:

    ≈ [√(4πn)(2n/e)^{2n}] / [√(2πn)(n/e)ⁿ]² = [√(4πn) · 4ⁿ n^{2n}e^{-2n}] / [2πn · n^{2n}e^{-2n}] = 4ⁿ/√(πn).

    So the constant is 1/√π ≈ 0.564. ∎ Check at n = 10: C(20,10) = 184{,}756 and 4¹⁰/√(10π) = 1048576/5.60 = 187{,}200 — 1.3% high, as expected from the 1+1/(12n) correction terms.

  6. Theory

    Show that a comparison sort needs at least n log₂n - 1.443n comparisons in the worst case.

    Solution

    A deterministic comparison sort is a binary decision tree: each internal node is a comparison with two outcomes, each leaf is an output permutation. To sort correctly it must have at least n! distinct leaves. A binary tree with L leaves has depth ≥ log₂L, and depth is the worst-case comparison count. So the worst case is at least

    log₂(n!) = n log₂n - n log₂e + Θ(log n) = n log₂n - 1.4427n + Θ(log n). ∎

    The bound is essentially achieved: mergesort uses ≤ n log₂n - n + 1 comparisons, within 0.44n of optimal. Counting sort and radix sort evade the bound entirely because they read the keys' values, not just comparison outcomes — the decision tree has more than two children per node.

  7. Theory

    Expected comparisons in randomised quicksort is 2(n+1)Hₙ - 4n. Derive the leading term and express the constant relative to n log₂n.

    Solution

    Derivation. Consider elements of rank i and j, i < j. They are compared iff the first pivot chosen from the range [i..j] is one of them (otherwise they are separated). That probability is 2/(j-i+1). By linearity,

    E = ∑ᵢ₂₃ 2/(j-i+1) = ∑k=2..n (n-k+1)·2/k = 2(n+1)Hₙ - 4n.

    Leading term: 2n ln n = 2 ln 2 · n log₂n ≈ 1.386 n log₂n. So randomised quicksort uses about 39% more comparisons than the information-theoretic minimum — and still beats mergesort in practice because its inner loop is a tight in-place partition with excellent locality and no auxiliary array.

  8. Program

    Implement Hₙ three ways for n = 10⁷: forward summation, backward summation, and the asymptotic formula. Compare accuracy against an exact rational computation.

    Solution
    import math
from fractions import Fraction

def fwd(n):  return sum(1.0/i for i in range(1, n+1))
def bwd(n):  return sum(1.0/i for i in range(n, 0, -1))
def asym(n):
    g = 0.5772156649015329
    return math.log(n) + g + 1/(2*n) - 1/(12*n*n) + 1/(120*n**4)
def exact(n): return float(sum(Fraction(1, i) for i in range(1, n+1)))

    Backward summation is more accurate. Adding smallest-to-largest keeps the running sum comparable in magnitude to each addend, so fewer low-order bits are lost; forward summation loses precision once the partial sum dwarfs 1/i. At n = 10⁷ forward is off by roughly 10⁻⁹ relative, backward by about 10⁻¹², and the asymptotic formula is accurate to 10⁻¹⁶ — better than either sum, and 10⁷ times faster. This is the Chapter 66 lesson arriving early: the numerically best algorithm here is not to sum at all.

  9. Program

    Compute the number of trailing zeros in n! for n ≤ 10¹⁸, and the number of decimal digits.

    Solution
    def trailing_zeros(n):        # Legendre's formula for the exponent of 5
    c = 0
    while n: n //= 5; c += n
    return c

def digits(n):                # via Stirling in log10
    import math
    if n < 2: return 1
    return int(n*math.log10(n/math.e) + 0.5*math.log10(2*math.pi*n)) + 1

    Trailing zeros count factors of 10 = 2·5, and 5s are scarcer, so the answer is ∑i≥1 ⌊n/5ᵢ⌋ — Legendre's formula, O(log₅n). For n = 10¹⁸ that is about 2.5·10¹⁷ zeros.

    The digit count uses Stirling in base 10. Beware precision: at n = 10¹⁸ the product n log₁₀(n/e) is about 1.7·10¹⁹, and a double has only ~15–16 significant digits, so the answer's last digits are unreliable. Use exact integer arithmetic on a scaled logarithm, or accept the approximation and say so.

  10. Program

    Simulate the coupon collector for n = 1000 over 10,000 trials and compare the empirical mean and variance to nHₙ and n²π²/6.

    Solution
    import random, math
def trial(n):
    seen, draws = set(), 0
    while len(seen) < n:
        seen.add(random.randrange(n)); draws += 1
    return draws

n, T = 1000, 10000
xs = [trial(n) for _ in range(T)]
mean = sum(xs)/T
var  = sum((x-mean)**2 for x in xs)/T
H = sum(1.0/i for i in range(1, n+1))
print(mean, n*H)                       # ≈ 7485 both
print(var,  n*n*math.pi**2/6)          # ≈ 1.64e6 both

    Why: the wait to go from i-1 to i distinct coupons is geometric with success probability (n-i+1)/n, so mean n/(n-i+1) and the total is n Hₙ by linearity. Variances add because the waits are independent, giving ∑ n²(1-p)/p² → n²π²/6 using ∑1/k² = π²/6.

    The standard deviation is nπ/√6 ≈ 1283 — 17% of the mean, so individual runs vary a lot. That spread is why cache-warming and randomised-sampling estimates need many trials, and it is directly the π²/6 from Chapter 9's table.

  11. Program

    Verify empirically that a random BST built from n random insertions has expected depth ≈ 4.31 ln n and average node depth ≈ 2 ln n.

    Solution
    import random, math
def build_depths(n):
    root = None; depth = {}
    tree = {}
    for x in random.sample(range(n*10), n):
        d, cur = 0, root
        while cur is not None:
            d += 1
            cur = tree[cur][0] if x < cur else tree[cur][1]
        ...   # standard BST insert, recording d
    return maxdepth, avgdepth

    Expected results at n = 10⁵: average node depth ≈ 2 ln n ≈ 23, maximum depth ≈ 4.311 ln n ≈ 50. A perfectly balanced tree would have depth log₂n ≈ 17.

    The average-depth result is the quicksort analysis in disguise: the comparisons made inserting all n keys are exactly the comparisons quicksort makes, because the BST's shape is quicksort's recursion tree with the same pivots. The maximum-depth constant 4.311 (the root of c ln(2e/c) = 1) is much harder and comes from a branching-process argument — worth knowing it is not 2 ln n, since the maximum and the average diverge by more than a factor of 2.

  12. Program

    Write a function that computes C(n,k) mod p for n ≤ 10⁶ in O(1) after preprocessing, and separately a floating-point C(n,k) for n ≤ 10⁹ that does not overflow.

    Solution
    # exact mod p, O(n) preprocessing then O(1) per query
MOD = 10**9 + 7; N = 10**6 + 1
f = [1]*N
for i in range(1, N): f[i] = f[i-1]*i % MOD
inv = [1]*N
inv[N-1] = pow(f[N-1], MOD-2, MOD)          # Fermat, Chapter 20
for i in range(N-1, 0, -1): inv[i-1] = inv[i]*i % MOD
def C(n, k): return 0 if k<0 or k>n else f[n]*inv[k]%MOD*inv[n-k]%MOD

# floating point, no overflow, for huge n
import math
def C_float(n, k):
    return math.exp(math.lgamma(n+1) - math.lgamma(k+1) - math.lgamma(n-k+1))

    The batched inverse trick — computing inv[N-1] once with one modular exponentiation and then walking down with inv[i-1] = inv[i]*i — turns O(n log p) into O(n + log p), and is worth knowing verbatim.

    The float version works to n = 10⁹ and beyond because lgamma stays in log space; computing n! directly overflows double at n = 171. Accuracy is about 13 significant digits, adequate for probabilities, useless for exact counting — which is why both versions exist.

  13. Program

    Implement a fast integer ⌊log₂n⌋ and ⌈log₂n⌉ using bit tricks, and verify against a floating-point version for all n ≤ 10⁷. Find where the float version is wrong.

    Solution
    int floor_log2(uint64_t n){ return 63 - __builtin_clzll(n); }      // n > 0
int ceil_log2 (uint64_t n){ return n <= 1 ? 0 : 64 - __builtin_clzll(n-1); }

// the buggy float version:
int bad(uint64_t n){ return (int)(log2((double)n)); }

    The float version fails at exact powers of two for large n: log2(2^49) can evaluate to 48.999999999999993, and truncation gives 48 instead of 49. It also fails for n > 2⁵³ where the double cannot represent n exactly. Testing all n ≤ 10⁷ may show no failures — the bug typically first appears around 2⁴⁵–2⁵⁰, which is precisely why it survives testing and breaks in production.

    Use the bit-count intrinsic (__builtin_clzll, Java's Long.numberOfLeadingZeros, Python's int.bit_length()). It is exact, branchless and faster.

  14. Program

    Given n up to 10⁵, compute ∑ᵢ₋₁ⁿ ⌊log₂i⌋ in O(log n) and explain the connection to the total bits needed to store 1..n.

    Solution

    Exactly 2ᵏ integers in [1,n] have ⌊log₂i⌋ = k (namely 2ᵏ..2^{k+1}-1), truncated at n.

    def sum_floor_log2(n):
    total, k, lo = 0, 0, 1
    while lo <= n:
        hi = min(n, 2*lo - 1)
        total += k * (hi - lo + 1)
        k += 1; lo *= 2
    return total

    O(log n) iterations. The value is ≈ n log₂n - 2n, consistent with log₂(n!) ≈ n log₂n - 1.44n since ⌊log₂i⌋ undercounts log₂i by about 0.5 on average.

    Interpretation: ⌊log₂i⌋+1 is the bit length of i, so this sum plus n is the total bits to store all integers from 1 to n — the size of a variable-length encoding, and the reason a delta-encoded sorted list of n values from a universe of size n compresses to O(n log(U/n)) bits rather than n log U.

Chapter 12

Amortized analysis: aggregate, accounting and potential

Some operations are occasionally expensive but cheap on average over any sequence — and "on average" here means guaranteed, not probabilistic. Three methods formalise it, and the potential method is the one that generalises.

Amortized analysis bounds the total cost of a sequence of operations, then divides. It is not average-case analysis: there is no probability distribution and no randomness. The bound holds for every sequence, including the worst one an adversary can construct. That distinction is the thing to say out loud.

Worst case per opAmortizedAverage case
Guaranteeevery opevery sequenceexpectation over inputs
Adversary-proofyesyesno
Examplebalanced BST insertvector push_backhash table lookup

Aggregate method

Bound the total cost of n operations directly, then divide by n. For a dynamic array that doubles when full, the copies happen at sizes 1, 2, 4, …, 2^{⌊log n⌋}, totalling < 2n. Plus n cheap appends, the total is < 3n, so the amortized cost per push is O(1). That is the entire argument, and it is a geometric series (Chapter 9) doing the work.

Accounting method

Charge each operation an amortized cost that may exceed its actual cost; the surplus is stored as credit on data-structure elements and spent on later expensive operations. The obligation is to prove the credit balance never goes negative.

Dynamic array by accounting
Charge 3 units per push.
   1 unit  pays for writing the new element.
   1 unit  is stored on the new element, to pay for copying it later.
   1 unit  is stored on an element in the first half of the array,
           to pay for copying that (uncredited) element.

When the array of size k doubles: the k/2 elements added since the last
resize each carry 1 credit for themselves and 1 for an older element,
so all k copies are paid for.  Balance never goes negative.
Hence amortized O(1) per push.  ∎

The choice of "3" is not magic — you pick a constant and check the invariant holds. If it does not, increase it; if no constant works, the operation is not O(1) amortized.

Potential method

Define Φ mapping each state to a real number, with Φ(D₀) = 0 and Φ(Dᵢ) ≥ 0 always. The amortized cost of operation i is

ĉᵢ = cᵢ + Φ(Dᵢ) − Φ(Dᵢ₋₁) Σ ĉᵢ = Σ cᵢ + Φ(Dₙ) − Φ(D₀) ≥ Σ cᵢ since Φ(Dₙ) ≥ 0 = Φ(D₀) So the total amortized cost UPPER BOUNDS the total actual cost.

The art is choosing Φ so that expensive operations release stored potential. For the dynamic array, take Φ = 2·(\text{size}) - (\text{capacity}). A cheap push: cost 1, potential rises by 2, so &ĉ = 3. A push triggering a resize from capacity k to 2k with size going k → k+1: cost k+1, and ΔΦ = [2(k+1)-2k] - [2k-k] = 2 - k, so &ĉ = (k+1)+(2-k) = 3. Constant either way. ∎

Principle

The potential function measures how far the structure is from its cheapest configuration — how much "mess" is stored up. Full array, unbalanced tree, long chain of same-rank nodes: all are high potential. If you can name what makes an operation expensive, that quantity is usually the potential.

The standard results

Structure / operationAmortizedPotential used
Dynamic array pushO(1)2·size - capacity
Binary counter incrementO(1)number of 1 bits
Two-stack queueO(1)size of the input stack
Splay tree operationsO(log n)∑ log(\text{subtree size})
Fibonacci heap decrease-keyO(1)\#trees + 2·\#marked
Union-find with both heuristicsO(α(n))rank-based, intricate
Move-to-front list2× optimalinversions vs. optimal order

The binary counter is the cleanest example: incrementing flips a trailing run of 1s to 0 and one 0 to 1. A single increment can cost Θ(log n), but with Φ = \#(\text{1 bits}), an increment that flips k ones costs k+1 actual and changes potential by 1-k, giving &ĉ = 2. Over n increments the total bit flips are < 2n, which you can also see by noting bit i flips every 2ᵢ increments: ∑ n/2ᵢ < 2n.

Where amortization breaks

Amortized bounds are about totals, so they fail when you need per-operation guarantees. A real-time system cannot accept a 100 ms pause even if the average is 1 μs — which is why real-time garbage collectors and deamortized data structures exist, spreading the expensive work across many operations at a constant-factor cost.

They also fail under persistence: if an old version can be reused repeatedly, an adversary can trigger the expensive operation from the same high-potential state many times, and the credit was only ever saved once. Amortized bounds for functional data structures need the stronger machinery of debits and lazy evaluation.

Interview angle

"What is the complexity of ArrayList.add?" — O(1) amortized, O(n) worst case for a single call, and the total for n adds is Θ(n). Adding "so a latency-sensitive path should presize the list" converts a definition question into an engineering answer.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove that n increments of a binary counter starting from 0 cost O(n) total bit flips, two different ways.

    Solution

    Aggregate: bit 0 flips every increment, bit 1 every 2, bit i every 2ᵢ. Total flips = ∑i≥0 ⌊n/2ᵢ⌋ < n ∑ 2^{-i} = 2n.

    Potential: Φ = number of 1 bits, non-negative and 0 initially. An increment flipping k trailing 1s to 0 and one 0 to 1 costs k+1 with ΔΦ = 1-k, so &ĉ = 2. Total amortized 2n ≥ total actual. ∎

    Both give the constant 2, which is tight: the counter 2ᵏ-1 → 2ᵏ really does flip k+1 bits, it just happens rarely.

  2. Theory

    A dynamic array doubles on overflow and halves when it drops below 1/2 full. Show this is not O(1) amortized, and give the standard fix.

    Solution

    Counterexample: fill to exactly capacity k. Then push (doubles to 2k, size k+1), pop (size k, now below half of 2k, halves back to k), push (doubles again)… Each operation costs Θ(k), so n operations cost Θ(nk) — no constant amortized bound. This is thrashing.

    Fix: halve only when the array drops below 1/4 full, shrinking to half capacity. Then immediately after any resize the array is exactly half full, so Ω(k) operations must occur before the next resize in either direction. Formally, Φ = |2·size - capacity| works: it is 0 right after a resize and must reach capacity/2 before another triggers.

    The general lesson: leave hysteresis between the grow and shrink thresholds. The same principle governs autoscaling policies and circuit breakers.

  3. Theory

    Show that a queue implemented with two stacks has O(1) amortized enqueue and dequeue.

    Solution

    Enqueue pushes to stack in. Dequeue pops from out; if out is empty, move everything from in to out first (reversing it), then pop.

    Take Φ = |in|. Enqueue: cost 1, ΔΦ = +1, so &ĉ = 2. Dequeue without a transfer: cost 1, ΔΦ = 0, &ĉ = 1. Dequeue with a transfer of k elements: cost k+1, ΔΦ = -k, so &ĉ = 1. ∎

    The intuition: every element is pushed to in once, moved once, and popped from out once — exactly 3 stack operations over its lifetime, no matter the interleaving.

  4. Theory

    Explain why amortized is not average-case, with a concrete example where the two differ.

    Solution

    Hash table lookup is O(1) average-case and O(n) worst-case, and it has no useful amortized bound: an adversary who knows the hash function can make every key collide, so n lookups cost Θ(n²). The O(1) depends on a probabilistic assumption about the keys (or a randomly chosen hash function).

    Dynamic array push is O(1) amortized with no assumptions whatsoever: for every sequence of n pushes, total cost is < 3n. There is nothing an adversary can do.

    So amortized is the stronger guarantee. The confusion arises because both are stated as "O(1) per operation", and the difference only shows up under adversarial input — which is exactly when it matters, as the hash-flooding DoS attacks demonstrated.

  5. Theory

    Give the potential function for a splay tree and state what it proves.

    Solution

    Let s(x) be the number of nodes in x's subtree and r(x) = log₂s(x) the rank. Then Φ = ∑x r(x).

    The Access Lemma: splaying node x to the root has amortized cost ≤ 3(r(root) - r(x)) + 1 = O(log n). Summing over a sequence of m operations on an n-node tree gives O((m+n)log n) total.

    Because Φ is defined purely by the tree shape and no balance information is stored, splay trees achieve O(log n) amortized with no bookkeeping fields — and additionally satisfy the static optimality theorem (within a constant of the best static BST for any access sequence) and the working-set property. Individual operations can still cost Θ(n), which is why splay trees are unsuitable for real-time systems.

  6. Theory

    Prove that move-to-front is 2-competitive against any static list ordering.

    Solution

    Run MTF alongside a fixed optimal ordering OPT, and let Φ = the number of inversions between the two lists (pairs ordered differently).

    Accessing an element at position k in MTF and k* in OPT: let x be the number of elements preceding it in both lists. Moving it to the front of MTF destroys k-1-x inversions and creates at most x, so ΔΦ ≤ x - (k-1-x) = 2x-k+1. Since those x elements precede the item in OPT too, x ≤ k*-1. Then

    &ĉ = k + ΔΦ ≤ k + 2x - k + 1 = 2x+1 ≤ 2k* - 1. ∎

    Summing: MTF's total cost is at most twice OPT's. This is a competitive analysis result — amortized analysis against an adversary and a reference algorithm — and it is why LRU-style self-organising heuristics are defensible without any assumption about the access distribution.

  7. Theory

    Why does a Fibonacci heap achieve O(1) amortized decrease-key, and what is the practical catch?

    Solution

    Φ = t + 2m where t is the number of trees in the root list and m the number of marked nodes. Decrease-key cuts the node from its parent (making a new tree, t{+}1) and cascades up while parents are marked — but each cascading cut unmarks a node, releasing 2 potential while costing 1, so the cascade pays for itself. Amortized O(1). The deferred consolidation happens in extract-min, which is O(log n) amortized.

    This makes Dijkstra O(E + V log V) instead of O(E log V) — asymptotically better for dense graphs.

    The catch: enormous constants and terrible cache behaviour. Each node needs parent, child, two sibling pointers, degree and a mark bit, and the structure is a pointer soup with no locality. In practice a binary heap or a pairing heap beats it at every realistic size, and Fibonacci heaps are essentially a theoretical result. Saying that is the mark of having actually used them.

  8. Program

    Implement a dynamic array from scratch with an explicit copy counter. Empirically verify the amortized cost is under 3 for growth factor 2, and measure it for factors 1.5 and 4.

    Solution
    class Vec:
    def __init__(self, g=2.0):
        self.buf = [None]; self.n = 0; self.g = g; self.copies = 0
    def push(self, x):
        if self.n == len(self.buf):
            new = [None] * max(self.n + 1, int(self.n * self.g))
            for i in range(self.n): new[i] = self.buf[i]
            self.copies += self.n
            self.buf = new
        self.buf[self.n] = x; self.n += 1

for g in (1.5, 2.0, 4.0):
    v = Vec(g)
    for i in range(10**6): v.push(i)
    print(g, v.copies / 10**6, "predicted", 1/(g-1))

    Measured copies-per-push converge to 1/(g-1): 2.0 for g = 1.5, 1.0 for g = 2, 0.33 for g = 4. Total cost per push is that plus 1 for the write itself, so the "3" of the accounting argument is a comfortable over-estimate for g = 2 (the true figure is 2).

    The trade-off is memory: worst-case waste is (g-1)/g of the buffer — 33% for g = 1.5, 50% for g = 2, 75% for g = 4. That is why growth factors above 2 are essentially never used.

  9. Program

    Implement a queue from two stacks and a stack that supports getMin() in O(1). Then combine them into a queue with O(1) amortized getMin().

    Solution
    class MinStack:                      # each entry carries the min below it
    def __init__(self): self.s = []
    def push(self, x):
        m = x if not self.s else min(x, self.s[-1][1])
        self.s.append((x, m))
    def pop(self):  return self.s.pop()[0]
    def getmin(self): return self.s[-1][1]

class MinQueue:                      # two min-stacks
    def __init__(self): self.i, self.o = MinStack(), MinStack()
    def push(self, x): self.i.push(x)
    def pop(self):
        if not self.o.s:
            while self.i.s: self.o.push(self.i.pop())
            # note: reversal here is what makes the transferred order correct
        return self.o.pop()
    def getmin(self):
        if not self.i.s: return self.o.getmin()
        if not self.o.s: return self.i.getmin()
        return min(self.i.getmin(), self.o.getmin())

    Each element is pushed twice and popped twice over its lifetime, so all three operations are O(1) amortized; getmin is O(1) worst case. This structure solves the sliding-window-minimum problem in O(n), and is often easier to get right than the monotonic-deque version because there is no window bookkeeping.

  10. Program

    Implement a hash table with open addressing and resizing at load factor 0.75. Instrument it to record probe counts and demonstrate the amortized O(1) claim, then break it with adversarial keys.

    Solution
    class Table:
    def __init__(self): self.k = [None]*8; self.n = 0; self.probes = 0
    def _idx(self, key):
        i = hash(key) & (len(self.k)-1)
        while self.k[i] is not None and self.k[i][0] != key:
            self.probes += 1; i = (i+1) & (len(self.k)-1)     # linear probing
        return i
    def put(self, key, val):
        if (self.n+1) / len(self.k) > 0.75: self._grow()
        i = self._idx(key)
        if self.k[i] is None: self.n += 1
        self.k[i] = (key, val)

    With random keys, the average probe count stabilises near \frac{1}{2}(1 + 1/(1-α)²) ≈ 8.5 at α = 0.75 for insertions — bounded, so amortized O(1) including rehashes (whose cost is a geometric series, exactly as for the dynamic array).

    Breaking it: choose keys all hashing to the same bucket (easy for a known, unseeded hash — hash(x) & 7 collides for many small integers). Then insertion becomes Θ(n) each and the total is Θ(n²). The counted probes make the attack visible. The mitigations — a randomly seeded hash per process, or switching a long bucket to a tree as Java's HashMap does at 8 entries — are worth naming.

  11. Program

    Implement union-find without path compression and without union by rank, and measure the degradation. Then add each heuristic separately.

    Solution
    def bench(n, use_rank, use_compress):
    p = list(range(n)); r = [0]*n; steps = 0
    def find(x):
        nonlocal steps
        root = x
        while p[root] != root: root = p[root]; steps += 1
        if use_compress:
            while p[x] != root: p[x], x = root, p[x]
        return root
    ...

    Typical asymptotics, all for m operations on n elements:

    • Neither heuristic: O(mn) worst case — a path can degenerate to a chain of length n.
    • Union by rank/size only: O(m log n) — trees have depth ≤ log₂n because a tree of height h has ≥ 2Ḥ nodes.
    • Path compression only: O(m log n) amortized (in fact O(m log_{1+m/n} n)).
    • Both: O(m α(n)) — inverse Ackermann, under 5 for any n that fits in the universe.

    The measured path lengths make the difference concrete: at n = 10⁶ the naive version averages thousands of steps per find, both-heuristics averages under 2.

  12. Program

    Write a "deamortized" dynamic array: instead of one big copy, migrate a few elements per operation so every push is O(1) worst case. State the cost.

    Solution
    class Deamortized:
    def __init__(self):
        self.cur = [None]*4; self.n = 0
        self.old = None; self.moved = 0
    def push(self, x):
        if self.old is None and self.n == len(self.cur):
            self.old, self.cur, self.moved = self.cur, [None]*(2*self.n), 0
        if self.old is not None:                # migrate a fixed quota
            for _ in range(3):
                if self.moved < len(self.old):
                    self.cur[self.moved] = self.old[self.moved]; self.moved += 1
            if self.moved == len(self.old): self.old = None
        self.cur[self.n] = x; self.n += 1

    Migrating 3 elements per push finishes the k-element copy within k/3 pushes, well before the new buffer of size 2k fills — so every push is O(1) worst case, not merely amortized. The costs are 2× memory during migration and a lookup that must check both buffers.

    This is exactly what an incremental or concurrent garbage collector does, and what Redis does when rehashing (it keeps two tables and migrates a bucket per operation). Deamortization trades a constant factor and memory for the removal of latency spikes — the right trade whenever a p99 matters more than a mean.

  13. Program

    Measure latency percentiles for ArrayList-style pushes and show the amortized mean hides a heavy tail. Produce the p50, p99, p99.9 and max.

    Solution
    import time
lat = []
v = Vec(2.0)
for i in range(10**6):
    t = time.perf_counter_ns(); v.push(i); lat.append(time.perf_counter_ns()-t)
lat.sort()
def pct(p): return lat[int(len(lat)*p)]
print("p50", pct(.5), "p99", pct(.99), "p99.9", pct(.999), "max", lat[-1])

    Typical shape: p50 and p99 are both a few hundred nanoseconds, p99.9 is similar, and the max is 3–5 orders of magnitude larger — the final resize copying 10⁶ elements. Only about 20 operations out of a million are resizes, so they do not even reach p99.99.

    The lesson for a service: an amortized bound says nothing about tail latency, and tail latency is what users experience. If a request can trigger the resize, one request in 10⁵ takes 10 ms. Presizing the collection, or deamortizing as in the previous problem, is the fix — and this measurement is how you justify it.

  14. Program

    Implement a monotonic stack solving "next greater element" and prove its O(n) total cost by an amortized argument, then verify with an operation counter.

    Solution
    def next_greater(a):
    n = len(a); res = [-1]*n; st = []; ops = 0
    for i in range(n):
        while st and a[st[-1]] < a[i]:
            res[st.pop()] = i; ops += 1
        st.append(i); ops += 1
    return res, ops

    Amortized argument: take Φ = |st|. Each iteration pushes exactly once (ΔΦ = +1, cost 1, &ĉ = 2) and pops k times (cost k, ΔΦ = -k, &ĉ = 0). Total amortized 2n, so total actual ≤ 2n = O(n). ∎ Equivalently: each index is pushed once and popped at most once.

    The counter confirms ops < 2n for every input, including the worst-looking ones (a strictly decreasing array pushes n times and pops 0; a strictly increasing one pushes n and pops n-1). The inner while loop makes the code look quadratic, and this argument is precisely what tells you it is not — the single most common place amortized reasoning is needed in day-to-day algorithm work.

Chapter 13

Lower bounds: decision trees, adversaries and information

Proving that no algorithm can do better is a different discipline from designing one. Three techniques — counting outputs, counting information, and playing adversary — cover almost every lower bound you will meet.

An upper bound is exhibited by an algorithm. A lower bound must quantify over all algorithms in a model, which is why it is harder and why the model must be stated precisely. "Sorting is Ω(n log n)" is false as stated — it is true for comparison-based sorting and false for radix sort. Every lower bound is a statement about a model.

Decision trees: counting outputs

Model the algorithm as a tree whose internal nodes are the queries it may make and whose leaves are the possible outputs. If there are L distinct outputs and each query has k outcomes, the tree has depth at least log_k L, and depth is the worst-case query count.

Sorting n elements L = n! k = 2 ⟹ ≥ log₂(n!) = Ω(n log n) Searching a sorted arr L = n+1 k = 3 ⟹ ≥ log₃(n+1) (with 2-way compare) L = n+1 k = 2 ⟹ ≥ log₂(n+1) Finding the minimum L = n k = 2 ⟹ ≥ log₂ n — too weak!

That last line is instructive: the counting bound gives log n, but the true answer is n-1 comparisons. Counting outputs is often too weak, and then you need an adversary.

Adversary arguments

Imagine an opponent who does not fix the input in advance but answers each query in whichever way keeps the most possibilities alive, subject only to remaining consistent with some valid input. If the adversary can survive T queries, no algorithm can finish in fewer.

Finding the minimum needs n-1 comparisons. Call an element "known non-minimal" once it has lost a comparison. Initially 0 elements are known non-minimal; the algorithm must finish with n-1 of them. Each comparison creates at most one new loser (comparing two already-known losers creates none). So at least n-1 comparisons are needed. ∎

Finding both min and max needs ⌈3n/2⌉ - 2. The adversary tracks each element's status: never compared (N), only won (W), only lost (L), both (B). A comparison of two N elements yields the most information — it converts two N's into a W and an L, and there are only ⌊n/2⌋ such comparisons available. Every other comparison advances the count by at most 1 toward the required total. Counting units of progress gives the bound, and it is achieved by pairing elements first.

Principle

The adversary is not choosing a hard input; it is refusing to commit. Any answer it gives must be consistent with at least one valid input, and the argument works because it maintains an invariant on the number of surviving possibilities. If you can define a progress measure that any single query can advance by at most c, you get a lower bound of (total progress needed)/c.

Information-theoretic bounds

An algorithm learning an answer from a set of N possibilities must extract log₂N bits. Each yes/no query supplies at most 1 bit; each comparison with three outcomes at most log₂3. This is the same argument as the decision tree, phrased in bits, and it generalises cleanly to randomised algorithms via Yao's principle: the expected cost of the best randomised algorithm on the worst input is at least the expected cost of the best deterministic algorithm on the worst input distribution. That converts a hard quantification over randomised algorithms into an easier one over distributions.

Classic lower bounds and their models
Ω(n log n)   comparison sorting            decision tree, n! outputs
Ω(n log n)   element uniqueness            algebraic decision tree
Ω(n)         any problem reading all input trivial, but state it
Ω(log n)     search in a sorted array      decision tree, n+1 outputs
n−1          find the minimum              adversary
⌈3n/2⌉−2     find min and max              adversary
n + ⌈log n⌉−2  find the second largest     adversary (tournament)
Ω(n²)        matrix transpose, I/O bound   external memory model
Ω(V + E)     any graph traversal           must touch each edge
Ω(2^{n/2})   subset sum (best known)       no proof — this is a conjecture

The last line matters: for most interesting problems we have no super-linear lower bound. Nobody has proved that SAT requires more than linear time; P vs NP is exactly the statement that we cannot prove such things. Lower bounds are rare and precious, and the ones above are essentially the complete list you will use.

Reductions: borrowing a lower bound

If problem A reduces to problem B in O(f) time, then a lower bound of L on A gives a lower bound of L - O(f) on B. Element uniqueness is Ω(n log n), and it reduces in linear time to closest pair, to convex hull, and to computing the mode — so all three are Ω(n log n).

Interview angle

"Can you do better than O(n log n)?" is often a lower-bound question in disguise. The strong answer is: "Not with comparisons — the decision-tree bound is log₂(n!). But if the keys are integers in a bounded range, radix sort is O(n) because it reads the key bits directly and escapes the model." Naming the model and the escape hatch is the answer they want.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove that any comparison-based algorithm to merge two sorted lists of length n each requires at least 2n-1 comparisons.

    Solution

    Adversary: interleave the two lists so the merged order alternates a₁ < b₁ < a₂ < b₂ < …. Every adjacent pair in the merged output comes from different lists, and the algorithm must determine the relative order of each of the 2n-1 adjacent pairs. If it skipped comparing some adjacent pair (x,y) directly, the adversary could swap them: no other comparison's outcome changes (they are adjacent, so nothing lies between), yet the correct output differs. So all 2n-1 adjacent pairs must be compared. ∎

    The standard merge achieves exactly 2n-1 in the worst case, so the bound is tight — and it explains why merging cannot be sped up asymptotically, only constant-factor-wise.

  2. Theory

    Prove that finding the second-largest element requires n + ⌈log₂n⌉ - 2 comparisons.

    Solution

    Upper bound: run a tournament. Finding the maximum takes n-1 comparisons. The second largest must have lost directly to the maximum, and the maximum played ⌈log₂n⌉ matches; the second largest is the best of those ⌈log₂n⌉ losers, found in ⌈log₂n⌉-1 more comparisons. Total n + ⌈log₂n⌉ - 2.

    Lower bound (adversary): the adversary assigns each element a weight, starting at 1, and answers a comparison in favour of the heavier element (ties broken arbitrarily), then transfers the loser's weight to the winner. Weights only ever double at most, so after k comparisons the maximum's weight is at most 2ᵏ; to reach total weight n it needs k ≥ log₂n comparisons, so the maximum must win at least ⌈log₂n⌉ times. Every other element must lose at least once (n-1 comparisons), and the second largest must additionally be compared against the other ⌈log₂n⌉-1 losers-to-max. Combining gives the bound. ∎

  3. Theory

    Show that any algorithm determining whether an n-element array contains a duplicate needs Ω(n log n) in the comparison model, and explain how hashing evades it.

    Solution

    Element uniqueness is Ω(n log n) in the algebraic decision tree model (Ben-Or's theorem), via a topological argument: the set of inputs with all distinct values has n! connected components, and a decision tree of depth d can distinguish at most 2^{O(d)} components — so d = Ω(log(n!)) = Ω(n log n).

    Hashing evades it by leaving the model: it computes h(x) mod m, an operation not available to a comparison or algebraic decision tree, and it uses randomness. The result is O(n) expected — but with no worst-case guarantee, since an adversary who knows h can force collisions. There is no contradiction: the two algorithms live in different models with different guarantees, which is the point of always naming the model.

  4. Theory

    What is the minimum number of comparisons to sort 5 elements? Justify both the lower and upper bound.

    Solution

    Lower: ⌈log₂(5!)⌉ = ⌈log₂120⌉ = ⌈6.907⌉ = 7.

    Upper: 7 is achievable. Compare a{:}b and c{:}d (2), compare the winners (3) — say a < b, c < d, b < d, giving the chain a < b < d and c < d. Insert e into a < b < d by binary search (2 more, total 5), then insert c into the appropriate prefix, which has at most 3 candidates positions, by binary search (2 more, total 7). ∎

    Note that the information-theoretic bound is achieved here, but it is not always: for n = 12 the bound gives 29 and the true minimum is 30. Merge-insertion (Ford–Johnson) is optimal for n ≤ 15, and the general problem of the exact minimum is open — a reminder that log₂(n!) is a bound, not a formula.

  5. Theory

    State Yao's minimax principle and use it to sketch a randomised lower bound.

    Solution

    Yao's principle: for any distribution D over inputs, the expected cost of the best deterministic algorithm on D lower-bounds the worst-case expected cost of any randomised algorithm. It follows from the minimax theorem applied to the zero-sum game between algorithm designer and input chooser.

    Sketch application — searching an unsorted array of n bits for a 1, promised exactly one exists. Take D = uniform over the n positions. Any deterministic algorithm probes positions in some fixed order and finds the 1 after (n+1)/2 probes in expectation. Hence every randomised algorithm has worst-case expected cost ≥ (n+1)/2 = Ω(n). ∎

    The power of the principle is that it removes the quantifier over randomised algorithms — the hardest part — leaving only the choice of a hard distribution, which is a design task you can reason about.

  6. Theory

    Prove that any data structure supporting insert and find-min must have insert + delete-min = Ω(log n) amortized, by reduction from sorting.

    Solution

    Given such a structure with insert cost I and delete-min cost D, sort n elements by inserting all and extracting all: total n(I+D). Since comparison sorting is Ω(n log n), we need n(I+D) = Ω(n log n), i.e. I + D = Ω(log n). ∎

    This is why no structure achieves O(1) for both — and it explains the design space precisely: a binary heap splits the cost (O(log n), O(log n)), a Fibonacci heap moves it all to one side (O(1), O(log n)), and an unsorted array does (O(1), O(n)). The sum is the invariant. It also shows the bound does not apply to integer keys: van Emde Boas trees achieve O(log log U) for both, again by leaving the comparison model.

  7. Theory

    Give a lower bound for the number of I/Os needed to sort N elements in the external-memory model with block size B and memory M.

    Solution

    The bound is Θ((N/B) log_{M/B}(N/B)) I/Os, achieved by (M/B)-way mergesort.

    Intuition for the lower bound: reading one block of B elements into memory reveals at most log₂C(M,B) ≈ B log₂(M/B) bits of new ordering information (the interleaving of the block with what is already in memory). Sorting requires log₂(N!) ≈ N log₂N bits, so the number of I/Os is at least N log N / (B log(M/B)) = (N/B) log_{M/B} N.

    Concretely with N = 10¹², B = 10⁶ (1 MB blocks), M = 10⁹ (1 GB): log_{1000}(10⁶) = 2, so two passes. This is why real external sorts are 2–3 passes and why the number is so insensitive to N — the base of the logarithm is huge.

  8. Program

    Write a program that computes the exact minimum number of comparisons to sort n elements for n ≤ 5 by exhaustive search over decision trees, and compare to ⌈log₂(n!)⌉.

    Solution
    from itertools import permutations
from functools import lru_cache

def solve(n):
    perms = frozenset(permutations(range(n)))
    @lru_cache(None)
    def best(S):                       # min depth to distinguish the set S
        if len(S) <= 1: return 0
        r = float('inf')
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i+1, n):
                A = frozenset(p for p in S if p.index(i) < p.index(j))
                B = S - A
                if not A or not B: continue        # useless comparison
                r = min(r, 1 + max(best(A), best(B)))
        return r
    return best(perms)

for n in range(1, 6):
    import math
    print(n, solve(n), math.ceil(math.log2(math.factorial(n))))

    Results: n = 1,2,3,4,5 give 0, 1, 3, 5, 7 — matching ⌈log₂(n!)⌉ = 0, 1, 3, 5, 7 exactly. The first divergence is at n = 12 (30 vs 29), far beyond this search's reach, since the state space is the power set of n! permutations.

  9. Program

    Implement an adversary for the "guess the number" game that answers higher/lower to maximise the number of remaining candidates, and verify that it forces ⌈log₂n⌉ guesses.

    Solution
    class Adversary:
    def __init__(self, n): self.lo, self.hi = 1, n     # never commits to a value
    def ask(self, g):
        left  = g - self.lo                            # candidates below g
        right = self.hi - g                            # candidates above g
        if g < self.lo or g > self.hi: return "wrong"
        if left >= right and left > 0:
            self.hi = g - 1; return "lower"
        if right > 0:
            self.lo = g + 1; return "higher"
        return "correct"                               # forced: only one left

    The adversary always keeps the larger side, so the candidate count drops by at most half per guess: from n it takes at least ⌈log₂n⌉ guesses to reach 1. Binary search achieves exactly that, so the bound is tight.

    This is the cleanest demonstration of the technique: the adversary never picks a number, and yet every answer it has given is consistent with at least one number — so the player genuinely cannot have finished earlier.

  10. Program

    Implement min-and-max in ⌈3n/2⌉-2 comparisons and verify the count empirically against the naive 2n-2.

    Solution
    def minmax(a):
    n = len(a); cmps = 0
    if n == 0: return None
    i = 0
    if n % 2: mn = mx = a[0]; i = 1
    else:
        mn, mx = (a[0], a[1]) if a[0] < a[1] else (a[1], a[0]); cmps += 1; i = 2
    while i + 1 < n:
        x, y = (a[i], a[i+1]) if a[i] < a[i+1] else (a[i+1], a[i]); cmps += 1
        if x < mn: mn = x
        cmps += 1
        if y > mx: mx = y
        cmps += 1
        i += 2
    return mn, mx, cmps

    Each pair costs 3 comparisons (one within the pair, one against the running min, one against the running max) and handles 2 elements, giving 3n/2 versus the naive 2 per element. At n = 10⁶ that is 1.5M versus 2M comparisons — a 25% saving, and it exactly meets the adversary lower bound, so no further improvement is possible in this model.

  11. Program

    Demonstrate that radix sort beats the comparison lower bound: sort 10⁷ 32-bit integers with LSD radix sort and with std::sort, and explain why there is no contradiction.

    Solution
    void radix(vector<uint32_t>& a){
    vector<uint32_t> buf(a.size());
    for (int shift = 0; shift < 32; shift += 8){
        size_t cnt[257] = {0};
        for (uint32_t v : a) cnt[((v >> shift) & 255) + 1]++;
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) cnt[i+1] += cnt[i];
        for (uint32_t v : a) buf[cnt[(v >> shift) & 255]++] = v;
        a.swap(buf);
    }
}

    Four passes of counting sort, each O(n): total O(4n) versus O(n log₂n) = O(23n) comparisons for n = 10⁷. Radix typically wins by 2–4× in wall time.

    No contradiction: radix sort never compares two elements. It reads the bits of each key and uses them as array indices, an operation outside the comparison model. Its cost is O(n · w/b) for w-bit keys and b-bit digits, which is linear in n but depends on the key width — for arbitrary-precision keys, w = Ω(log n) and the two bounds coincide.

  12. Program

    Write a brute-force verifier that, for a small problem, enumerates all decision trees up to depth d and reports whether any solves it — i.e. computationally proves a lower bound.

    Solution
    def solvable_within(states, queries, d, answer):
    """states: set of possible inputs; queries: list of functions
       state -> outcome; answer: state -> required output."""
    def rec(S, depth):
        if len({answer(s) for s in S}) <= 1: return True   # decided
        if depth == 0: return False
        for q in queries:
            parts = {}
            for s in S: parts.setdefault(q(s), set()).add(s)
            if len(parts) < 2: continue                    # useless query
            if all(rec(P, depth-1) for P in parts.values()): return True
        return False
    return rec(frozenset(states), d)

# Then:  d* = min d such that solvable_within(..., d) is True
# and    "not solvable_within(..., d*-1)"  IS the lower-bound proof.

    This is a genuine machine-checked lower bound for the finite instance: it exhausts every decision tree of depth d^*-1 and finds none that works. The limitation is combinatorial explosion — the recursion branches over every query at every node — so it is practical only for tiny instances (sorting 5 elements, weighing 12 coins). For those it is decisive, and it is how the exact comparison counts in the literature were originally established.

  13. Program

    The 12-coin problem: one of 12 coins is counterfeit (heavier or lighter). Prove 3 weighings suffice and that 2 do not, and implement the solution.

    Solution

    Lower bound: there are 12 × 2 = 24 possible answers (which coin, and which direction). Each weighing has 3 outcomes, so 3ᵏ ≥ 24 requires k ≥ 3 since 3² = 9 < 24. ∎

    Upper bound: 3 weighings give 3³ = 27 ≥ 24 outcomes, and a non-adaptive assignment achieves it. Label coins with distinct sequences from {L, R, off}³ chosen so that no sequence is the reverse of another and each weighing has 4 coins per side:

    weighing 1:  L: 1,2,3,4     R: 5,6,7,8
weighing 2:  L: 1,2,5,9     R: 3,6,10,11
weighing 3:  L: 2,4,6,9     R: 1,7,8,12
# the triple of outcomes (<, =, >)^3 maps uniquely to (coin, heavy/light)

    Note 3³ = 27 against 24 needs — only 3 slack outcomes, which is why the coin-to-pattern assignment is delicate and why 13 coins (26 answers) is the true maximum for 3 weighings when you may not use a known-good reference coin.

  14. Program

    Empirically estimate the constant in the Ω(n log n) sorting bound: count comparisons for mergesort, quicksort and heapsort on random data, and compare each to log₂(n!).

    Solution
    import math, random
def ratio(sorter, n, trials=20):
    tot = 0
    for _ in range(trials):
        a = [random.random() for _ in range(n)]
        tot += sorter(a)                       # returns comparison count
    return tot/trials / math.log2(math.factorial(n))

n = 100000
print("mergesort", ratio(merge_count, n))      # ≈ 1.02
print("quicksort", ratio(quick_count, n))      # ≈ 1.39
print("heapsort",  ratio(heap_count,  n))      # ≈ 2.0

    Mergesort is within 2% of the information-theoretic minimum — essentially optimal in comparisons. Quicksort uses 39% more (the 2 ln 2 constant of Chapter 11) but wins in wall time on locality. Heapsort uses about twice the minimum and has poor cache behaviour, which is why it is used as introsort's fallback rather than its main algorithm.

    The experiment makes the lower bound tangible: it is not an abstraction but a floor that real algorithms sit within a small constant of, and the differences between them are entirely in that constant.

III. Number Theory
Chapter 14

Divisibility, gcd and the Euclidean algorithm

The gcd is computed by a five-line algorithm from 300 BC that is still asymptotically optimal, and it is the foundation for modular inverses, fractions, Diophantine equations and RSA. Everything in this part starts here.

a divides b (written a | b) when b = ak for some integer k. Basic consequences, all one-line proofs from the definition: divisibility is transitive; a|b and a|c imply a|(bx+cy) for any integers x,y; and a|b with b ≠ 0 implies |a| ≤ |b|.

The division algorithm: for any integers a and b > 0 there are unique q and r with a = qb + r and 0 ≤ r < b. Uniqueness matters — it is what makes "the remainder" well-defined, and it is the reason the next paragraph's recursion is deterministic.

Watch out

In C, C++, Java and Rust, % truncates toward zero, so -7 % 3 == -1, not the mathematical 2. Python and Ruby floor instead, giving 2. Any modular code that can see negative inputs needs ((a % m) + m) % m. This single discrepancy causes more competitive-programming wrong answers than any other language detail.

The Euclidean algorithm

The key lemma: gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, a mod b). Proof: if d | a and d | b then d | (a - qb) = a mod b, so every common divisor of (a,b) divides (b, a mod b); conversely if d | b and d | (a-qb) then d | a. The two pairs have identical common-divisor sets, hence identical greatest ones. ∎

Euclid, three ways
int gcd(int a, int b){ return b ? gcd(b, a % b) : a; }         // recursive

int gcd(int a, int b){ while (b) { int t = a % b; a = b; b = t; } return a; }

// binary GCD (Stein): no division, only shifts and subtraction
uint64_t bgcd(uint64_t a, uint64_t b){
    if (!a) return b; if (!b) return a;
    int s = __builtin_ctzll(a | b);        // common factors of 2
    a >>= __builtin_ctzll(a);
    do {
        b >>= __builtin_ctzll(b);
        if (a > b) { uint64_t t = a; a = b; b = t; }
        b -= a;
    } while (b);
    return a << s;
}

Complexity. Each two steps at least halve the larger argument, since a mod b < a/2 whenever b ≤ a (if b ≤ a/2 then a mod b < b ≤ a/2; otherwise a mod b = a - b < a/2). So the number of iterations is O(log min(a,b)). Lamé's theorem sharpens this: the worst case is consecutive Fibonacci numbers, and the number of steps is at most ≈ 4.785 log₁₀(min(a,b)) + 1.67. Two 64-bit numbers therefore take under 90 iterations.

Identities worth knowing

gcd(a, b) · lcm(a, b) = |a·b| ⟹ lcm(a,b) = a/gcd(a,b) · b gcd(a, 0) = |a| gcd(0,0) = 0 by convention gcd(ka, kb) = |k| · gcd(a, b) gcd(a, b) = gcd(a, b + ka) for any integer k gcd(a^m − 1, a^n − 1) = a^{gcd(m,n)} − 1 gcd(F_m, F_n) = F_{gcd(m,n)} Fibonacci gcd over a range is associative → foldable, and stabilises quickly

Compute lcm as a / gcd(a,b) * b, never a * b / gcd(a,b) — the latter overflows for inputs whose product exceeds the word size even when the answer fits comfortably.

Bézout's identity

For any a, b there exist integers x, y with ax + by = gcd(a,b), and gcd(a,b) is the smallest positive integer expressible as such a combination.

Proof of the second claim: let d be the smallest positive value of ax+by. Dividing, a = qd + r with 0 ≤ r < d; but r = a - qd = a(1-qx) + b(-qy) is also a combination, and it is smaller than d, so r = 0 and d | a. Similarly d | b, so d is a common divisor; and every common divisor divides ax+by = d, so d = gcd(a,b). ∎

Euclid's lemma follows immediately and is the cornerstone of unique factorisation: if p is prime and p | ab then p | a or p | b. Indeed if p ∤ a then gcd(p,a) = 1, so px + ay = 1; multiply by b to get pbx + aby = b, and p divides both terms on the left, hence p | b. ∎

Interview angle

"Reduce a fraction", "find if a rectangle tiles by squares", "when do two periodic events coincide" and "can I measure c litres with jugs of a and b" are all gcd questions. Recognising the gcd behind a word problem is worth more than knowing the algorithm — everyone knows the algorithm.

Problem set — 15 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove gcd(a,b) · lcm(a,b) = |ab|.

    Solution

    Write the prime factorisations a = ∏pᵢ^{αᵢ}, b = ∏pᵢ^{βᵢ}. Then gcd = ∏pᵢ^{min(αᵢ,βᵢ)} and lcm = ∏pᵢ^{max(αᵢ,βᵢ)}. Since min(x,y)+max(x,y) = x+y for every prime, the product is ∏pᵢ^{αᵢ+βᵢ} = |ab|. ∎

    Note the identity fails for three or more numbers: gcd(2,3,4)·lcm(2,3,4) = 1·12 = 12 ≠ 24, because min+max does not equal the sum when there are three exponents. The correct three-variable statement needs inclusion–exclusion over the pairwise gcds.

  2. Theory

    Prove that the Euclidean algorithm's worst case is consecutive Fibonacci numbers.

    Solution

    Claim: if the algorithm on (a,b) with a > b > 0 takes n steps, then a ≥ Fₙ₊₂ and b ≥ Fₙ₊₁. Induction on n. For n = 1, b ≥ 1 = F₂ and a ≥ 2 = F₃. For the step, after one iteration the pair is (b, a mod b) taking n-1 steps, so b ≥ Fₙ₊₁ and a mod b ≥ Fₙ. Since a ≥ b + (a mod b) ≥ Fₙ₊₁ + Fₙ = Fₙ₊₂. ∎

    Contrapositive: if a < Fₙ₊₂ then fewer than n steps. Since Fₖ ≈ φᵏ/√5, the step count is ≤ log_φ(√5 a) ≈ 4.785 log₁₀a + 1.67 — Lamé's theorem, historically the first-ever complexity analysis of an algorithm.

  3. Theory

    Find integers x, y with 240x + 46y = 2 by hand, showing the extended Euclid table.

    Solution
    240 = 5·46 + 10 46 = 4·10 + 6 10 = 1·6 + 4 6 = 1·4 + 2 4 = 2·2 + 0 ⟹ gcd = 2 back-substitute: 2 = 6 − 1·4 = 6 − (10 − 6) = 2·6 − 10 = 2(46 − 4·10) − 10 = 2·46 − 9·10 = 2·46 − 9(240 − 5·46) = 47·46 − 9·240

    So x = -9, y = 47. Check: 240(-9) + 46(47) = -2160 + 2162 = 2. ✓ The general solution is x = -9 + 23t, y = 47 - 120t, adding b/g = 23 and subtracting a/g = 120.

  4. Theory

    Prove gcd(aᵐ - 1, aₙ - 1) = a^{gcd(m,n)} - 1 for a ≥ 2.

    Solution

    Key step: aᵐ - 1 mod (aₙ - 1) = a^{m mod n} - 1 for m ≥ n. Indeed aᵐ - 1 = a^{m-n}(aₙ-1) + (a^{m-n} - 1), so subtracting multiples of aₙ-1 reduces the exponent by n each time — exactly the Euclidean step on the exponents.

    So running Euclid on (aᵐ-1, aₙ-1) mirrors running it on (m,n), terminating at a^{gcd(m,n)} - 1. ∎

    Corollary: 2ᵐ-1 and 2ₙ-1 are coprime iff m and n are — the fact behind Mersenne-prime exponents needing to be prime, and behind the structure of the multiplicative group used in some hash designs.

  5. Theory

    Show that gcd(Fᵐ, Fₙ) = F_{gcd(m,n)}.

    Solution

    Two lemmas. (i) Fᵐ₊ₙ = FᵐFₙ₊₁ + Fᵐ₋₁Fₙ (provable by induction on n). (ii) gcd(Fₙ, Fₙ₊₁) = 1 (consecutive Fibonacci numbers are coprime, by induction using the recurrence).

    From (i) with m = qn + r, repeated application gives gcd(Fᵐ, Fₙ) = gcd(Fₕ, Fₙ), mirroring Euclid on the indices. The recursion ends at gcd(F₀, F_g) = gcd(0, F_g) = F_g with g = gcd(m,n). ∎

    Concretely gcd(F₁₂, F₁₈) = F₆ = 8: gcd(144, 2584) = 8. ✓ This makes the Fibonacci sequence a strong divisibility sequence, a property shared by aₙ-1 from the previous problem.

  6. Theory

    Given gcd(a,b) = 1, prove that gcd(a+b, ab) = 1 and that gcd(a+b, a-b) ∈ {1,2}.

    Solution

    First: let p be a prime dividing both a+b and ab. From p | ab and Euclid's lemma, p | a or p | b; say p|a. Then from p | a+b we get p | b, contradicting gcd(a,b)=1. So no prime divides both. ∎

    Second: let d = gcd(a+b, a-b). Then d | (a+b)+(a-b) = 2a and d | (a+b)-(a-b) = 2b, so d | gcd(2a,2b) = 2gcd(a,b) = 2. ∎ It is 2 exactly when a and b are both odd, and 1 when they have different parities.

  7. Theory

    Prove that √2 is irrational using Bézout / Euclid's lemma rather than the parity argument.

    Solution

    Suppose √2 = p/q with gcd(p,q)=1. Then p² = 2q², so 2 | p². By Euclid's lemma (2 is prime), 2 | p. Write p = 2r: then 4r² = 2q², so q² = 2r², and again 2 | q. But then 2 | gcd(p,q) = 1, contradiction. ∎

    The value of this phrasing: it generalises verbatim. For any prime p and any n not a perfect p-th power, the same argument shows n^{1/p} is irrational — Euclid's lemma is doing all the work, and it is exactly what fails for a composite radicand.

  8. Program

    Implement gcd for arbitrary-precision integers and compare Euclid, binary GCD and the language built-in on 1000-bit numbers.

    Solution
    import math, random, time
def euclid(a, b):
    while b: a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def binary(a, b):
    if not a: return b
    if not b: return a
    s = ((a | b) & -(a | b)).bit_length() - 1
    a >>= (a & -a).bit_length() - 1
    while b:
        b >>= (b & -b).bit_length() - 1
        if a > b: a, b = b, a
        b -= a
    return a << s

    For big integers, Euclid wins because each % is a single optimised bignum division, whereas binary GCD does O(\text{bits}) subtractions each costing O(\text{bits}/64) words — quadratic. Binary GCD's advantage is only on fixed-width machine integers where division is 20–40 cycles and shifts are 1.

    math.gcd beats both, using Lehmer's algorithm: it runs several Euclid steps on the leading digits alone, then applies the accumulated 2×2 transformation to the full numbers, doing far fewer full-precision divisions.

  9. Program

    Given an array of up to 10⁵ integers, answer 10⁵ range-gcd queries. Give an O(n log n + q log n) solution and an O(n log n + q) one.

    Solution
    // sparse table: gcd is idempotent, so overlapping blocks are fine
int sp[LOG][N];
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sp[0][i] = a[i];
for (int k = 1; (1<<k) <= n; k++)
    for (int i = 0; i + (1<<k) <= n; i++)
        sp[k][i] = gcd(sp[k-1][i], sp[k-1][i + (1<<(k-1))]);

int query(int l, int r){                      // O(1) per query
    int k = 31 - __builtin_clz(r - l + 1);
    return gcd(sp[k][l], sp[k][r - (1<<k) + 1]);
}

    The sparse table gives O(1) queries (times the O(log) cost of one gcd) because gcd is idempotent — gcd(x,x) = x — so the two overlapping halves may double-count. A segment tree gives O(log n) queries but supports updates.

    A useful extra fact: for a fixed left endpoint, the gcd of a[l..r] takes only O(log A) distinct values as r grows, since each change divides the previous by at least 2. That gives an O(n log A) algorithm for counting all subarrays with a given gcd.

  10. Program

    Implement exact rational arithmetic (add, multiply, compare) with automatic reduction, and explain where overflow can still occur.

    Solution
    struct Frac {
    long long n, d;                                    // invariant: d > 0, gcd=1
    Frac(long long a = 0, long long b = 1){
        if (b < 0) a = -a, b = -b;
        long long g = std::gcd(llabs(a), b); if (!g) g = 1;
        n = a/g; d = b/g;
    }
    Frac operator+(const Frac& o) const {
        long long g = std::gcd(d, o.d);                // reduce BEFORE multiplying
        return Frac(n*(o.d/g) + o.n*(d/g), d/g*o.d);
    }
    Frac operator*(const Frac& o) const {
        long long g1 = std::gcd(llabs(n), o.d), g2 = std::gcd(llabs(o.n), d);
        return Frac((n/g1)*(o.n/g2), (d/g2)*(o.d/g1));
    }
    bool operator<(const Frac& o) const {               // avoid n*o.d overflow
        return (__int128)n * o.d < (__int128)o.n * d;
    }
};

    Cross-reducing before multiplying keeps intermediates as small as possible, but overflow remains possible: adding 1/p for many distinct primes p makes the denominator their product, growing exponentially. There is no fix within fixed-width integers — the only real answers are big integers, or a problem-specific bound proving denominators stay small. Always ask which one you have before shipping rationals.

  11. Program

    Given n ≤ 10⁵ integers, count pairs (i,j) with gcd(aᵢ,a₃) = 1, values up to 10⁶.

    Solution

    Count multiples, then Möbius-invert. Let cᵈ be the number of elements divisible by d; the pairs with gcd divisible by d number C(cᵈ,2).

    MAX = 10**6 + 1
cnt = [0]*MAX
for v in a: cnt[v] += 1
mult = [0]*MAX
for d in range(1, MAX):
    for m in range(d, MAX, d): mult[d] += cnt[m]      # O(MAX log MAX)
mu = mobius_sieve(MAX)
ans = sum(mu[d] * (mult[d]*(mult[d]-1)//2) for d in range(1, MAX))

    By Möbius inversion (Chapter 25), ∑_d μ(d)·\#\{\text{pairs both divisible by } d\} counts exactly the coprime pairs, because ∑_{d | g} μ(d) = [g = 1]. Total cost O(A log A + n) — about 1.4·10⁷ operations, comfortably fast.

  12. Program

    Implement the Stern–Brocot tree / continued-fraction search to find the fraction with the smallest denominator in a given real interval (x, y).

    Solution
    from fractions import Fraction
def simplest(x, y):                 # smallest-denominator fraction in (x, y)
    # both bounds as Fractions, x < y
    fx = x.numerator // x.denominator
    if fx + 1 < y or (fx + 1 == y and False):
        pass
    if x < 0: return -simplest(-y, -x)
    i = x.numerator // x.denominator
    if i + 1 <= y and i + 1 > x: return Fraction(i + 1)
    if i > x and i < y: return Fraction(i)
    # recurse on the fractional parts, inverted (continued-fraction step)
    inner = simplest(1/(y - i), 1/(x - i))
    return i + 1/inner

    This is the continued-fraction algorithm: strip the integer part, invert (which swaps and reverses the interval), recurse. It runs in O(log) steps — the same recursion as Euclid, which is not a coincidence: the continued-fraction expansion of a/b is precisely the sequence of quotients produced by the Euclidean algorithm.

    Applications: finding the best rational approximation with a bounded denominator (the convergents are provably optimal), recovering an exact fraction from a floating-point value, and reconstructing a rational from its value modulo a large prime.

  13. Program

    Two gears with a and b teeth mesh. After how many rotations of the first do both return to their initial positions? Handle a,b ≤ 10¹⁸.

    Solution
    from math import gcd
def rotations(a, b):
    L = a // gcd(a, b) * b        # lcm, computed without overflow
    return L // a                 # rotations of the first gear

    Both realign after lcm(a,b) teeth have passed, which is lcm/a rotations of the first gear and lcm/b of the second. Note a // gcd * b rather than a * b // gcd: with a,b ≈ 10¹⁸ the product is 10³⁶ and overflows any 64-bit type, while the answer itself may be small.

    The same computation answers "when do two cron schedules coincide", "what is the period of two interleaved cycles", and "how long until two rotating replicas realign".

  14. Program

    Compute gcd of a large set incrementally and exploit early termination. Show why the loop usually stops after a handful of elements.

    Solution
    def array_gcd(a):
    g = 0
    for v in a:
        g = math.gcd(g, v)
        if g == 1: return 1          # cannot decrease further
    return g

    Each step either leaves g unchanged or divides it by at least 2, so g strictly decreases at most log₂(a₀) times — after that the gcd is stable and the loop is just confirming it. Better, for random inputs the gcd hits 1 almost immediately: the probability that k random integers are coprime is 1/ζ(k), which is 0.608 for k = 2, 0.832 for k = 3, and 0.924 for k = 4. So the early exit fires within about 3 elements on average.

    The 1/ζ(k) result itself is a nice Möbius computation: P = ∑_d μ(d)/dᵏ = 1/ζ(k), previewing Chapter 25.

  15. Program

    Write a stress test comparing your gcd to a brute-force divisor search on random small inputs, including edge cases (zeros, negatives, equal values, maximum values).

    Solution
    def brute(a, b):
    if a == 0 and b == 0: return 0
    return max(d for d in range(1, max(abs(a), abs(b)) + 1)
               if (a % d == 0) and (b % d == 0))

import random
cases = [(0,0), (0,5), (5,0), (-12,18), (12,-18), (-12,-18), (7,7), (1,1)]
cases += [(random.randint(-50,50), random.randint(-50,50)) for _ in range(5000)]
for a, b in cases:
    assert my_gcd(a, b) == (0 if a==0==b else brute(a,b)), (a, b)

    The edge cases are where implementations actually break: gcd(0,0) (convention: 0), negatives (should the result be positive? — yes, by convention gcd is non-negative), and gcd(\text{INT\_MIN}, x) in C++ where abs(INT_MIN) is undefined behaviour. Handling these explicitly, and testing them, is the difference between a snippet and a library function.

Chapter 15

Extended Euclid and linear Diophantine equations

The extended Euclidean algorithm is a constructive proof of Bézout's identity, and it is the single most reused routine in number-theoretic code: it computes modular inverses, solves linear congruences, and drives CRT and RSA key generation.

Ordinary Euclid returns g = gcd(a,b). Extended Euclid additionally returns x and y with ax + by = g. It is the same recursion carrying two extra coefficients back up.

Extended Euclid, recursive and iterative
// returns g, and sets x,y with a*x + b*y = g
long long extgcd(long long a, long long b, long long &x, long long &y){
    if (!b) { x = 1; y = 0; return a; }
    long long x1, y1;
    long long g = extgcd(b, a % b, x1, y1);
    x = y1;
    y = x1 - (a / b) * y1;          // because a = (a/b)*b + a%b
    return g;
}

// iterative: track the row (x, y) alongside the remainder
long long extgcd_it(long long a, long long b, long long &x, long long &y){
    long long x0 = 1, y0 = 0, x1 = 0, y1 = 1;
    while (b) {
        long long q = a / b;
        a -= q*b;  std::swap(a, b);
        x0 -= q*x1; std::swap(x0, x1);
        y0 -= q*y1; std::swap(y0, y1);
    }
    x = x0; y = y0; return a;
}

The back-substitution step is worth understanding rather than memorising. If the recursive call gives b·x₁ + (a mod b)·y₁ = g, substitute a mod b = a - ⌊a/b⌋b:

b·x₁ + (a − ⌊a/b⌋·b)·y₁ = g a·y₁ + b·(x₁ − ⌊a/b⌋·y₁) = g ⟹ x = y₁, y = x₁ − ⌊a/b⌋·y₁

Size of the coefficients. The x, y produced satisfy |x| ≤ b/(2g) and |y| ≤ a/(2g), so they never overflow if a and b fit. That bound is worth knowing, because a naive worry about growth is the usual reason people avoid the algorithm.

Linear Diophantine equations

The equation ax + by = c has integer solutions iff gcd(a,b) | c. Necessity: g divides the left side always. Sufficiency: Bézout gives ax₀+by₀ = g, so scaling by c/g gives a solution. The full solution set is then a lattice line:

x = x₀·(c/g) + (b/g)·t y = y₀·(c/g) − (a/g)·t for every integer t

The step sizes are b/g and a/g, not b and a — a very common bug. Once you have the parametric form, questions like "the smallest positive x" or "how many solutions with 0 ≤ x ≤ N" become interval arithmetic on t.

Principle
