To find the solution with smallest non-negative x: compute m = b/g, then x = ((x₀(c/g)) mod m + m) mod m, and recover y from the equation. Doing the reduction in the t parameter rather than by looping is what makes this O(log) rather than O(\text{answer}).

Linear congruences

ax ≡ b (mod n) is the Diophantine equation ax + ny = b in disguise. So it is solvable iff g = gcd(a,n) divides b, and then there are exactly g solutions modulo n, spaced n/g apart.

Solving ax ≡ b (mod n) completely
vector<long long> solve(long long a, long long b, long long n){
    long long x, y, g = extgcd(a, n, x, y);
    if (b % g) return {};                       // no solution
    long long x0 = (__int128)x % n * (b / g) % n;
    x0 = ((x0 % (n/g)) + n/g) % (n/g);          // canonical smallest
    vector<long long> r;
    for (long long i = 0; i < g; i++) r.push_back(x0 + i * (n/g));
    return r;                                   // exactly g solutions mod n
}

The special case g = 1 gives a unique solution, and with b = 1 that solution is the modular inverse of a — the subject of Chapter 17, and the reason extended Euclid appears in every modular-arithmetic library.

Interview angle

"Given jugs of 3 and 5 litres, can you measure 4?" is 3x + 5y = 4, solvable since gcd = 1 | 4, with x = 3, y = -1 — fill the 3-litre jug three times and empty the 5-litre once. Framing the puzzle as a Diophantine equation gives both the yes/no answer and the procedure, which is a much stronger response than a search.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Solve 1215x + 1000y = 5 completely, showing the back-substitution.

    Solution
    FORWARD (Euclid) BACKWARD (substitute upward) 1215 = 1·1000 + 215 5 = 65 − 6·10 1000 = 4· 215 + 140 = 65 − 6(75 − 65) = 7·65 − 6·75 215 = 1· 140 + 75 = 7(140 − 75) − 6·75 = 7·140 − 13·75 140 = 1· 75 + 65 = 7·140 − 13(215 − 140) = 20·140 − 13·215 75 = 1· 65 + 10 = 20(1000 − 4·215) − 13·215 65 = 6· 10 + 5 = 20·1000 − 93·215 10 = 2· 5 + 0 = 20·1000 − 93(1215 − 1000) gcd = 5 = 113·1000 − 93·1215

    So x₀ = -93, y₀ = 113. Check: 1215(-93) + 1000(113) = -112{,}995 + 113{,}000 = 5. ✓

    General solution, stepping by b/g = 200 and a/g = 243:

    x = -93 + 200t,\quad y = 113 - 243t.

    Smallest non-negative x is at t = 1: (x,y) = (107, -130), and indeed 1215·107 - 1000·130 = 130{,}005 - 130{,}000 = 5. ✓

    Hand back-substitution is error-prone — each line rewrites one remainder in terms of the two above it, and a single sign slip propagates to the end. Always verify by substituting into the original equation; in code, assert a*x + b*y == g before returning.

  2. Theory

    Prove that ax + by = c has an integer solution iff gcd(a,b) | c.

    Solution

    (⟹) Let g = gcd(a,b). Then g | a and g | b, so g | ax+by = c.

    (⟸) By Bézout there are x₀,y₀ with ax₀+by₀ = g. If c = kg, then a(kx₀) + b(ky₀) = kg = c. ∎

    For the solution set: if (x,y) and (x',y') both work, subtracting gives a(x-x') = -b(y-y'), so (a/g)(x-x') = -(b/g)(y-y'). Since gcd(a/g, b/g) = 1, (b/g) | (x-x'), giving the parametrisation with step b/g.

  3. Theory

    Prove the bound |x| ≤ b/(2g) on the coefficient extended Euclid returns.

    Solution

    Track the sequences xᵢ and yᵢ generated by the iterative algorithm. Two facts, both by induction: the signs of consecutive xᵢ alternate, and |xᵢ| is non-decreasing with |xᵢ₊₁| = |xᵢ₋₁| + qᵢ|xᵢ|. One also shows the invariant |xᵢ| ≤ bᵢ₊₁/(2g) where bᵢ is the remainder sequence. At termination this gives |x| ≤ b/(2g). ∎

    Practically: for 64-bit inputs the coefficients stay within 64 bits, so no overflow occurs inside extgcd. Overflow appears only when you afterwards multiply x by c/g — which is why the code in the chapter casts to __int128 at exactly that step.

  4. Theory

    How many solutions does 6x ≡ 4 (mod 10) have modulo 10? List them.

    Solution

    g = gcd(6,10) = 2, and 2 | 4, so there are exactly 2 solutions mod 10, spaced 10/2 = 5 apart.

    Reduce: 3x ≡ 2 (mod 5). Since 3·2 = 6 ≡ 1, the inverse of 3 mod 5 is 2, giving x ≡ 4 (mod 5). So x ∈ {4, 9} mod 10. Check: 6·4 = 24 ≡ 4 ✓, 6·9 = 54 ≡ 4 ✓.

    The general recipe — divide the whole congruence by g including the modulus, solve the now-invertible congruence, then expand back to g solutions — is the one to internalise.

  5. Theory

    The Frobenius / Chicken McNugget problem: for coprime a,b > 1, prove the largest integer not representable as ax+by with x,y ≥ 0 is ab-a-b.

    Solution

    Not representable: suppose ab-a-b = ax+by with x,y ≥ 0. Then ab = a(x+1) + b(y+1), so a | b(y+1); as gcd(a,b)=1, a | y+1, so y+1 ≥ a. Similarly x+1 ≥ b. Then a(x+1)+b(y+1) ≥ 2ab > ab, contradiction. ∎

    Everything larger is representable: for n > ab-a-b, choose x ≡ n b^{-1} (mod a) with 0 ≤ x < a. Then n - bx ≡ 0 (mod a) and n - bx > ab-a-b - b(a-1) = -a, so n-bx ≥ 0 and divisible by a. ∎

    Bonus: exactly (a-1)(b-1)/2 non-negative integers are unrepresentable — a nice symmetry, since n is representable iff ab-a-b-n is not.

  6. Theory

    Show that ax ≡ b (mod n) has a unique solution for every b iff gcd(a,n) = 1.

    Solution

    (⟸) If gcd(a,n)=1, extended Euclid gives a^{-1}, and x ≡ a^{-1}b is a solution; it is unique because ax ≡ ax' implies a(x-x') ≡ 0, and multiplying by a^{-1} gives x ≡ x'.

    (⟹) If g = gcd(a,n) > 1, take b = 1: g | ax + ny always, but g ∤ 1, so there is no solution at all — failing existence, hence failing uniqueness-for-every-b. ∎

    Restated: x ↦ ax is a bijection on ℤ/n exactly when a is a unit. This is why the "multiply by an odd constant" step in hash functions mixing modulo 2ᵏ is invertible — odd numbers are precisely the units mod a power of 2.

  7. Theory

    Given ax+by = c with a solution, count the solutions with 0 ≤ x ≤ X and 0 ≤ y ≤ Y.

    Solution

    Parametrise: x = x₀+ (b/g)t, y = y₀ - (a/g)t. Each constraint becomes an interval on t:

    0 ≤ x₀+(b/g)t ≤ X gives t ∈ [\lceil -x₀g/b \rceil, \lfloor (X-x₀)g/b \rfloor], and 0 ≤ y₀-(a/g)t ≤ Y gives another interval (with reversed direction since the coefficient is negative).

    The answer is the length of the intersection, max(0, hi - lo + 1). ∎ The whole problem is one gcd plus careful floor/ceiling arithmetic on negatives — which is where the bugs live, so use floordiv/ceildiv helpers rather than inline division.

  8. Program

    Implement extended Euclid iteratively and verify ax+by=g plus the coefficient bounds on 10⁶ random 62-bit pairs.

    Solution
    def extgcd(a, b):
    old_r, r = a, b
    old_s, s = 1, 0
    old_t, t = 0, 1
    while r:
        q = old_r // r
        old_r, r = r, old_r - q*r
        old_s, s = s, old_s - q*s
        old_t, t = t, old_t - q*t
    return old_r, old_s, old_t          # g, x, y

import random
for _ in range(10**6):
    a = random.randrange(1, 1 << 62); b = random.randrange(1, 1 << 62)
    g, x, y = extgcd(a, b)
    assert a*x + b*y == g
    assert g == math.gcd(a, b)
    assert abs(x) <= b // (2*g) + 1 and abs(y) <= a // (2*g) + 1

    The +1 slack in the assertion covers the degenerate cases a = b and b | a, where the bound is attained exactly. Testing with random and structured inputs (equal values, one dividing the other, consecutive Fibonacci numbers) is what catches the sign errors that random testing misses.

  9. Program

    Solve ax+by=c for |a|,|b|,|c| ≤ 10¹⁸, printing the solution with the smallest non-negative x, or reporting impossibility.

    Solution
    def solve(a, b, c):
    g, x, y = extgcd(abs(a), abs(b))
    if c % g: return None
    x *= c // g; y *= c // g
    if a < 0: x = -x
    if b < 0: y = -y
    m = abs(b) // g                        # period of x
    k = (-x) // m + (1 if (-x) % m else 0) if x < 0 else -(x // m)
    x += k * m
    if x < 0: x += m
    y = (c - a*x) // b
    return x, y

    Two traps. First, x *= c // g can overflow 64-bit even though the final reduced answer is small — do the multiplication in 128-bit, or reduce x modulo b/g before scaling and again after. Second, Python's floor division makes the shift arithmetic easy, while C++'s truncating division needs an explicit floordiv; mixing the two conventions is the standard source of off-by-one here.

  10. Program

    Implement modular inverse via extended Euclid, and separately via Fermat when the modulus is prime. Benchmark and state when each is correct.

    Solution
    def inv_egcd(a, m):                  # works for ANY m with gcd(a,m)=1
    g, x, _ = extgcd(a % m, m)
    if g != 1: raise ValueError("not invertible")
    return x % m

def inv_fermat(a, p):                # requires p PRIME and p ∤ a
    return pow(a, p-2, p)

    Extended Euclid does O(log m) divisions; Fermat does O(log p) modular multiplications, roughly 1.5 log₂p of them. In practice extgcd is 3–10× faster because division, though slower than multiplication, is needed far fewer times.

    Correctness domains differ and this matters: Fermat silently returns a wrong answer for composite moduli rather than failing — pow(2, 8, 10) = 6, and 2·6 = 12 ≡ 2 ≠ 1 (mod 10). Since 2 is not invertible mod 10 at all, the correct behaviour is an error. Always use extgcd unless the modulus is a known prime.

  11. Program

    Compute inverses of 1..n modulo a prime p in O(n) total.

    Solution
    inv = [0]*(n+1)
inv[1] = 1
for i in range(2, n+1):
    inv[i] = (p - (p//i) * inv[p % i] % p) % p

    Derivation: write p = qi + r with q = ⌊p/i⌋ and r = p mod i. Then qi + r ≡ 0 (mod p), so qi ≡ -r. Multiply both sides by i^{-1}r^{-1}: q r^{-1} ≡ -i^{-1}, giving i^{-1} ≡ -q · r^{-1} (mod p). Since r < i, the value is already computed. ∎

    One multiplication and one subtraction per element — vastly faster than n separate pow calls (O(n log p)) and simpler than the prefix-product method. It requires p prime and n < p.

  12. Program

    Given a coin system with two coprime denominations a and b, compute the Frobenius number and the count of unrepresentable amounts. Then handle three denominations by DP.

    Solution
    # two coins: closed form
frob = a*b - a - b
unrep = (a-1)*(b-1)//2

# three or more: no closed form; DP over residues mod the smallest coin
def frobenius(coins):
    a = min(coins)
    INF = float('inf')
    dist = [INF]*a; dist[0] = 0          # dist[r] = smallest reachable ≡ r mod a
    import heapq
    pq = [(0, 0)]
    while pq:
        d, r = heapq.heappop(pq)
        if d > dist[r]: continue
        for c in coins:
            nd = d + c; nr = nd % a
            if nd < dist[nr]: dist[nr] = nd; heapq.heappush(pq, (nd, nr))
    return max(dist) - a                  # largest unrepresentable

    This is the "coin problem" / Round Robin algorithm: a shortest-path computation on the residue graph mod the smallest coin, O(a · k log a). There is no closed form for three coins — computing the Frobenius number for a general coin set is NP-hard when the number of coins is part of the input, which is exactly why the two-coin formula feels like such a gift.

  13. Program

    Implement the "water jugs" solver that outputs an actual sequence of pours reaching a target c, using the Diophantine solution rather than BFS.

    Solution
    def pours(a, b, c):
    g = math.gcd(a, b)
    if c % g or c > max(a, b): return None
    # Strategy: repeatedly fill A, pour into B, empty B when full.
    # This realises c = a*x - b*y for the x, y from extended Euclid.
    x = y = 0; steps = []
    while x != c and y != c:
        if x == 0: x = a; steps.append(f"fill A -> ({x},{y})")
        elif y == b: y = 0; steps.append(f"empty B -> ({x},{y})")
        else:
            t = min(x, b - y); x -= t; y += t
            steps.append(f"pour A->B -> ({x},{y})")
    return steps

    The loop terminates because the sequence of states (x,y) cycles through all multiples of g modulo b, hitting c by Bézout. The number of steps is O(a+b), and the reverse strategy (fill B, pour into A) gives the other Bézout coefficient and may be shorter — checking both and picking the shorter is the complete answer.

    The point of the chapter is that existence is settled in O(log) by a gcd, with no search at all; the search is only needed to print a witness.

  14. Program

    Write a solver for systems of two linear Diophantine equations in three unknowns, reducing to the one-equation case.

    Solution
    # a1*x + b1*y + c1*z = d1
# a2*x + b2*y + c2*z = d2
# Eliminate one variable by taking an integer combination that clears it.
def solve2(a1,b1,c1,d1, a2,b2,c2,d2):
    g, u, v = extgcd(c1, c2)                 # u*c1 + v*c2 = g
    if (c1 and c2) and (d1*c2 - d2*c1) % g: pass
    # Row-reduce over Z using the Hermite normal form of the 2x3 matrix,
    # keeping the unimodular transform to map solutions back.
    ...

    The systematic method is the Hermite normal form: apply unimodular row operations (add an integer multiple of one row to another; swap; negate) to reduce the coefficient matrix to an upper-triangular form, tracking the transform. Solvability is then read off the triangular system, and the solution set is a coset of the integer kernel lattice.

    Equivalently, for two equations in three unknowns: eliminate z using extended Euclid on (c₁,c₂) to get one equation in x and y, solve that as in the chapter, then back-substitute for z — and check that the substitution divides exactly. The general theory is the Smith normal form, which handles any number of equations and unknowns and gives the structure of the solution lattice.

Chapter 16

Modular arithmetic as a ring

Working modulo n means working in a finite ring where addition and multiplication behave normally but division does not. Knowing exactly which rules survive is what keeps contest code and cryptographic code correct.

a ≡ b (mod n) means n | (a-b). This is an equivalence relation (Chapter 5), and its classes — the residue classes — form the set ℤ/nℤ = {0, 1, …, n-1}.

The essential fact is that addition and multiplication are well defined on classes: if a ≡ a' and b ≡ b' then a+b ≡ a'+b' and ab ≡ a'b'. Proof of the second: ab - a'b' = ab - a'b + a'b - a'b' = b(a-a') + a'(b-b'), and n divides both terms. ∎

That is exactly what licenses reducing at every step, which is the single most important practical consequence: you never need to compute a huge intermediate value, only its residue.

These SURVIVE modular reduction These DO NOT ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── a + b, a − b, a · b a / b (needs an inverse) a^k for k ≥ 0 a^k for k in an exponent (exponents reduce mod φ(n), Ch. 20) comparisons of equality comparisons of order (<, >) √a, log a (hard problems)

ℤ/n is a commutative ring with unity: closed, associative, commutative, distributive, with 0 and 1. It is a field — every non-zero element invertible — precisely when n is prime. For composite n there are zero divisors: 2 · 3 ≡ 0 (mod 6) even though neither factor is 0, which is why cancellation fails.

Principle

ac ≡ bc (mod n) does not imply a ≡ b (mod n). The correct cancellation law is a ≡ b (mod n/gcd(c,n)). Example: 2·4 ≡ 2·1 (mod 6) but 4 ¬equiv; 1 (mod 6) — only 4 ≡ 1 (mod 3). Cancelling blindly is the most common modular-arithmetic bug in proofs.

Implementation, where it actually goes wrong

Safe modular arithmetic in a fixed-width language
const long long MOD = 1'000'000'007;

long long add(long long a, long long b){ return (a + b) % MOD; }
long long sub(long long a, long long b){ return (a - b + MOD) % MOD; }   // !
long long mul(long long a, long long b){ return a * b % MOD; }          // ok: MOD² < 2⁶³

// If MOD can be up to 10¹⁸, a*b overflows.  Use __int128:
long long mul_big(long long a, long long b, long long m){
    return (__int128)a * b % m;
}

// Normalising an arbitrary (possibly negative) value:
long long norm(long long a, long long m){ return ((a % m) + m) % m; }

Three rules that prevent nearly all bugs: subtract by adding MOD first; check that MOD² fits in your integer type before multiplying; and normalise any value that could be negative before using it as an index or comparing it.

Choice of modulus

Contest problems use 10⁹+7 or 998{,}244{,}353. Both are prime, so every non-zero residue is invertible. The second is chosen for NTT (Chapter 51): 998244353 = 119 · 2²³ + 1, so it has 2²³-th roots of unity, and 3 is a primitive root. 10⁹+7 has no such structure — 10⁹+6 = 2 · 500000003 — so convolution modulo it needs three NTTs and CRT.

Both are just under 2³⁰, so a product of two residues fits in 60 bits and never overflows a signed 64-bit integer. That is not a coincidence; it is why those specific primes were chosen.

Divisibility tests, explained

Every schoolroom divisibility rule is a statement about the base modulo n. Since 10 ≡ 1 (mod 9), every power of 10 is ≡ 1, so a number is congruent to its digit sum mod 9. Since 10 ≡ -1 (mod 11), powers alternate +1, -1, giving the alternating-sum rule. Since 1000 ≡ -1 (mod 7·11·13), grouping digits in threes with alternating signs tests all three at once.

Interview angle

"Compute aⁿ mod m for huge n" is a two-part answer: binary exponentiation for the algorithm, and — if the exponent is itself huge — reduce it modulo φ(m) by Euler's theorem, but only when gcd(a,m)=1. The second half is what distinguishes someone who has used this from someone who has read about it.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove the cancellation law: ac ≡ bc (mod n) implies a ≡ b (mod n/gcd(c,n)).

    Solution

    Let g = gcd(c,n), c = gc', n = gn' with gcd(c',n')=1. From n | c(a-b) we get gn' | gc'(a-b), so n' | c'(a-b). Since gcd(c',n')=1, Euclid's lemma gives n' | (a-b), i.e. a ≡ b (mod n/g). ∎

    When gcd(c,n)=1 this reduces to ordinary cancellation. The general form is what you need whenever the multiplier shares a factor with the modulus — for instance dividing a congruence through by 2 when the modulus is even.

  2. Theory

    Find the last two digits of 7²⁰²⁵.

    Solution

    Work mod 100. gcd(7,100)=1 and φ(100) = 100(1-1/2)(1-1/5) = 40. So 7⁴⁰ ≡ 1, and 2025 = 40·50 + 25, giving 7²⁰²⁵ ≡ 7²⁵.

    Now 7² = 49, 7⁴ = 2401 ≡ 1 (mod 100). So the order of 7 divides 4, and 7²⁵ = 7^{4·6+1} ≡ 7.

    Answer: 07. The shortcut — noticing 7⁴ ≡ 1 directly — beats Euler here, and looking for a small order before invoking the theorem is generally worthwhile.

  3. Theory

    Prove the divisibility rules for 9, 11 and 7·11·13 = 1001 from modular arithmetic.

    Solution

    Write N = ∑ dᵢ 10ᵢ.

    • 9: 10 ≡ 1, so 10ᵢ ≡ 1 and N ≡ ∑ dᵢ — the digit sum. Same for 3.
    • 11: 10 ≡ -1, so 10ᵢ ≡ (-1)ᵢ and N ≡ d₀ - d₁ + d₂ - … — the alternating sum.
    • 1001: group digits in threes, N = ∑ gᵢ 1000ᵢ with 1000 ≡ -1 (mod 1001), so N ≡ g₀ - g₁ + g₂ - …. Since 1001 = 7·11·13, this single alternating three-digit-group sum tests divisibility by all three primes.

    The general principle: a divisibility rule in base b for modulus m is easy exactly when bᵏ ≡ ±1 (mod m) for a small k — i.e. when the multiplicative order of b mod m is small.

  4. Theory

    Show that ℤ/n is a field iff n is prime.

    Solution

    (⟸) If n = p is prime and 1 ≤ a < p, then gcd(a,p) = 1 (p's only divisors are 1 and p), so Bézout gives ax + py = 1, i.e. ax ≡ 1. Every non-zero element is invertible.

    (⟹) If n is composite, write n = ab with 1 < a,b < n. Then a ≠ 0 in ℤ/n, but if a had an inverse a^{-1}, multiplying ab ≡ 0 by it gives b ≡ 0 — false. So a is a zero divisor and not invertible; ℤ/n is not a field. ∎ (And n = 1 gives the zero ring, which is excluded by convention.)

  5. Theory

    How many solutions does x² ≡ 1 (mod n) have for n = 8, 15, 105? Find the pattern.

    Solution
    • n = 8: x = 1,3,5,7 all square to 1 mod 8 — 4 solutions.
    • n = 15 = 3·5: solutions mod 3 are ±1 (2 of them), mod 5 likewise (2), so by CRT 4: x = 1,4,11,14.
    • n = 105 = 3·5·7: 2³ = 8 solutions.

    Pattern: the count is 2^k where k is the number of odd prime factors, multiplied by 1, 1, 2 or 4 for the power of 2 dividing n (respectively 2⁰/2¹, 2², 2ᵏ with k≥3). The n = 8 case having four square roots of 1 is why RSA-style square-root attacks work modulo composites, and why Rabin's cryptosystem produces four decryptions.

  6. Theory

    Explain why 998244353 is the preferred NTT modulus and what makes a prime "NTT-friendly".

    Solution

    An NTT of length 2ᵏ needs a primitive 2ᵏ-th root of unity in ℤ/p, which exists iff 2ᵏ | p-1. So an NTT-friendly prime has the form p = c · 2ᵏ + 1 with k large.

    998244353 = 119 · 2²³ + 1, supporting transforms up to length 2²³ = 8{,}388{,}608, with 3 as a primitive root (so g = 3^{(p-1)/2ᵏ} is the required root). It is also just under 2³⁰ so products fit in 64 bits.

    By contrast 10⁹+7 - 1 = 2 · 500000003, divisible by only 2¹, so no NTT of length beyond 2 exists modulo it. Convolving modulo 10⁹+7 requires either three NTTs modulo different friendly primes plus CRT, or splitting each coefficient into high and low halves and using floating-point FFT.

  7. Theory

    Prove that n⁵ ≡ n (mod 30) for every integer n.

    Solution

    30 = 2·3·5, so by CRT it suffices to check each prime separately, and Fermat's little theorem gives each immediately:

    • mod 2: n² ≡ n, so n⁵ ≡ n.
    • mod 3: n³ ≡ n, so n⁵ = n³·n² ≡ n·n² = n³ ≡ n.
    • mod 5: n⁵ ≡ n directly.

    Agreeing modulo 2, 3 and 5, they agree mod 30. ∎

    Generalisation: nᵏ ≡ n (mod m) for all n exactly when m is squarefree and (p-1) | (k-1) for every prime p | m — the Korselt criterion, which also characterises Carmichael numbers (Chapter 23).

  8. Program

    Implement a modular arithmetic struct with add, sub, mul, pow and inverse, templated on the modulus, and stress-test it against Python's arbitrary precision.

    Solution
    template<long long MOD>
struct Mint {
    long long v;
    Mint(long long x = 0){ v = x % MOD; if (v < 0) v += MOD; }
    Mint operator+(Mint o) const { long long r = v + o.v; return Mint(r >= MOD ? r-MOD : r); }
    Mint operator-(Mint o) const { long long r = v - o.v; return Mint(r < 0 ? r+MOD : r); }
    Mint operator*(Mint o) const { return Mint((__int128)v * o.v % MOD); }
    Mint pow(long long e) const {
        Mint r = 1, b = *this;
        while (e){ if (e & 1) r = r*b; b = b*b; e >>= 1; }
        return r;
    }
    Mint inv() const { return pow(MOD - 2); }        // MOD must be prime
};

    Using conditional subtraction rather than % for add and sub is a measurable win (a branch or a cmov against a 20–40 cycle division). The __int128 cast in mul is what makes it safe for any MOD < 2⁶³; without it, correctness silently depends on MOD < 2³¹.

    Stress test by generating random operation sequences and replaying them with Python integers reduced only at the end — that catches exactly the overflow and normalisation bugs this struct exists to prevent.

  9. Program

    Compute a · b mod m for m up to 10¹⁸ without 128-bit integers, three ways.

    Solution
    // 1. Russian peasant: O(log b), always correct
long long mulmod(long long a, long long b, long long m){
    long long r = 0; a %= m;
    while (b){ if (b & 1) r = (r + a) % m; a = (a + a) % m; b >>= 1; }
    return r;
}

// 2. long double trick: O(1), correct for m < 2^62 on x86 (80-bit extended)
long long mulmod_fast(long long a, long long b, long long m){
    long long q = (long long)((long double)a * b / m);
    long long r = a*b - q*m;                    // both sides wrap identically
    return r < 0 ? r + m : (r >= m ? r - m : r);
}

// 3. Barrett / Montgomery reduction: precompute m⁻¹ mod 2⁶⁴, replace
//    division by multiplication and shifts — the fastest when m is fixed.

    Method 1 is 64× slower but portable and obviously correct. Method 2 relies on long double having 64 bits of mantissa (true on x86, false on ARM where it is 53 bits or 113) — a genuine portability trap. Method 3 is what real libraries use, amortising a per-modulus setup over many operations.

  10. Program

    Given a string of up to 10⁶ digits, compute it modulo m, and answer queries for the value of any substring modulo m.

    Solution
    # prefix values: val[i] = int(s[:i]) mod m
val = [0]*(n+1)
for i, ch in enumerate(s):
    val[i+1] = (val[i]*10 + int(ch)) % m

pw = [1]*(n+1)
for i in range(1, n+1): pw[i] = pw[i-1]*10 % m

def substring(l, r):                 # s[l:r] as a number, mod m
    return (val[r] - val[l]*pw[r-l]) % m

    Each query is O(1) after O(n) preprocessing. The formula is the same "remove the prefix, shifted" identity that underlies polynomial (Rabin–Karp) hashing — a substring hash is a substring value in a base, modulo a prime.

    The Python % returns a non-negative result so the subtraction is safe; in C++ the same line needs (val[r] - val[l]*pw[r-l] % m + m) % m with the multiplication reduced first.

  11. Program

    Implement Rabin–Karp string matching with a modular rolling hash, and demonstrate a collision attack against a fixed base and modulus.

    Solution
    def rolling(text, pat, B=131, M=(1<<61)-1):
    n, m = len(text), len(pat)
    if m > n: return []
    hp = 0
    for c in pat: hp = (hp*B + ord(c)) % M
    h, Bm = 0, pow(B, m, M)
    out = []
    for i, c in enumerate(text):
        h = (h*B + ord(c)) % M
        if i >= m: h = (h - ord(text[i-m])*Bm) % M
        if i >= m-1 and h == hp and text[i-m+1:i+1] == pat:   # verify!
            out.append(i-m+1)
    return out

    The attack: with a fixed B and M (a common shortcut), an attacker solves for two strings with equal hash. For M ≈ 2&sup6;&sup4;, the Thue–Morse sequence construction produces colliding pairs of length O(\log M) for any base; more simply, a birthday search over 2³² random strings finds a collision in minutes. Codeforces "anti-hash" tests exploit exactly this.

    Mitigations: pick B randomly at run time from [2, M-2]; use M = 2⁶¹-1 (a Mersenne prime, with fast reduction) rather than 2⁶⁴; use a double hash with two independent moduli; and always verify a hash match with a direct comparison, as the code above does — which makes the algorithm correct regardless, and only the running time probabilistic.

  12. Program

    Compute n! mod p for n ≥ p, and explain the trap.

    Solution
    def fact_mod(n, p):
    if n >= p: return 0          # the product contains the factor p
    r = 1
    for i in range(2, n+1): r = r*i % p
    return r

    The trap: for n ≥ p the answer is trivially 0, which makes C(n,k) mod p a 0/0 form if you compute it as n!·(k!)^{-1}·((n-k)!)^{-1} — and 0!^{-1} does not exist. The naive formula silently produces garbage.

    The fixes are Lucas' theorem (Chapter 29) for prime p — express n and k in base p and multiply the digit-wise binomials — or, when you need n! mod p with the factors of p stripped out, Wilson's theorem plus the generalised factorial (n!)_p. Recognising that n ≥ p changes the problem entirely is the whole point.

  13. Program

    Given 10⁵ queries of the form "is aⁿ ≡ b (mod m)" with huge n given as a decimal string, answer each in O(|n|).

    Solution
    def pow_bigexp(a, n_str, m):
    # reduce the exponent modulo φ(m) when gcd(a,m)=1 (Euler);
    # otherwise use the lifting-the-exponent form of Euler's theorem.
    phi = totient(m)
    e = 0
    for ch in n_str: e = (e*10 + int(ch)) % phi
    if math.gcd(a, m) == 1:
        return pow(a, e, m)
    # generalised: if n >= log2(m), a^n ≡ a^{(n mod φ) + φ} (mod m)
    big = len(n_str) > 7 or int(n_str) >= m.bit_length()
    return pow(a, e + (phi if big else 0), m) if big else pow(a, int(n_str), m)

    The coprime case is straight Euler. The non-coprime case needs the generalised Euler theorem: for n ≥ log₂m, aⁿ ≡ a^{(n mod φ(m)) + φ(m)} (mod m). Applying the plain reduction when gcd(a,m) > 1 gives wrong answers — for instance 2⁵ = 32 ≡ 8 (mod 12) while φ(12) = 4 and 2^{5 mod 4} = 2 ¬equiv; 8. The +φ(m) is what repairs it.

  14. Program

    Write a checker that detects modular-arithmetic bugs in a codebase: scan for subtraction without normalisation and for multiplication that can overflow.

    Solution
    import re, sys
PATTERNS = [
  (r'\(\s*\w+\s*-\s*\w+\s*\)\s*%\s*MOD',
   "subtraction mod without + MOD: may yield a negative residue"),
  (r'(?<!__int128\)\s)\b(?:int|long)\s+\w+\s*=\s*\w+\s*\*\s*\w+\s*%\s*MOD',
   "32-bit multiplication before mod: overflow"),
  (r'pow\(\s*\w+\s*,\s*MOD\s*-\s*2',
   "Fermat inverse: verify MOD is prime and the base is nonzero"),
  (r'%\s*MOD\s*\]',                      # using a residue as an index
   "residue used as an index: ensure it is normalised non-negative"),
]
for path in sys.argv[1:]:
    for i, line in enumerate(open(path), 1):
        for pat, msg in PATTERNS:
            if re.search(pat, line): print(f"{path}:{i}: {msg}\n    {line.strip()}")

    Crude, but it catches the four highest-frequency modular bugs, and a linter that flags (a - b) % MOD pays for itself. The deeper check — proving no intermediate exceeds the type's range — is a job for a static analyser with interval arithmetic, or for switching to a Mint type where the invariant "always in [0, MOD)" is maintained by construction and cannot be violated by a caller.

Chapter 17

Modular inverses and division

Division does not exist modulo n; multiplication by an inverse does, and only for elements coprime to n. Four algorithms compute inverses, each with a different precondition and cost, and picking the wrong one is a silent correctness bug rather than a crash.

The modular inverse of a modulo n is the element a^{-1} with a · a^{-1} ≡ 1 (mod n). It exists iff gcd(a,n) = 1, and then it is unique modulo n.

Existence follows from Bézout: ax + ny = 1 means ax ≡ 1. Uniqueness: if ab ≡ ac ≡ 1 then b ≡ b(ac) = (ba)c ≡ c. Non-existence when g = gcd(a,n) > 1: any ax is divisible by g, so it cannot be ≡ 1. ∎

The invertible elements form the unit group (ℤ/n)^×, of size φ(n). When n is prime this is everything non-zero, which is why prime moduli are so much more pleasant.

Four algorithms

MethodPreconditionCostWhen to use
Extended Euclidgcd(a,n)=1O(log n) divisionsthe general default
Fermat: a^{p-2}p prime, p ∤ aO(log p) multsprime modulus, one-liner
Euler: a^{φ(n)-1}gcd(a,n)=1O(log n) + factoringrarely — needs φ(n)
Batch prefix-productall coprime to nO(k) + one inversemany inverses at once
The four, in code
// 1. Extended Euclid — works for any modulus
long long inv(long long a, long long m){
    long long x, y, g = extgcd(((a % m) + m) % m, m, x, y);
    if (g != 1) return -1;                 // NOT invertible — do not ignore
    return ((x % m) + m) % m;
}

// 2. Fermat — p must be PRIME
long long inv_p(long long a, long long p){ return power(a, p - 2, p); }

// 4. Batch: invert k elements with ONE modular exponentiation
vector<long long> batch_inv(vector<long long> a, long long m){
    int k = a.size();
    vector<long long> pre(k + 1); pre[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) pre[i+1] = pre[i] * a[i] % m;
    long long cur = inv(pre[k], m);        // one inverse for all of them
    vector<long long> r(k);
    for (int i = k - 1; i >= 0; i--){
        r[i] = pre[i] * cur % m;
        cur  = cur * a[i] % m;
    }
    return r;
}

The batch trick is worth internalising: aᵢ^{-1} = (a₀…aᵢ₋₁) · (a₀…aᵢ)^{-1}, so one inverse plus 3k multiplications inverts the whole array. It turns O(k log m) into O(k + log m), and it is used everywhere in elliptic-curve implementations, where inversion is the dominant cost.

Principle

Never write pow(a, MOD-2, MOD) without knowing MOD is prime and a ¬equiv; 0. If MOD is composite the result is silently wrong; if a ≡ 0 the result is 0, which is not an inverse of anything. Extended Euclid at least detects non-invertibility, which is why it is the safer default even when Fermat applies.

Fractions modulo a prime

Contest answers are often "a fraction p/q modulo 10⁹+7". This means p · q^{-1} mod (10⁹+7) — well defined as long as q is not a multiple of the modulus. It is not an approximation; the map from rationals with small denominators to residues is injective, and the original fraction can be recovered by continued fractions (Chapter 14, problem 12).

The practical consequence: probability and expectation problems can be computed entirely in ℤ/p with exact arithmetic, no floating point, no accumulated error. An expectation E = ∑ pᵢxᵢ becomes a sum of products of residues, and the answer is exact.

Inverses when the modulus is not prime

Two situations arise. If gcd(a,n) = 1 but n is composite, use extended Euclid — it does not care. If gcd(a,n) = g > 1, no inverse exists, and the usual repair is to work with the equation ax ≡ b (mod n) directly (Chapter 15): divide a, b and n all by g, provided g | b.

A third situation, common in binomial computations: you need a/b mod p^k where b is divisible by p. Then track the power of p separately — write b = p^e b' with p ∤ b', invert b', and handle p^e by cancellation against the numerator. That is exactly the technique behind computing binomials modulo prime powers.

Interview angle

"How would you compute C(n,k) mod 10⁹+7 for n ≤ 10⁶ and 10⁵ queries?" — precompute factorials in O(n), precompute inverse factorials in O(n) using one modular exponentiation and the backward recurrence invfact[i-1] = invfact[i] · i, then each query is three multiplications. Mentioning that the backward recurrence avoids n separate exponentiations is the detail that shows practice.

Problem set — 14 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Find 17^{-1} mod 43 by hand.

    Solution

    Euclid: 43 = 2·17 + 9, 17 = 1·9 + 8, 9 = 1·8 + 1.

    Back: 1 = 9 - 8 = 9 - (17 - 9) = 2·9 - 17 = 2(43 - 2·17) - 17 = 2·43 - 5·17.

    So -5·17 ≡ 1 (mod 43), giving 17^{-1} ≡ -5 ≡ 38. Check: 17·38 = 646 = 15·43 + 1. ✓

  2. Theory

    Prove that the modular inverse, when it exists, is unique modulo n.

    Solution

    Suppose ab ≡ 1 and ac ≡ 1 (mod n). Then

    b ≡ b · 1 ≡ b(ac) = (ba)c ≡ 1 · c ≡ c (mod n). ∎

    The proof uses only associativity and the two inverse relations — it is the standard group-theoretic uniqueness-of-inverse argument, valid in any monoid. Note it does not need commutativity, which is why the same statement holds for matrix inverses.

  3. Theory

    For which a in {1,…,11} does a^{-1} mod 12 exist? Compute all of them and identify the group structure.

    Solution

    Invertible iff coprime to 12: a ∈ {1,5,7,11}, so |(ℤ/12)^×| = φ(12) = 4.

    Each is its own inverse: 5² = 25 ≡ 1, 7² = 49 ≡ 1, 11² = 121 ≡ 1. So every element has order 1 or 2, and the group is ℤ₂ × ℤ₂ (the Klein four-group), not cyclic.

    Consequence: 12 has no primitive root. The moduli with primitive roots are exactly 1, 2, 4, pᵏ, 2pᵏ for odd primes p — a fact that matters when a problem needs a generator (Chapter 24).

  4. Theory

    Show that if p is prime, a^{-1} ≡ a^{p-2} (mod p), and explain what goes wrong if p is composite.

    Solution

    By Fermat's little theorem, a^{p-1} ≡ 1 for p ∤ a. So a · a^{p-2} = a^{p-1} ≡ 1, making a^{p-2} the inverse. ∎

    Composite failure: take n = 15, a = 2. Then 2^{13} = 8192 = 546·15 + 2 ≡ 2 (mod 15), and 2·2 = 4 ≠ 1. The true inverse is 8 (2·8 = 16 ≡ 1). Fermat's exponent n-2 is simply the wrong exponent; the correct one is φ(n)-1 = 7, and 2⁷ = 128 ≡ 8 ✓.

    The danger is that it fails silently — no exception, just a wrong number propagating through the computation.

  5. Theory

    Derive the recurrence inv[i] = -(p/i) · inv[p mod i] mod p for computing all inverses mod a prime p in linear time.

    Solution

    Write p = qi + r with q = ⌊p/i⌋, r = p mod i, so 0 ≤ r < i. Reducing mod p: qi + r ≡ 0, hence qi ≡ -r.

    Multiply both sides by i^{-1}r^{-1}: q r^{-1} ≡ -i^{-1}, i.e. i^{-1} ≡ -q · r^{-1} = -\lfloor p/i \rfloor · (p mod i)^{-1} (mod p). ∎

    Since r < i, the needed value is already known, so a single forward pass computes all of 1^{-1},…,n^{-1} in O(n). Requires p prime and n < p (so that no i is ≡ 0).

  6. Theory

    Explain how to recover the rational p/q from its residue r = p q^{-1} mod M, given |p|, q < √(M/2).

    Solution

    This is rational reconstruction. Run the extended Euclidean algorithm on (M, r), recording the remainder sequence rᵢ and the coefficient sequence tᵢ with rᵢ ≡ tᵢ · r (mod M). Stop at the first i with rᵢ < √(M/2). Then p = rᵢ and q = tᵢ (up to sign), and this pair is unique under the size bound.

    Uniqueness: if two pairs (p₁,q₁) and (p₂,q₂) both satisfied the congruence with all parts below √(M/2), then p₁q₂ - p₂q₁ ≡ 0 (mod M) while |p₁q₂ - p₂q₁| < M, forcing it to be 0 — so the fractions are equal. ∎

    This is how computer algebra systems do exact linear algebra: work modulo a large prime (fast, no coefficient growth), then reconstruct the rationals at the end.

  7. Theory

    Given n and its factorisation, count how many a ∈ [1,n] are self-inverse, i.e. a² ≡ 1 (mod n).

    Solution

    By CRT the condition splits over prime powers. For odd pᵏ, x² ≡ 1 has exactly 2 solutions (±1), since pᵏ | (x-1)(x+1) and p cannot divide both factors. For 2ᵏ: 1 solution when k = 1, 2 when k = 2, and 4 when k ≥ 3 (namely ±1, 2^{k-1}±1).

    So the count is 2^{ω_{odd}(n)} · c₂ where ω_{odd} counts distinct odd primes and c₂ ∈ \{1,1,2,4\} by the power of 2.

    Application: the product of all units mod n is -1 when there is exactly one self-inverse element other than 1 (odd prime powers and twice them), and +1 otherwise — the generalisation of Wilson's theorem, and the reason Wilson's (p-1)! ≡ -1 works: all units pair off with their distinct inverses except ±1.

  8. Program

    Implement all four inverse algorithms and verify they agree on every invertible residue for all n ≤ 200.

    Solution
    import math
def inv_eg(a, m):
    g, x, _ = extgcd(a % m, m)
    return None if g != 1 else x % m
def inv_fermat(a, p): return pow(a, p-2, p)
def inv_euler(a, m):  return pow(a, totient(m)-1, m)
def inv_brute(a, m):
    for x in range(m):
        if a*x % m == 1: return x
    return None

for m in range(2, 201):
    for a in range(m):
        e = inv_eg(a, m); b = inv_brute(a, m)
        assert e == b, (a, m, e, b)
        if math.gcd(a, m) == 1:
            assert inv_euler(a, m) == b
            if is_prime(m) and a: assert inv_fermat(a, m) == b

    The test that matters is the non-invertible case: inv_eg must return None, while inv_fermat and inv_euler return numbers that are not inverses. Asserting that they agree only when the precondition holds is what documents the precondition in executable form.

  9. Program

    Precompute factorials and inverse factorials mod 10⁹+7 up to 10⁷, then answer 10⁶ binomial queries. Give both the O(n log p) and O(n + log p) preprocessing.

    Solution
    MOD = 10**9 + 7; N = 10**7 + 1
fact = [1]*N
for i in range(1, N): fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD

# O(n + log p): ONE exponentiation, then walk down
ifact = [1]*N
ifact[N-1] = pow(fact[N-1], MOD-2, MOD)
for i in range(N-1, 0, -1): ifact[i-1] = ifact[i] * i % MOD

def C(n, k):
    if k < 0 or k > n: return 0
    return fact[n] * ifact[k] % MOD * ifact[n-k] % MOD

    The naive version calls pow once per index: 10⁷ × 30 multiplications ≈ 3·10⁸, several seconds. The backward recurrence uses 10⁷ multiplications plus one exponentiation — about 30× faster.

    Why it works: (i-1)!^{-1} = (i!)^{-1} · i, since i! = (i-1)! · i. It is the batch-inverse trick specialised to a sequence where each element is a prefix product of the one before.

  10. Program

    Implement batch inversion for 10⁶ arbitrary residues and measure the speed-up over per-element Fermat.

    Solution
    def batch_inv(a, m):
    k = len(a)
    pre = [1]*(k+1)
    for i in range(k): pre[i+1] = pre[i] * a[i] % m
    cur = pow(pre[k], m-2, m)                 # one exponentiation total
    out = [0]*k
    for i in range(k-1, -1, -1):
        out[i] = pre[i] * cur % m
        cur = cur * a[i] % m
    return out

    Cost: 3k multiplications plus one exponentiation (\approx 1.5 log₂m ≈ 45 multiplications). Per-element Fermat costs 45k. At k = 10⁶ that is 3M versus 45M multiplications — a measured 10–15× speed-up.

    Caveat: every element must be invertible, since a single zero makes the whole prefix product zero and corrupts all outputs. Guard with a filter, or substitute 1 for the zeros and mask the results afterwards.

  11. Program

    Compute the expected value of a random process exactly as a residue mod 10⁹+7, and verify it against a floating-point simulation.

    Solution
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
def inv(x): return pow(x, MOD-2, MOD)

# Example: expected number of rolls of a fair d-sided die to see all faces
# (coupon collector) = d * H_d, exactly, as a residue.
def expected_collect(d):
    r = 0
    for i in range(1, d+1):
        r = (r + inv(i)) % MOD          # H_d as a residue
    return r * d % MOD

# verify: reconstruct the rational and compare to the float value
from fractions import Fraction
def check(d):
    exact = d * sum(Fraction(1, i) for i in range(1, d+1))
    assert expected_collect(d) == exact.numerator % MOD * inv(exact.denominator) % MOD

    The residue carries the exact rational, not an approximation — so no error accumulates across a long DP, which is the whole reason contest problems ask for the answer in this form. To sanity-check during development, compute the same DP in Fraction for small inputs and compare, since a residue is unreadable to a human and a sign or inverse error looks like any other 9-digit number.

  12. Program

    Given a, b, n with n composite, solve ax ≡ b (mod n) and return all solutions, using the gcd reduction rather than an inverse.

    Solution
    def solve_lin(a, b, n):
    g = math.gcd(a, n)
    if b % g: return []                         # no solution
    a1, b1, n1 = a//g, b//g, n//g
    x0 = b1 * inv_eg(a1 % n1, n1) % n1          # now gcd(a1,n1)=1
    return [(x0 + i*n1) % n for i in range(g)]  # exactly g solutions mod n

    Test cases that matter: solve_lin(6,4,10) → [4,9]; solve_lin(6,3,10) → [] since gcd(6,10)=2 ∤ 3; solve_lin(0,0,n) → every residue.

    The point of the chapter: reaching for an inverse first is the wrong instinct when the modulus is composite, because the inverse may not exist even though the equation is perfectly solvable. Reduce by the gcd, then invert.

  13. Program

    Implement Montgomery multiplication (multiplication mod n using only shifts, multiplications and additions) and benchmark it against %.

    Solution
    // Precompute for odd n:  R = 2^64,  n' = -n^{-1} mod R,  R2 = R^2 mod n
struct Mont {
    uint64_t n, n_inv, r2;
    Mont(uint64_t n_): n(n_) {
        n_inv = 1;
        for (int i = 0; i < 6; i++) n_inv *= 2 - n * n_inv;   // Newton, mod 2^64
        n_inv = -n_inv;
        r2 = -(__uint128_t)n % n;
    }
    uint64_t reduce(__uint128_t t) const {
        uint64_t m = (uint64_t)t * n_inv;
        uint64_t y = (t + (__uint128_t)m * n) >> 64;
        return y >= n ? y - n : y;
    }
    uint64_t mul(uint64_t a, uint64_t b) const { return reduce((__uint128_t)a*b); }
};

    The Newton iteration computes n^{-1} mod 2⁶⁴ in 6 steps because each doubles the number of correct bits (1 → 2 → 4 → … → 64). Requires n odd, which is why Montgomery is used for prime moduli and RSA.

    Measured: 2–4× faster than % for repeated multiplications with a fixed modulus, because 64-bit division is 20–40 cycles while Montgomery reduction is two multiplications and a shift. Values live in "Montgomery form" aR mod n, so convert in once (mul(a, r2)) and out at the end (reduce(a)) — the conversion only pays off across many operations, which is exactly the modular-exponentiation workload.

  14. Program

    Write a fuzz test that finds inputs where a pow(a, MOD-2, MOD)-based inverse silently produces a wrong answer.

    Solution
    import random, math
bad = []
for _ in range(200000):
    m = random.randrange(3, 1000)
    a = random.randrange(1, m)
    r = pow(a, m-2, m)
    if a * r % m != 1:
        bad.append((a, m, r, math.gcd(a, m), is_prime(m)))
# group the failures
from collections import Counter
print(Counter((g > 1, prime) for _,_,_,g,prime in bad))

    Two failure classes appear. (a) composite m with gcd(a,m)=1: an inverse exists but Fermat's exponent is wrong — e.g. a=2, m=15 gives 2, not 8. (b) gcd(a,m) > 1: no inverse exists at all, yet pow happily returns a number.

    Notably there are no failures for prime m with a ≠ 0 — the precondition is exactly right. That is the value of this fuzz test: it converts "MOD must be prime" from a comment into a demonstrated fact, and it is a good regression test to keep, because someone will eventually change MOD to a non-prime.

Chapter 18

Primes and the sieve of Eratosthenes

The sieve is the fastest way to know everything about every number up to n, and its variants — linear, segmented, smallest-prime-factor — cover almost every "for each number up to a million" problem you will meet.

A prime is an integer > 1 whose only positive divisors are 1 and itself. The fundamental theorem of arithmetic says every integer > 1 factors into primes uniquely up to order — existence by strong induction, uniqueness by Euclid's lemma.

Two facts about density govern all the algorithms:

π(n) ~ n / ln n prime number theorem p_k ~ k ln k the k-th prime π(10⁶) = 78,498 π(10⁹) = 50,847,534 π(10¹²) ≈ 3.76×10¹⁰ Probability a random n-digit number is prime ≈ 1/(2.3n) ⟹ finding a 1024-bit prime takes ≈ 355 random odd candidates

The second line is why RSA key generation is fast: primes are dense enough that random search works, and each candidate is rejected in microseconds by trial division against small primes before an expensive Miller–Rabin test is run.

The classical sieve

Sieve of Eratosthenes, with the standard optimisations
vector<bool> sieve(int n){
    vector<bool> comp(n+1, false);
    for (int i = 2; (long long)i*i <= n; i++)
        if (!comp[i])
            for (int j = i*i; j <= n; j += i)   // start at i², not 2i
                comp[j] = true;
    return comp;                                 // comp[x] == false ⟺ x prime
}

Two optimisations carry all the weight. Starting the inner loop at i² is correct because any smaller multiple ki with k < i was already crossed off when the smaller prime factor of k was processed. Stopping the outer loop at √n is correct because a composite ≤ n has a prime factor ≤ √n.

Complexity is ∑p ≤ n n/p = n ln ln n + O(n) by Mertens' theorem — effectively linear, with ln ln 10⁸ ≈ 2.9.

Three variants worth knowing

Smallest prime factor sieve. Store spf[x] instead of a boolean, and factorisation of any x ≤ n becomes O(log x) with no division loop. This is the single most useful sieve variant for contest work — one array answers "factorise", "is prime", "count divisors", and "is squarefree".

Linear sieve. Each composite is crossed off exactly once, by its smallest prime factor, giving Θ(n) and — more importantly — a natural place to compute any multiplicative function (Chapter 19) alongside.

Linear sieve with smallest prime factor
vector<int> primes; vector<int> spf(n+1, 0);
for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++){
    if (!spf[i]) { spf[i] = i; primes.push_back(i); }
    for (int p : primes){
        if (p > spf[i] || (long long)p*i > n) break;   // ← the crucial guard
        spf[p*i] = p;
    }
}

The p > spf[i] break is what makes it linear: it stops before p exceeds i's smallest prime factor, so each composite p·i is written exactly once, at the moment p is its smallest prime factor.

Segmented sieve. To find primes in [L, R] with R up to 10¹² and R-L ≤ 10⁶: sieve base primes up to √R, then cross their multiples off a window of size R-L+1. Memory is O(√R + (R-L)) rather than O(R).

Principle

Trial division to √n tests one number in O(√n). A sieve tests every number up to n in O(n log log n) — that is O(log log n) amortised each. So: one query, trial-divide; many queries over a bounded range, sieve; one query with n astronomically large, Miller–Rabin (Chapter 23).

Interview angle

"Count primes below n" is a sieve question, and the follow-up is memory: a vector<bool> for n = 10⁹ is 125 MB, which fits, but sieving only odd numbers halves it and a wheel on {2,3,5} cuts it to 27%. If asked for n = 10¹², the answer is a segmented sieve, or the Meissel–Lehmer method which counts primes without enumerating them in about O(n^{2/3}).

Problem set — 15 problemstheory & programming
  1. Theory

    Prove that it suffices to test divisors up to √n when checking primality by trial division.

    Solution

    If n = ab with 1 < a ≤ b < n, then a² ≤ ab = n, so a ≤ √n. Hence any composite n has a divisor in (1, √n]. Contrapositive: if no divisor ≤ √n exists, n is prime. ∎

    Implementation note: write the loop condition as i*i <= n, not i <= sqrt(n). The latter calls a floating-point function whose result can be off by one ulp near perfect squares, producing a wrong answer for specific large n — and i*i must itself be computed in a wide enough type.

  2. Theory

    Prove the sieve's inner loop may start at i².

    Solution

    Consider a multiple ki with 2 ≤ k < i. Let q be the smallest prime factor of k; then q ≤ k < i, and q was processed by the outer loop earlier. At that time ki was crossed off, since ki is a multiple of q and ki ≥ q² (because ki ≥ 2i > 2q and… more directly, ki/q ≥ i > q so ki ≥ q·i > q²). So no multiple below i² needs crossing again. ∎

    The saving is real: without it, the work is ∑p≤nn/p ≈ n ln ln n anyway asymptotically, but the constant roughly halves and, more importantly, the write traffic to memory halves — which dominates for large n.

  3. Theory

    Estimate π(10¹⁸) using the prime number theorem and state the accuracy of the estimate.

    Solution

    π(n) ≈ n/ln n: 10¹⁸/(18 ln 10) = 10¹⁸/41.45 ≈ 2.41 × 10¹⁶.

    The better estimate is the logarithmic integral li(n) = ∫₂ⁿ dt/ln t ≈ 2.4739 × 10¹⁶, and the true value is 24{,}739{,}954{,}287{,}740{,}860. So n/ln n is about 2.6% low while li(n) is accurate to 9 significant figures.

    The error term is where the Riemann hypothesis lives: RH is equivalent to |π(n) - li(n)| = O(√n log n). Unconditionally we only know a weaker bound, which is why the difference between the two estimates is not a detail.

  4. Theory

    Prove the linear sieve crosses off each composite exactly once.

    Solution

    Every composite m has a unique smallest prime factor p, so it is uniquely written as m = p · i with i = m/p and p ≤ spf(i).

    The algorithm, at iteration i, loops over primes p in increasing order and breaks when p > spf(i). So it writes p·i exactly for the primes p ≤ spf(i) — which is precisely the set of pairs (p,i) in the unique representation above. Each composite is therefore produced by exactly one (p,i) pair. ∎

    Total work is O(n), but the classical sieve is often faster in wall time despite doing n ln ln n work, because its writes are sequential strides while the linear sieve accesses primes[] and spf[] irregularly. Use the linear sieve when you need the multiplicative-function hook, not for speed alone.

  5. Theory

    Show that there are arbitrarily large gaps between consecutive primes.

    Solution

    For any n, the n-1 consecutive integers n!+2, n!+3, …, n!+n are all composite: k | n! and k | k, so k | (n!+k) for 2 ≤ k ≤ n. ∎

    So gaps of any length exist. The construction is wasteful — it places the gap near n!, an astronomically large number — whereas the maximal gap below 10¹⁸ is only 1,476. Cramér's conjecture predicts gaps are O(log² p); the best proved bound is far weaker.

    Contrast with the Bertrand–Chebyshev theorem: there is always a prime between n and 2n. So gaps grow, but never as fast as the numbers themselves.

  6. Theory

    How much memory does a bitset sieve to 10⁹ need? Design a wheel-based layout reducing it further.

    Solution
    • Naive vector<bool>: 10⁹ bits = 125 MB.
    • Odd numbers only: 62.5 MB (drop all evens; index x at bit (x-3)/2).
    • Wheel mod 30: only 8 residues coprime to 30 (1,7,11,13,17,19,23,29), so 8/30 = 26.7\% → 33 MB, one byte per 30 integers.
    • Wheel mod 210 (adding 7): 48/210 = 22.9\% → 28.6 MB, with more complex indexing.

    The mod-30 wheel is the sweet spot: one byte per block of 30 makes the indexing a table lookup, and it is what primesieve and similar libraries use. Beyond that, a segmented wheel sieve keeps the working set inside L2 cache, which matters more than total memory — cache misses, not bytes, are the binding constraint above 10⁸.

  7. Theory

    Prove that if 2ₙ-1 is prime then n is prime, and that the converse fails.

    Solution

    If n = ab with 1 < a,b < n, then 2ᵃ-1 divides 2ₙ-1, since x-1 | xᵇ-1 with x = 2ᵃ. As 1 < 2ᵃ-1 < 2ₙ-1, the latter is composite. Contrapositive gives the claim. ∎

    Converse fails: n = 11 is prime but 2¹¹-1 = 2047 = 23 × 89. Only 52 Mersenne primes are known, and searching for them (GIMPS) uses the Lucas–Lehmer test, which is O(n²)-ish per candidate and specific to this form — dramatically faster than general primality testing, which is why the largest known primes are all Mersenne.

  8. Program

    Implement the sieve, the odd-only sieve and a mod-30 wheel sieve to 10⁹, and compare time and memory.

    Solution
    // odd-only: bit i represents the number 2i+3
vector<uint64_t> bits((n/2)/64 + 2, 0);
auto isc = [&](int x){ return (bits[x>>6] >> (x&63)) & 1; };
auto set = [&](int x){ bits[x>>6] |= 1ull << (x&63); };
for (int i = 0; (2*i+3)*(long long)(2*i+3) <= n; i++)
    if (!isc(i)){
        int p = 2*i+3;
        for (long long j = (long long)p*p; j <= n; j += 2*p)   // odd multiples only
            set((j-3)/2);
    }

    Typical timings at n = 10⁹ on a modern CPU: naive ~6 s / 125 MB, odd-only ~3 s / 62 MB, mod-30 wheel ~1.5 s / 33 MB, segmented wheel ~0.8 s / a few MB. The segmented version wins not by doing less work but by keeping the active window in L2 cache — the same Chapter 8 lesson that the RAM model omits memory hierarchy.

    Note j += 2*p: since p is odd, every second multiple is odd, so stepping by 2p skips the evens that are not represented at all.

  9. Program

    Build a smallest-prime-factor table to 10⁷ and use it to factorise 10⁶ queries in O(log x) each.

    Solution
    N = 10**7
spf = list(range(N+1))
i = 2
while i*i <= N:
    if spf[i] == i:
        for j in range(i*i, N+1, i):
            if spf[j] == j: spf[j] = i        # only if not yet assigned
    i += 1

def factorise(x):
    f = {}
    while x > 1:
        p = spf[x]; e = 0
        while x % p == 0: x //= p; e += 1
        f[p] = e
    return f

    Each division removes at least a factor of 2, so factorisation is O(log x) — at most 23 steps for x ≤ 10⁷. Compare trial division at O(√x) = 3163 steps: a 100× difference per query, which at 10⁶ queries is the difference between 0.2 s and 30 s.

    Memory is the cost: 10⁷ 32-bit ints = 40 MB. Storing only the smallest prime factor as a 32-bit value is already minimal; below 2¹⁶ a 16-bit array halves it.

  10. Program

    Implement a segmented sieve listing all primes in [L, R] with R ≤ 10¹² and R - L ≤ 10⁶.

    Solution
    def segmented(L, R):
    lim = int(R**0.5) + 1
    base = simple_sieve(lim)                     # primes up to sqrt(R)
    mark = bytearray(R - L + 1)                  # 0 = prime candidate
    for p in base:
        start = max(p*p, ((L + p - 1) // p) * p) # first multiple of p ≥ L
        for j in range(start, R+1, p):
            mark[j - L] = 1
    if L == 1: mark[0] = 1
    return [L + i for i, m in enumerate(mark) if not m]

    Memory is O(√R + (R-L)) — about 1 MB of base primes plus the window. The subtle line is start: beginning at p² when p² ≥ L avoids marking p itself as composite, and the ceiling division finds the first multiple ≥ L otherwise.

    Watch the edge cases: L = 1 (1 is not prime), L = 0, and windows lying entirely below √R. Each has bitten every implementation of this at least once.

  11. Program

    Implement the linear sieve computing, in the same pass, φ(i), μ(i) and the divisor count d(i) for all i ≤ n.

    Solution
    phi = [0]*(N+1); mu = [0]*(N+1); d = [0]*(N+1); cnt = [0]*(N+1)
phi[1] = mu[1] = d[1] = 1
primes = []; spf = [0]*(N+1)
for i in range(2, N+1):
    if not spf[i]:
        spf[i] = i; primes.append(i)
        phi[i] = i-1; mu[i] = -1; d[i] = 2; cnt[i] = 1
    for p in primes:
        if p > spf[i] or p*i > N: break
        j = p*i; spf[j] = p
        if p == spf[i]:                       # p divides i: same prime again
            phi[j] = phi[i]*p
            mu[j]  = 0                        # square factor
            cnt[j] = cnt[i] + 1
            d[j]   = d[i] // (cnt[i]+1) * (cnt[j]+1)
        else:                                 # p is new: multiplicativity applies
            phi[j] = phi[i]*(p-1)
            mu[j]  = -mu[i]
            cnt[j] = 1
            d[j]   = d[i]*2

    The two branches are exactly the two cases of a multiplicative function: when p is a new prime the values multiply, and when p already divides i you must use the prime-power rule. Tracking cnt[i] (the exponent of the smallest prime) is what makes the divisor-count update possible in O(1).

    This one pass, O(n) total, replaces three separate sieves and is the standard preprocessing for any problem summing a multiplicative function over a range.

  12. Program

    Count primes below 10¹² without enumerating them, using the Meissel–Lehmer / Lucy_Hedgehog method.

    Solution
    def prime_count(n):
    r = int(n**0.5)
    V = [n//i for i in range(1, r+1)]
    V += list(range(V[-1]-1, 0, -1))
    S = {v: v-1 for v in V}                 # S[v] = count of 2..v, refined
    for p in range(2, r+1):
        if S[p] > S[p-1]:                   # p is prime
            sp, p2 = S[p-1], p*p
            for v in V:
                if v < p2: break
                S[v] -= S[v//p] - sp        # remove multiples of p
    return S[n]

    O(n^{3/4}) time and O(√n) memory — 10¹² in a couple of seconds, where a sieve would need 125 GB.

    The idea is a dynamic programming over "the count of integers in [2,v] with no prime factor below p", evaluated only at the O(√n) distinct values of ⌊n/i⌋ — the same divisor-block structure as Chapter 9's ∑⌊n/i⌋. The same recurrence, with v-1 replaced by a sum, computes ∑p≤np and other prime sums.

  13. Program

    Generate a random 1024-bit probable prime, as an RSA key generator would. Report how many candidates were tested.

    Solution
    import random
SMALL = [p for p in range(3, 1000) if all(p % q for q in range(2, int(p**.5)+1))]

def random_prime(bits):
    tried = 0
    while True:
        tried += 1
        c = random.getrandbits(bits) | (1 << (bits-1)) | 1   # top and bottom bit set
        if any(c % p == 0 for p in SMALL): continue           # cheap rejection
        if miller_rabin(c, rounds=40): return c, tried

    By the prime number theorem the density of primes near 2¹⁰²⁴ is 1/ln(2¹⁰²⁴) = 1/710; restricting to odd numbers doubles it to 1/355, and the small-prime pre-filter removes about 80% of the remaining composites at negligible cost. Expect roughly 355 candidates, of which only ~70 reach Miller–Rabin.

    Setting the top bit guarantees the modulus n = pq has the full 2048 bits; setting the bottom bit skips evens. Both are what real implementations do, and omitting the top bit is a genuine (if benign) key-generation bug.

  14. Program

    For each k from 1 to 20, find the smallest prime gap of length ≥ k below 10⁸, and compare to the log²p heuristic.

    Solution
    ps = sieve_list(10**8)
best = {}
for a, b in zip(ps, ps[1:]):
    g = b - a
    if g not in best: best[g] = a
for g in sorted(best): print(g, best[g])

    Results: the first gap of 10 is after 139; of 20, after 887; of 50, after 19,609; of 100, after 370,261; of 148, after 2,010,733; the maximal gap below 10⁸ is 220, after 47,326,693.

    Cramér's heuristic predicts the maximal gap near p is about ln²p: at p = 5×10⁷, ln²p ≈ 313 — the right order of magnitude, and the observed maxima track 0.7 ln²p closely across the whole range. The record-gap sequence is one of the cleanest examples of a heuristic with no proof matching data over many orders of magnitude.

  15. Program

    Given q ≤ 10⁵ queries "how many primes in [l, r]" with r ≤ 10⁷, answer each in O(1).

    Solution
    N = 10**7
comp = sieve(N)
pre = [0]*(N+2)
for i in range(2, N+1):
    pre[i+1] = pre[i] + (0 if comp[i] else 1)
def count(l, r): return pre[r+1] - pre[l]

    Prefix sums over the sieve: O(N log log N) preprocessing, O(1) per query, O(N) memory (40 MB as 32-bit ints, or 20 MB as 32-bit with N = 10⁷ — fine).

    If memory is tight, store the prefix count only every 64th index and recompute the remainder from the bitset with a popcount — 64× less memory at the cost of one popcount per query, which is still effectively O(1). That trade is worth knowing whenever a prefix-sum array over a bitmask blows the memory budget.
